The Crow Language Specification

Crow Language Specification

This is the reference: every construct, every builtin, and a Design Note for every deliberately rejected feature. New to Crow? Start with The Crow Book (docs/book; this document is Part II of the same site) — it teaches in order and every code block there is machine-verified. The other documents are indexed in docs/README.md.

Design Notes about one feature sit next to that feature; the language-wide ones are collected in section 19.

1. Variables and Types

Declaration & Assignment

Variables are declared by simply assigning a value. No keywords needed.

name = 'crow'
age = 3
pi = 3.14
active = true

Reassignment

Variables can be reassigned, but the type cannot change. int and float are compatible with each other.

x = 10
x = 20          --- ok, same type
x = 3.14        --- ok, int/float compatible
x = 'hello'     --- error: cannot reassign from int to string

A variable whose first value could not fail holds no error. A later value that can fail lands when it is good; when it failed, the assignment refuses it, as a decision refuses one (§10, Errors in Conditions): a function hands the error back to its caller, so a function that can fail this way can fail, as one with such a condition can; a handler stops; a view renders error: reason in its own place; and the top level halts with runtime error: line N: cannot assign an error value to 'x' — <reason>. To keep a failure to look at, give it a name whose first value can fail: r = float[s], then if not r.ok.

x = 1.0
x = float['2.5']     --- 2.5 lands
x = float['abc']     --- halts: cannot assign an error value to 'x' — cannot convert 'abc' to float

An empty list cannot fail either: xs = [], then xs = push[xs, float[s]], holds numbers, and a failed item is refused where it is pushed. Nor does a list of plain items given a literal whose items can fail: parts = ['a'], then parts = ['bx'] of an x that can fail, holds 'b…' when x is good, and the assignment refuses it when x failed (it had been refused as a change of kind).

Design Note: a plain variable refuses an error. Considered: letting the variable hold the error, its type widening to one that can fail, which the interpreter did while the compiled program returned at the assignment. Decided (Vijay, 2026-09-25): the compiled program's rule, in both modes. A variable's type does not change (above), and an error that silently took the variable's place would surface lines later, far from its cause; refused where it is assigned, it names the line and the variable.

A variable that is ever assigned a float is a float for its whole scope. Assigning an int to it stores the float value (x = 1 above stores 1.0), and so does handing an int to a parameter that is a float: half[3], where fn half[x] is also called with 2.5, divides 3.0. A parameter is a variable of its function: one the body assigns a float (x = x + 0.5), or a list of ints the body gives floats (xs = xs + 1), is a float — a list of floats — and every call hands it so; given another kind (t = t + 1 of a parsed value), it is refused as a local is. (The compiled program handed the function its int and did not build.) So does a record's field that holds floats — P[1, 2] beside P[1, 2.5] holds 2.0 — and a list, a map or a comparison of floats given an int: push[xs, 2] onto floats pushes 2.0, an item of a list of floats assigned an int holds its float (xs[0] = 2 stores 2.0; compiled, it had not built), and map[lr = 0.01, epochs = 100] holds 100.0. The other way round too: a list or a map of ints handed a float holds floats from then on — push[range[3], 2.5] is [0.0, 1.0, 2.0, 2.5], set[counts, 'k', 2.5] a map of floats, concat and contains likewise — and a record's float field given an int that can fail holds a float that can fail. (Compiled, those did not build; interpreted, the int was kept among the floats.) (Compiled, those did not build; interpreted, the int was kept, so type[m.epochs] said int beside a float division.) Note for interpreted mode: the widening applies from the first float assignment onward, so an int division performed before any float assignment may differ from compiled mode, where the variable is float from its declaration — don't depend on int division of variables that later hold floats.

Types

Crow is statically typed with full type inference — you never write type annotations, but every variable has a concrete type determined at compile time. The compiler infers types from usage; if a type cannot be determined, the program is invalid.

The built-in types are:

TypeExample
int42, -7, 0 — a whole number of any size
float3.14, -0.5
decimaldecimal['12.30'], decimal[2, digits = 50] — an exact base-ten number
fractionfraction[1, 3] — an exact ratio of two ints
str'hello', 'crow'
booltrue, false
array[1, 2, 3], [[1, 2], [3, 4]]
mapmap[home = 78701, office = 78702]

Arrays are homogeneous — all elements must be the same type. Use maps for mixed-type named data.

[1, 2, 3]                    --- int array
['a', 'b', 'c']              --- str array
[[1, 2], [3, 4]]             --- 2D int array
[1, 'hello', true]           --- error: mixed types

Numbers That Keep Every Digit

An int is a whole number of any size. Arithmetic on ints gives the exact answer however large it grows: 2 ^ 100 is 1267650600228229401496703205376, a factorial of a thousand has its 2,568 digits, and an int read from text keeps every digit it was written with — as does one written in the program: 123456789012345678901234567890 is that int (a literal past 64 bits had been refused in Rust's words, number too large to fit in target type). Nothing is written to ask for it, in either mode. The compiled program keeps an int in 64 bits where it can show the int never grows past them — a loop's counter below its bound, a position in a list, a value taken % n, a sum a loop adds to — and in a form that grows where it cannot, so a program pays for the size only where a number can have it. In showing it, the compiler counts no part of a program as running 2^48 times (days of computing at a billionth of a second a round): a sum taken in a loop, or over a recursion's calls, grows by fewer bits than that past what it adds, and a program that ran longer and grew such a sum past 64 bits would stop with an integer overflow, never with a wrong digit. Where an int past 64 bits is handed to what takes a 64-bit one — a position, a size, a count (range[n], xs[i], repeat[s, n]), a map's key, a frame's cell, a tensor's number — it is refused where it is handed, with what took it and what it was (its first twenty digits and how many there are, past forty): a whole number past 64 bits where range[] takes an int of 64 bits: 1267650600228229401496703205376. What shows or holds any value takes one as it is — str, log, text, type, a list's items, a map's values, a record's fields, encode — and so do abs, min, max and sort, and floor, ceil and round of an int are the int itself, every digit; a list or a map of ints handed one past 64 bits (push[xs, 2 ^ 100], set[m, k, 2 ^ 100]) holds it, its ints held as ints that grow from then on (each had stopped, a whole number past 64 bits where an int is held in 64 bits); float[n] is the nearest float, and int[x], floor[x], ceil[x] and round[x] of a float are the whole number it holds, every digit (int[10.0 ^ 30] is 1000000000000000019884624838656, the float's own value; of NaN 0, and of an infinite float a stop). A literal list of whole numbers that holds one growing past 64 bits is a list of ints, as one past 2^53 is (a tensor's numbers are floats). A function handed as a value — to another function, kept in a variable — is called through it as a function of ints of 64 bits: an answer past them is refused where it crosses (a whole number past 64 bits where a function handed as a value answers an int of 64 bits: …), while a call of the function by its name keeps every digit (the roadmap holds function values that grow). An int past 64 bits encodes as JSON text of its digits — "123456789012345678901234567890" — as JSON's readers hold numbers in 64 bits or as floats, which would lose them; one within 64 bits is a JSON number.

A decimal is an exact base-ten number. decimal['12.30'] holds 12.30 as written, its two places kept (str shows 12.30); decimal[12] and decimal[0.1] hold 12 and 0.1 (a float as the shortest text that reads back as it). +, - and * of decimals are exact and keep their places (decimal['12.30'] + decimal['1.1'] is 13.40, decimal['1.10'] * decimal['2.0'] is 2.200), so decimal['0.1'] + decimal['0.2'] eq decimal['0.3'] holds, and a sum of money never drifts. / of decimals has 28 significant digits, the last rounded half away from zero, as round rounds, unless a decimal in it holds more: decimal[x, digits = n] is x rounded to n significant digits, and every result it takes part in — +, -, *, /, ^, and sqrt, exp, ln, sin, cos, tan, atan of it — is rounded to the most digits of the decimals in it. That is the language's big float: sqrt[decimal[2, digits = 50]] is √2 to fifty digits. round[d, places] rounds a decimal to places places after the point, half away from zero (round[decimal['2.345'], 2] is 2.35); round, floor and ceil of a decimal are ints; int[d] cuts toward zero and float[d] is the nearest float.

A fraction is an exact ratio of two ints. fraction[1, 3] is one third, held in lowest terms with its sign on the top (fraction[2, -4] is -1/2); fraction[x] of an int or a decimal is that number exactly (fraction[decimal['0.25']] is 1/4). +, -, *, / and ^ of fractions are exact; a fraction or a decimal of 0, 1 or -1 (a decimal written with no places) to any int is exact, as an int is, and any other to a power past 32 bits stops, a number no memory holds. A fraction shows as n/d, a whole one as its int (str[fraction[4, 2]] is 2); int, float, round, floor and ceil read it as a number; fraction[n, 0] is an error value of kind 'math', as an int divided by zero is a stop.

Kinds meet the exact way. An int meeting a decimal is the decimal it is, and an int or a decimal meeting a fraction is the fraction it is: the answer is the exact one. A float meeting a decimal or a fraction is refused before the program runs, in both modes — a float holds a nearby binary number, and the exact answer would be a guess: price * rate with rate a float is refused with a decimal and a float: decimal[rate] reads the float as the decimal it shows, float[price] reads the decimal as a float. A literal written with a point is the number it writes, so price * 1.1 is price times eleven tenths. Two decimals, two fractions, or a decimal and a fraction compare by value in either order (decimal['2.50'] eq decimal['2.5'], fraction[1, 2] eq decimal['0.5']), and with an int; with a float, the comparison is refused as arithmetic is.

A decimal and a fraction encode as JSON text of what they show — "12.30", "1/3" — every digit kept, as an int past 64 bits does (JSON's readers hold a number as a float, which would lose digits, and JSON has no ratio); a list, a map or a record holds decimals and fractions as it holds any value, and a fact keeps one as that text. A frame's columns hold ints, floats, text, truths and moments: a column of decimals or fractions is refused where it is made, and a decimal column a database answers is a column of floats (the roadmap holds columns of decimals).

Design Note: ints grow. Considered: stopping on overflow (Crow until 2026-10-09: integer overflow: … does not fit in 64 bits), wrapping (Rust's release build, Java, Go), and a kind of its own the program asks for where it starts a number (q = big[1]). Decided (Vijay, 2026-10-09, asked whether Crow could tell by itself which numbers need to be big): the int grows and nothing is written, so the answer is the exact one as Python's is. Whether a number outgrows 64 bits depends on the data and on how long a loop runs, so the compiler cannot know it before the program runs — what it can show before the program runs is the opposite, that an int stays within 64 bits, and there it keeps the 64-bit form, so a loop over positions costs what it did.

Design Note: one decimal for exact money and for big floats. Considered: a binary big float beside the decimal (Julia's BigFloat, MPFR), and a precision set for the whole program (Python's getcontext().prec). Decided (Vijay asked for exact decimals, rationals and big floats, 2026-10-09): one decimal kind, exact where exactness is possible — +, -, * — and rounded to the digits its values ask for where it is not. A precision belongs to the values, never to a mode of the program, so a function rounds the same wherever it is called; a binary big float would be a second way to hold a number with chosen digits, whose digits print in base ten anyway.

Design Note: a float meets no exact number silently. Considered: a float meeting a decimal makes a float (C#'s decimal refuses it; Python's Decimal refuses it), or a decimal of the float's exact binary value (Decimal(0.1) is 0.1000000000000000055511151231257827…). Decided: refused before the program runs, in both modes, with both conversions named — a computed float is a nearby binary number, and either answer would be a guess the program did not make — while a literal written with a point is the number it writes, which a decimal or a fraction reads exactly.

String Escape Sequences

Strings support escape sequences with backslash:

EscapeCharacter
\nNewline
\tTab
\rCarriage return (a line of a wire protocol ends \r\n)
\\Literal backslash
\'Literal single quote
\`Literal backtick (not interpolation)

A backslash before any other character is itself, the backslash kept, so a regular expression's escapes are written as they are: '\d+', '\.', sub[s, '\|[^|]*\|', '-']. (Until 2026-10-09 any other character after a backslash was refused as an unknown escape, against strings being inert, and a regex needed [|] for \|.)

log['line1\nline2']
log['col1\tcol2']
log['it\'s here']
log['a \`code span\` in prose']    --- prints: a `code span` in prose

Comments and string literals accept any Unicode text. Identifiers are ASCII — a deliberate restriction that keeps the grammar and tooling simple.

String Interpolation

Backticks delimit an expression inside a string — like a markdown code span. Everything else in a string is inert text:

name = 'crow'
greeting = 'hello, `name`'
result = '1 + 2 = `1 + 2`'
li['`item.name` — qty `item.qty`']
log['best: `max[scores]`']
cfg = json['`"a": 1, "hosts": ["x"]`']   --- braces and brackets: plain text
m = matches[s, '[a-z]`2,4`']             --- regex: fully raw

Rules:

  • `expr` evaluates any Crow expression and splices its value. Its names are read like any others: an undefined one, or a module variable read inside a function, is refused before the program runs.
  • Inside an interpolation you are still inside the string — quotes stay escaped: 'up: upper['hi']'.
  • An unterminated or empty backtick span is an error (never silent).
  • For repeated formatting, name it: fn money[x] + 'P&L: money[pnl]' (see Formatting).

Design Note: Strings Are Inert; Backticks Delimit Code

What was considered: brace interpolation {expr} (the original design), \[expr] (Swift-style escape activation), bare `name (marker without terminator).

Why paired backticks won: any bare delimiter collides with real text — {} silently corrupted regex quantifiers ('[0-9]{4}' interpolated the {4}), bare [] would have destroyed regex character classes and [INFO]-style prose, and bare `name could not delimit (`names? `name.csv?) or express a + b. Pairing the marker gives full expressions, zero termination rules, and one form. Strings become inert by default — JSON, regex, CSS, and prose need no escapes — and interpolation is explicitly activated, unshifted, with the one notation everyone already reads as "this is code". The design lineage (braces → escape-activation → bare marker → paired) is preserved in docs/design-review.md.

Multiple Assignment

Multiple variables can be assigned in a single line, a value for each name.

x, y, z = 1, 2, 3

Values of another count are refused before the program runs, in both modes, and so is one value for several names: Crow takes no list apart into names (Design Note: No Destructuring). c, d = pair[] is refused with the form to write, r = pair[], then c = r[0], d = r[1]. (Each had run: one value had been taken apart interpreted and had not built compiled, a value past the names had been dropped, and a name past the values left unset.)

Swap

a, b = b, a

Scope

Variables are scoped to the block they are defined in. Inner blocks can access outer variables — but function boundaries are opaque: module variables are not visible inside functions (see Function Scope, section 7).

x = 10
if true
    y = 20
    log[x]    --- 10 — accessible: blocks see outer variables

log[y]        --- error — y is not in scope

Reading y there is refused before the program runs, in both modes, with what to write instead: set y before the block and assign it inside. So is reading a variable before the line that gives it its first value: it holds none yet. Two blocks may each set a variable of the same name, and a name set again after its block has ended is a new variable. They are different variables and may hold different types:

if ok
    x = 1
else
    x = 'one'     --- a different x

2. Comments

A comment starts with --- and runs to the end of the line. There is no closing delimiter.

--- a comment line

x = 5  --- inline comment

--- multi-line commentary is just
--- consecutive comment lines

Design Note: Comments End at the Newline

What was replaced: paired ---...--- comments (open and close delimiters, spanning lines).

Why: an unclosed paired comment silently swallowed the following line into code — the comment ran until it found the --- that opened the next comment, and the remainder of that line executed. This failure mode produced real incidents during development (corrupted test files; one bug misdiagnosed as a Unicode issue). End-of-line comments make the failure unrepresentable: every line is locally, independently correct, and commenting out a line is just prepending ---.

Migration: old single-line comments (--- text ---) still parse — the trailing --- is now inert text — and crow fmt strips these cosmetic closers. Old block comments with bare --- fence lines are no longer supported; write one --- per line.

3. Blocks

Blocks are defined by indentation. A new block starts after a statement (e.g. if, fn, for) and ends when the indentation returns to the previous level.

if x gt 5
    log['big']
    log['done']

next_thing[]

Blocks can be nested.

if x gt 5
    if y gt 10
        log['both big']

next_thing[]

A line holds one statement: x = 1 y = 2 is refused — the second starts a line of its own (it had run as two). A line is indented only under one that opens a block; one indented under a plain statement is refused, and a block's first line with no indented body under it is too.

A line that holds only a comment opens and closes no block, whatever its indentation, as a blank line does. A comment that goes on from a trailing one may be aligned under it, and one at the margin inside a block leaves the block open:

fn hello[name]
    words = 'hi `name`'     --- the greeting, as the
                            --- page shows it
--- a note at the margin, inside the function
    return words

(Such a line had been refused as indented under a line that opens no block, or had ended the block it sat in.)

4. Operators

Arithmetic

+  -  /  *  %  ^

^ is the power operator (right-associative):

2 ^ 10       --- 1024
2 ^ 3 ^ 2   --- 512 (right-associative: 2^(3^2) = 2^9)
2.0 ^ 0.5   --- 1.4142...
-3 ^ 2       --- -9 (the power first: -(3^2))
(-3) ^ 2     --- 9
2.0 ^ -1     --- 0.5

A power binds tighter than a minus sign before it, as in mathematics and in Python, R and Julia: -x ^ 2 is -(x ^ 2), and an exponent may carry its own sign. (The sign had been part of the base, as in a spreadsheet: -3 ^ 2 was 9, and a Gaussian's exp[-x ^ 2 / 2] grew where it should fall — crow check --self found it, holding its cases to Python's answers.)

Division is type-determined — one operator, no ambiguity:

3 / 2        --- 1 (both int → int, rounded toward zero)
-7 / 2       --- -3
-7 % 2       --- -1 (the remainder has the sign of the left side; `%` takes ints — a float on either side is refused before the program runs)
3 / 2.0      --- 1.5 (float involved → float)
7 / 3        --- 2
7.0 / 3      --- 2.3333...

An int divided by zero stops the program (When a Program Stops, §10); a float divided by zero is infinity.

The operands are numbers: text is refused before the program runs, and so is a list of text (['1', '2'] + 1 is cannot use '+' with array[str] and int). (Compiled, a list of text had been parsed as numbers, where the interpreter made each item an error.)

Tensor Arithmetic

For tensors (numeric arrays), operators follow MATLAB/Julia semantics:

OperatorMeaning
*Matrix multiply (matmul) for tensor×tensor; scalar multiply for tensor×scalar
.*Element-wise multiply
/Scalar divide (tensor÷scalar only; tensor÷tensor is an error — use ./)
./Element-wise divide
^Scalar power
.^Element-wise power
+, -Element-wise (always)
use math
W = eye[2]
x = ones[2, 1]
y = W * x                   --- matmul
a = ones[3] .* full[3, 2]   --- element-wise multiply
b = ones[3] ./ full[3, 2]   --- element-wise divide
c = full[3, 2] .^ 3    --- element-wise power
d = ones[3] * 2        --- scalar multiply

A tensor's shape is in its type. A shape the program states is known before it runs — a literal ([[1.0, 2.0], [3.0, 4.0]] is 2×2), a constructor given literal sizes (zeros[2, 3], ones, full, randn, eye[3], linspace), reshape given literal sizes, and what transpose (its last two dimensions swapped), the element-wise operators and * make of them. The type carries the shape where the tensor goes: a reassignment binds the variable anew, as a frame's does (see Frames), so after x = zeros[3, 3] it is 3×3 from that line on; where branches, or a loop's rounds, meet, it has the dimensions they agree on; a function's parameter has the dimensions its calls agree on, and a call has the shape of what the function returns. An operation two known dimensions make certainly wrong is refused before it runs, in both modes: * of tensors that are not both 2-D, or whose inner dimensions differ (matmul: incompatible shapes [2, 2] × [3, 3] — …), and an element-wise operation of two shapes that do not broadcast (each pair of dimensions, aligned from the innermost, equal or one of them 1: `+` of tensors of shapes [2, 2] and [2, 3]: dimension 2 is 2 in one and 3 in the other — …), read in a function with the parameters the tensors were handed as ((the tensors batch[] is handed as `a` and `b`)). A dimension the program computes (zeros[n, 3]), one a function's calls disagree on, and any of a tensor's once a cell or a row of it is assigned (m[0] = …) are not known, and nothing about them is refused before the program runs: an operation whose shapes do not meet stops the program where it runs, with the same words, in both modes.

A chain of element-wise operations is computed in one pass. `x * 2.0

  • x .* x - 1.0reads each element ofx` once and writes one tensor, where each operator would make a tensor of its own — across the machine's cores for a large one. Each element is what the operators compute, in their order and with their formulas, so the answer is the same to the bit, and a shape that does not meet stops with the operator's words at the first operator whose operands do not meet. A chain's operands are what only read — a variable, a fact, a derivation, a number written; one that calls something is computed where the program calls it, and its chain by the operators. The same in both modes.

Design Note: a function is one function whatever shapes it is handed. Considered: a copy of a function for each shape its calls hand it, as a frame's columns make one (see Frames), so that each copy's operations are checked against its own shapes. Rejected: a frame's columns are its row type, but a tensor compiles alike whatever its shape, so a copy per shape would grow the build for nothing but earlier refusals inside the function. Decided: a parameter has the dimensions all its calls agree on; a call whose shapes the others do not share is checked where it runs.

Scalar-array operations broadcast the scalar:

[1, 2, 3] + 10              --- [11, 12, 13]
[1, 2, 3] * 2               --- [2, 4, 6]
10 - [1, 2, 3]              --- [9, 8, 7]
-[1, 2, 3]                  --- [-1, -2, -3]

A list of numbers however it was made — grown by push, read from a file, a mask's truths as 1 and 0 — is a tensor to every operator, as it is to .*: push[push[[], 1], 2] + 10 is [11, 12], and / of one by a number divides each, [1.5, 0.5] of [3, 1] / 2. (+, -, * and / of a list grown by push had been refused, cannot use '+' with array[int] and int, where .* took it — crow check --self found it, holding its cases to Python's answers.) A tensor divided by a number divides each number, as numpy does: [3.0] / 2.5 is [1.2] (it had multiplied by 1 / 2.5, which rounds twice: 1.2000000000000002).

Internally, all-numeric arrays use a fast tensor representation for element-wise operations. This is transparent — numeric arrays behave identically to regular arrays for indexing, iteration, comparison and all builtins: two lists of numbers are eq when they hold the same numbers in the same shape, however each was built (a list grown by push and a literal, ints and the same floats, a list of rows and a matrix literal). A list of rows — tensors of one shape, grown by push from a matrix's rows, or lists of numbers of one length — is a matrix wherever a tensor is taken (transpose[rows], stack[rows], tensor[rows], mean), as a list of numbers is a vector; stack[rows, 1] stacks its columns. Rows of two shapes stop the program with the reason, in both modes (compiled, a list of rows had been refused where a tensor was taken; a list of lists of numbers had stopped the interpreter and not built compiled). A list of lists whose numbers can fail is no matrix: it is refused before the program runs, and its numbers are read first. A matrix stack makes, or tensor of a list of lists, has a rank the program knows, so its columns walk as rows and each is numbers (its columns had been read as numbers, and mean of one refused).

A tensor holds its numbers as floats, and a float holds a whole number exactly only up to 2^53 (9007199254740992): a literal list of ints past it is a list of ints, which holds each as it is (a tensor had held 9007199254740993 as …992), and a matrix literal of them is refused, in both modes. A literal list of whole numbers that a function goes on to hold as a list of ints it builds — q = [1], then q = grow[q, k] with grow pushing ints — is a list of ints from its first line (it had held floats, converted to ints and back at every call and assignment); one used as numbers stays a tensor. A computed whole number past 2^53 in a list written down as a vector stops where the list is made, in both modes — [a, 3] with a = 2 ^ 60 + 1 (Vijay, 2026-10-29: it had held 2^60 silently, and xs[0] eq a was false); a list grown with push is a list of ints and keeps every digit.

Expression Grouping

Use () to group expressions and control evaluation order. Parentheses are only for grouping — function calls always use [].

mid = (low + high) / 2
result = (a + b) * (c - d)

Comparison

No symbols — use words only.

OperatorMeaning
eqequal
nenot equal
gtgreater than
ltless than
gtegreater or equal
lteless or equal
if x eq 5
    log['equal']

if x gt 10
    log['big']

eq compares two values whole, in both modes alike: numbers by value (an int beside a float as a float: 1 eq 1.0), text by its characters, a list item by item, a map entry by entry, and a record field by field — two records are equal when they are the same struct and every field is (Point[1, 2] eq Point[1, 2]). An item or a field that failed equals nothing, not even another failure. contains compares a list's items the same way. A frame's column compared whole — with a column, a list or a tensor — is compared as a list is, cell by cell, a missing cell equal to nothing. A list compared with a single value is a mask instead (see Arrays). (Compiled, comparing two records did not build, and the interpreter answered false for records and maps with equal contents; a column of text with a missing cell had equalled itself, and a column compared with a list had not built.)

A function is no value to compare. f eq g, f eq 'a' and every order of a function value are refused before the program runs, with its name as the way to ask: describe[f].name. (The interpreter had compared by name and the compiled program by address, so same[one, one] was false in one mode and true in the other.)

Two values of two kinds are not compared. Each of the six compares a value with one of its kind: numbers with numbers (an int beside a float as a float), text with text, bools with bools, and lists, maps and records with their own kind. A number is not text, nor a bool either: '1' eq 1, true eq 1, a list of text with a list of numbers, a column of text with a number (df.name eq 2), 1 in ['a'] and push[['a'], 1] are refused before the program runs — eq compares values of one kind, not text with a number — and where a kind shows only as the program runs — a parsed value, a cell of a file's column — the comparison stops the program with the same words. A parsed value is compared with a value of its kind (j.status eq 'ok'), and JSON's null equals nothing. (Both modes had answered false for a number and text, and contains had too; compiled, 'a' eq 1 had not built; interpreted, a parsed 1 had not equalled 1.0.) A handle — a database connection, a response, a socket, a mailer — is compared with nothing, another of its kind included: each is its own, equal to no other, and conn eq conn is refused where it is written (a database connection is not compared: a handle is its own, equal to no other — compare what it holds; the interpreter had answered false, and the compiled program had not built). str of one is what text shows of it (<db:sqlite>), type its kind, and len, encode and every other function that takes something else refuse it in the table's words.

gt, lt, gte and lte order numbers with numbers and text with text, by character code — the order sort gives text: 'b' gt 'a', 'B' lt 'a', and '10' lt '9', text and not numbers. Numbers compare exactly, and NaN is equal to nothing and unordered, so of the six only ne holds for it. Bools have no order (gt compares numbers with numbers or text with text, not a bool with a bool), nor two lists, and a parsed value is read as the kind the other side says, as arithmetic on one reads it as a number (see JSON). (Compiled, true gt false and 'b' gt 'a' had answered where the interpreter stopped.)

Design Note: a number is not text. Considered: answering false for values of two kinds, as Python's == does and as both modes had; converting one side, as JavaScript, R and MySQL do ('10' < 9). Decided (Vijay, 2026-10-01: "a string is not a number"): a comparison of two kinds is the program's mistake, not a question with an answer — an identifier read as text and compared with a number matched nothing, and a filter dropped every row without a word. So all six refuse it where both kinds are known, as a statically typed language does, and stop the program where a kind shows only as it runs.

Design Note: text has an order. Considered: refusing an order between texts, as the interpreter had by stopping, so that gt is for numbers alone. Decided (2026-10-01; Vijay: text with text and numbers with numbers are compared): text is ordered by character code, the order sort already gives it — one order for text, not a second — so df[df.day gte '2026-01-01'] keeps the rows of ISO dates written as text from that day on, as the compiled program had. It is not a locale's alphabetical order ('B' lt 'a'); lower first compares without case.

Logical

and  or  not

if x gt 0 and y lt 10
    log['in range']

if not active
    log['inactive']

if x eq 1 or x eq 2
    log['one or two']

and and or decide from the left: the right side is evaluated only where the left does not decide the answer — false and … is false and true or … is true without it. An error on the left is the value, since an error has no truth value (Errors in Conditions); where the right side is evaluated, its error is the value. So a guard reads as it does in nearly every language:

if i lt len[xs] and xs[i] gt 0      --- xs[i] is read only when i is in range
    log['positive']

if r.ok and r.price gt 0            --- r's price is read only when r did not fail
    log['priced']

A call on the right runs only where the left does not decide. and and or of two lists of truths (masks) join them item by item, and both sides are evaluated. Every operator that computes both sides — a mask's and, a comparison, arithmetic — computes the left first, and what the right side needs done ahead of the statement (the refusal of a failed value it reads) waits for the left's value: put[d, f] eq 1 and latest[d].id eq ['a', 'b'] puts, then reads.

Design Note: and and or Decide From the Left

Considered: evaluating both sides, the left first, as every other operator does — the rule from 2026-09-23 to 2026-10-11: an error on either side was the value, and nothing was evaluated conditionally except by if and while.

Decision (Vijay, 2026-10-11): the right side is evaluated only where the left does not decide. Evaluating both turned the commonest guard into a stop: i lt len[xs] and xs[i] gt 0 with i past the end stopped the program, because an index outside its list stops rather than failing as a value; inside a while condition the nested if the rule prescribed meant rewriting the loop around a break, and benchmark authors padded their arrays instead (sudoku, n-queens). Measured, the cost of evaluating both was small (n-queens' gap was elsewhere; a brainfuck interpreter about 9%): the reason is the guard, not speed. Both modes decide from the same lowered program — the right side's own refusals wait under the left's decision there — so they cannot part as they did when the compiled program short-circuited on its own.

Membership

x in xs is true where xs holds x, and x not in xs where it does not — the one way to ask, in words: an item of a list, compared as eq compares; a number in a tensor; a part of text; a key of a map or of a parsed value; and over a frame's column, cell by cell, a mask (t['AA' in t.sym]). It binds as a comparison does — tighter than and and or, looser than arithmetic — so n + 1 in [3, 4] and n in [2] reads as written. not binds tighter than any comparison, as a minus sign does: not x in xs turns x over first (refused unless x is a truth), so the question is asked x not in xs, as not (x eq 5) asks the other. A value of another kind than the items is refused before the program runs, in both modes: 1 in ['a'] is "x in xs takes a list and an item of its kind: its items are text, not a number". contains and has are how it asks, so a function of either name is refused where it is declared: no call could reach it.

if node.tag not in ['button', 'input', 'select', 'summary']
    lines = lay_lines[node, inner_w]

Design Note: membership is an operator. Considered: keeping contains[xs, x] and has[m, key] as functions (rejected: node.tag ne 'button' and node.tag ne 'input' and … was written in the layout engine where a list would do, because not contains[list, node.tag] reads backwards from how it is said, and has beside contains was two words for one question); adding in beside them (rejected: two ways to write one call). Decision (Vijay, 2026-10-08): in and not in, a pair as eq and ne are, and the functions retired — contains[…] or has[…] written in a program is refused with the operator to write. The parser writes the operator as a call of the row named contains (a map's and a parsed value's rows are has's functions under that name), so nothing below it knows an operator exists, as with then.

Then Chaining

then pipes the left value as the first argument to the right function, enabling left-to-right composition instead of nested calls.

--- x then f = f[x]
s = '  hello  '
result = s then trim                --- trim[s]

--- x then f[a] = f[x, a]
result = 'hello world' then split[' '] then join['-']
--- split['hello world', ' '] then join[..., '-'] → 'hello-world'

Chains of any length:

result = s then trim then split[' '] then join[', ']

then binds tighter than comparisons and logical operators, but looser than arithmetic:

x + 1 then str          --- str[x + 1]
x then str eq '5'       --- (str[x]) eq '5'

Since all builtins are immutable and return new values, then works with every builtin:

nums = nums then sort then reverse then slice[0, 3]

A chain as lines. An assignment's chain may be written one step a line, each line starting with then, indented under the assignment. A step that is a call takes the value so far as its first argument, as above. A step that names the variable being assigned reads the value so far where it says, as often as it says, so a step may be any expression over it, and the value's kind may change along the way:

pr = frame.new[node = keys[m], value = values[m]]
    then join[pr, table, 'node']
    then max[abs[pr.value - pr.pagerank]]

pr is the frame through the join and the number at the end. Each step is a statement of its own — a refusal names the step's line, and a step whose value failed hands the failure on, as a call of a failed value does (n = '42' then int then n + 1 is 43; from 'x' it is the error, held by n). The lines belong to an assignment: a then line under anything else, or under a loop used as a value, is refused before the program runs, in both modes. Style: name a value you use across lines or want to explain, nest a value used once as a first argument, and write then lines for two or more steps.

Design Note: a chain's steps name the variable. Considered: a word for the value flowing through — it, self — as Kotlin's scope functions and magrittr's . have (rejected: a keyword on every line of every chain); the dot form, x.f[a] for f[x, a] (rejected: a second way to write every call, and the dot already reads a field — a frame's ok column is unreachable by dot today, and every function name would join it); a pipe symbol (rejected: the shift key, and the same second way); a step continued by indentation alone (rejected: indentation would mean a body under for and a step under a value); bare column names inside a step over a frame, as pandas's eval reads them (rejected: where a name came from would go unsaid, and a variable of a column's name would collide); a dotted statement that replaces its receiver, pr.join[…] (rejected: a statement that changes a variable without =, the confusion push as a statement had). Decision (Vijay, 2026-10-08): the variable being assigned names the value so far, and a step that does not name it is a call that takes it first. One rule, read either way, and a reader can expand the chain into pr = … lines knowing nothing more. The parser does exactly that, each step assigned to a hidden name so the kind may change, which is why nothing below the parser — the lowering, the IR, either backend — knows a chain exists.

5. Control Flow

if / else

if x gt 5
    log['big']

if x gt 5
    log['big']
else
    log['small']

if x gt 5
    log['big']
else if x gt 2
    log['medium']
else
    log['small']

A condition is true or false. A number, text or a list decided on — if count, while name — is refused before the program runs: compare it, count gt 0, len[xs] gt 0, name ne ''. (A number had reached the compiled program as "the lowered program disagrees with itself", a bug in Crow's words.) A value that can fail is decided on as Errors in Conditions says.

Design Note: No Pattern Matching or Sum Types

Crow intentionally omits pattern matching (match) and sum types (enums with variants).

Why no pattern matching? Without sum types, match is syntactic sugar for if/else. It adds a second way to branch, violating "one way to do things." Use if/else instead:

if value eq 0
    log['zero']
else if value gt 0 and value lt 10
    log['small']
else
    log['big']

Why no sum types? Sum types shine in systems programming and compiler design, where you model distinct variants (e.g. Shape = Circle | Rectangle). Crow targets data science and practical programming, where data lives in collections, tables, and structs — not algebraic types. Crow's error-value system (section 10) handles the most common sum type use case (success vs failure) without additional syntax.

If both were added, they would need to come together. Pattern matching without sum types is pointless sugar. Sum types without pattern matching are unusable. Neither earns its place alone in a language optimized for simplicity and LLM-friendliness.

6. Loops

for

for loops run their iterations in parallel when nothing the body does can show the order they ran in, and in order when something can (section 14). Results are always collected in the original order.

for x in [1, 2, 3]
    log[x]              --- prints 1, 2, 3: output keeps a loop in order

A loop walks a list, a tensor (its rows, or its numbers when it has one axis), a frame's column (its cells) or a parsed list. A map, text, a frame or a number has no items, and a loop over one is refused before the program runs, in both modes: walk keys[m] or values[m], split[s, ''], or a column of the frame.

When a for loop is used as a statement (not assigned to a variable), the results of each iteration are discarded. The loop runs only for its side effects:

for x in [1, 2, 3]
    log[x]               --- prints 1, 2, 3; results discarded

for as Expression

Assign a for loop to a variable to collect results into an array.

--- transform each item
doubled = for x in [1, 2, 3]
    x * 2
--- doubled eq [2, 4, 6]

--- filter items
evens = for x in [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
    if x % 2 eq 0
        x
--- evens eq [2, 4]

--- filter and transform
result = for x in [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
    if x gt 2
        x * 10
--- result eq [30, 40, 50]

An item that fails is kept, not dropped: nums = for s in ['1', 'b', '3'] / int[s] is a list of three whose second item is the error, shown by its line and reason — [1, error: line 1: cannot convert 'b' to int, 3] — as a list literal holding a failed value is. The list itself did not fail (nums.ok is true, see Error Handling); each item's own .ok says which did. Handing a failed value to a function is another matter: push[xs, int['b']] is that error, by auto-propagation. A list whose items can fail, handed whole to a function that takes numbers (sum[nums], mean[nums]), is its numbers, as a column's cells are: a failed item stops the program (item 1 of the list failed: cannot convert 'b' to int), in both modes. (Compiled, it did not build.) Compared whole with a tensor or a list of numbers (nums eq [1, 2], each item a map's read or a function's answer that can fail) it is its numbers the same way, and the comparison is one truth (the interpreter had compared the items; the compiled program had not built).

The body's last line is each round's item, so it is a value: a last line that answers nothing (log[i]) or is a loop statement (an inner for or while) is refused, with the fix — an inner list is a collecting loop held by a name (row = for b in …, then row). (The interpreter had collected an inner loop's last value, and the compiled program nothing.)

while

while loops run sequentially. They support break and continue.

while x gt 0
    x = x - 1

break and continue

break exits the loop immediately. continue skips to the next iteration.

x = 0
while x lt 100
    if x eq 10
        break
    x = x + 1

total = 0
i = 0
while i lt 10
    i = i + 1
    if i eq 5
        continue
    total = total + i

In a for loop and outside every loop break and continue are refused before the program runs: a for loop's rounds run at once, in no order, so there is no round after this one to skip to and none before it that stopping would keep — loop with while to stop early or skip in order, skip an item of a for with if, and search with find, any or all. (They had run in a for, the loop quietly made sequential; Vijay, 2026-10-08: "for is parallel, while is for sequential".)

Design Note: break and continue are only valid in while loops. for loops are parallel by default (section 14), and break/continue are inherently sequential concepts. Using break in a parallel loop would silently force sequential execution — a hidden performance trap. Use if inside for to skip items, any/all/find for searches that should stop early (they early-exit in parallel — see the Search Builtins Design Note), or while for genuine sequential iteration.

7. Functions

Declaration

Functions are declared with fn. The last expression in the body is the implicit return value. Use explicit return for early returns.

fn batch[a, b]
    a + b                    --- implicit return

fn greet[name]
    log['hello, `name`']

fn safe_div[a, b]
    if b eq 0
        return 0             --- early return
    a / b                    --- implicit return

A function answers on every path or on none. One that returns a value somewhere returns one wherever it ends: a return with no value beside it, or a body that can end without one — a loop that finds nothing, an if with no else — is refused in both modes, at the line where the value is missing (first_big[] returns an int, and its body can end here without returning one). A while true that only a return leaves ends nowhere else, and a path that ends where the program does — halt[…], exit[…] — owes no answer. A function that answers nothing, or only fails (return error[…]), may end anywhere. (The interpreter had answered done where the function's value was an int, and the compiled program had not built.)

Named Arguments with Defaults

Parameters with = value are named arguments with default values. Named arguments must come after all positional arguments. Default values must be literals (int, float, str, bool) or an empty collection ([], map[]) — expressions are not allowed, so evaluation order and scope never come into question. A parameter defaulted to [] or map[] is an empty list or map at a call that leaves it out — as a parameter handed [] is — and its items are of the kind the other calls hand it, or, where none does, of the kind the body reads of them: a parameter handed where a builtin takes numbers (tensor[pred], mse's) is a list of numbers, so the call's [] is an empty tensor where the formula computes with it (as the row had read it); chat shows each tool's schema as text ('t'), so a call naming no tools hands the wire a list of text all the same.

fn train[data, lr = 0.01, epochs = 100]
    for i in range[epochs]
        update[data, lr]

Callers can omit named arguments (uses default), provide them by name, or by position:

train[my_data]                     --- uses defaults
train[my_data, lr = 0.001]         --- override one
train[my_data, 0.001, 50]          --- positional also works

Named arguments are required for axis parameters in math operations:

s = sum[x, axis = 0]              --- sum along axis 0
mx = max[x, axis = 1]             --- max along axis 1

Named arguments are resolved at compile time (a lowering pass rewrites them to positional form before execution), so they work only when the callee is a directly named function, struct, builtin with named parameters, or ui element. A function received as a value (fn apply[f, x] calling f[...]) must be called with positional arguments.

Calling

Functions are called using [].

greet['crow']
result = batch[3, 4]

A function answers one kind. Its returns settle what it answers — a value that can fail where a branch answers an error, a float where one return is an int and another a float (§1), a tensor where one return is a list of numbers — and a return of another kind than the others settled is refused before the program runs, naming both: return 'a' beside return x of an int is returns an int where the function answers text — a function answers one kind. A function called with arguments of different kinds is a copy per signature, each answering its own (Named Arguments with Defaults; section 13's opening for the standard modules); the rule holds in each copy. (It had been the IR verifier's internal error, in both modes.)

Function Scope

A function sees its parameters, its own locals, and the program's vocabulary — other functions, structs, and builtin constants (pi, e). Module variables are invisible inside functions. The only way data enters a function is through its parameters; the only way it leaves is through its return value.

count = 5

fn build[]
    return 2 + 3          --- ok: self-contained

fn scaled[n]
    count = n * 2         --- ok: a fresh LOCAL named count —
    return count          ---     unrelated to the module variable

fn bad[x]
    --- error: 'count' is a module variable, which a function does not see —
    --- pass it as a parameter
    return x + count

count = 6                 --- ok: top-level code mutates top-level state freely
log[scaled[count]]        --- 12: pass it in

Because module variables were never visible, a local reusing a module name shadows nothing — it is simply a new binding. There are no global declarations and no scope-resolution rules to learn: inside a function, every name is a parameter, a local, or a function/struct/constant.

Exception — views and event handlers: in a ui program, top-level assignments become managed reactive state. Only view declarations and event handlers (functions referenced from views or page[]) access that state — through the state machinery, rewritten to state.get/state.set. Ordinary fn declarations are fully isolated even under use ui. That is the sanctioned mutable-state channel — owned by the runtime and synchronized, like LiveView assigns.

Exception — a route's handler reads what the app holds: a function a top-level route[…] answers with reads, by name, a derivation the app holds — one over tables, the clock and facts no session moves (Derivations, A route reads what the app holds). It still sees no module variable, and a function it calls sees no derivation: what a helper needs, the handler hands it.

A fact gets one value at the top level. Its top-level assignment declares it, and that value is the one every session starts from; a test runs the declarations and nothing else of the top level, and each declaration's value is computed before the top level's other statements run, in the order written, so a declaration may read a fact or use a connection declared above it (tripled = base * 3, made = query[book, 'CREATE TABLE …']; compiled, the first had not built and the second had stopped the program as it started, the connection not yet set). A fact holds no error: a declaration whose value can fail — a file loaded, a query, a document parsed — is refused where the facts are set, so the program stops before anything is served, runtime error: line 8: cannot assign an error value to 'trades' — load: No such file or directory (os error 2), and the fact holds the value itself: trades = load['trades.csv'] is a frame, which a page reads as one. (The interpreter had held the error in the fact, and a compiled program with such a fact had not built: a session's state could not keep it.) So a statement of the top level that changes a fact after its declaration — a second assignment, a loop that pushes into it, cfg['a'] = 2 — would reach only the rest of the top level under crow run, and no session, no test, and no declaration after it (n = len[xs] after such a loop would be the declared xs's length). It is refused: 'xs' is state, declared on line 2: it gets one value at the top level, which every session starts from, and handlers move it after that — give it its whole value where it is declared (a function can build it), or derive it. A loop of the top level may bind names of its own, which are not facts (the book's site generator writes its pages in one). A use web program that declares a derivation holds state the same way, for its routes (Derivations): its top-level names are facts, each given one value, which its derivations read — 'xs' is state, declared on line 2: a program with derivations gives each top-level name one value, which its derivations read — ….

The state is the file's that imports ui, or that imports web and declares a derivation: an entry file that does neither keeps its top-level variables plain, whatever its parts import, so a script that imports a part — to call its functions over real data — runs its top level in order, as any program does.

Design Note: No Ambient Mutability

What was considered: letting functions read (and possibly write) module variables, as in Python (global keyword), Julia, Go, or Lua.

Why it was rejected:

  1. Same reason closures were rejected. Reading ambient state is capture — a hidden data dependency that makes a function's behavior depend on things not in its signature. Purity detection, auto-testing (crow check), and refactoring all rely on parameters telling the whole story.
  2. Parallel for requires it. Iterations run concurrently, and the loop rule is sound because every read and write in the body is visible at the call site. An ambient read inside a function would be invisible, so an iteration calling f[] could race with another that writes what f secretly reads. Immutability-by-construction is the only zero-cost fix; the alternatives are interprocedural analysis or locks.
  3. The permissive languages document the damage. Python's global/UnboundLocalError is its most-asked scoping question; Julia re-fought its scope rules for two years; JavaScript needed strict mode to stop accidental globals. The concurrency-serious languages — Rust (no unsynchronized mutable statics), Erlang/Elixir (no shared mutable state at all) — sit exactly where Crow now sits.

What to use instead: parameters in, return values out. For shared mutable state across events, use the explicit managed channels: ui state, the database, files.

First-Class Functions

A function name without [] evaluates to the function itself. Functions can be passed as arguments, stored in arrays and maps, and called later with [].

fn double[x]
    return x * 2

fn apply[f, x]
    return f[x]

result = apply[double, 5]    --- 10

ops = [double, square]
ops[0][3]                     --- 6

A function handed as a value is typed by what the parameter it is handed to is applied with — apply[double, 5] types double's x as an int, by f[x] inside apply — however far it is handed on: through a function that only passes it along to the one that applies it, and to a module's function called qualified (crossval.crossval[x, y, 3, 1, fit_ridge, score_ridge] in the models package types fit_ridge by the rows crossval fits it to). A function handed as a value that nothing types — one never called directly, handed to a parameter that is only copied into a variable — is refused before the program runs, with the fix: apply it in the function it is handed to, or call it directly once. (Compiled, such a function had been left out of the program, and one handed on had been read as text.) A gradient handed as a value (bfgs[f, grad[f], x0]) is a function of what f takes, answering f's gradient — a tensor, or a float — and f is typed by what the parameter is applied with, through however many hands; a function only a test hands to grad is typed the same way. (Compiled, the parameter's answers had been read as text, and f's parameters had been guessed floats.)

A member of a list of functions is called through the list (ops[0][3]), and a function taking a function may be handed one: a call through it dispatches over the list's members, answering their one kind. Where another call hands that function a function by name, the member is refused with the fix — hand the function by name, or call the member where it is.

No Lambdas

Crow has no anonymous functions. Every function must be declared with fn and a name. To pass behavior, define a named function and pass it.

--- correct
fn is_big[x]
    return x gt 100

hit = find[items, is_big]

--- not supported
hit = find[items, fn[x] x gt 100]    --- no anonymous functions

This follows Crow's "one way to do things" principle — there is exactly one way to define a function.

8. Collections

Arrays hold ordered values of one type — an all-numeric array is a tensor (see Tensor Arithmetic and the math module). Strings index like arrays. Maps are dictionaries: string keys, values of one type. Records with mixed, named fields are structs (section 9); tables are frames (section 13).

Arrays

Arrays are functions from index to value. Negative indices count from the end: -1 is the last element, -2 is second-to-last, etc.

numbers = [1, 2, 3]
numbers[0]               --- 1
numbers[-1]              --- 3 (last)
numbers[-2]              --- 2 (second-to-last)

Negative indices also work for assignment:

numbers[-1] = 99         --- sets last element

A write lands at one position, and holds the items' kind. The position written is an int (a map's, a key of its keys' kind): a mask picks what is read, never where a write lands, so xs[mask] = v is refused where it is written. The value written is of the items' kind — text into a list of numbers is refused (an item of 'b' is a number, and the value written to it is text); a value that can fail is decided where it is written, as one assigned to a variable is; a parsed value is read as the items' kind; and a list or a map of ints written a float holds floats from then on, as a variable does (§1). Text, a frame, a frame's column and a parsed value are not written by position: text is made anew (concat[[slice[s, 0, i], 'x', slice[s, i + 1, len[s]]]]), a column whole, where the frame lies (df.name = values, see Frames), and what a parsed value holds is made into a map or a list of the program's own to change. (Each had stopped interpreted or been written there, and not built compiled — crow check --self found them.) A value with no positions — a number, a bool, a moment, a record, a sparse matrix (read whole), a response, a database connection, a socket, a mailer, the standard library's bytes (read by byte[b, i]) and its stream — is refused where it is indexed, and so is [] written and indexed at once.

A size has a ceiling. A value holds at most 2^36 items (68,719,476,736 — more numbers than any machine's memory holds): a size past it — range[2 ^ 40], zeros[n, n], repeat[s, n], arange[…], round[d, places] of a decimal — stops the program where it is asked for, before anything is made, in the same words on every machine (range: a size too large to hold (… items; at most 68719476736)). It had panicked in Rust's words, with no line compiled, or grown until the machine's memory ran out.

A mask picks. Where the index is a list of bools — what a comparison over a list or a tensor answers — the result is the items where it is true: names[labels eq j]. Of a matrix it is the rows, so mean[x[labels eq j], 0] is the centre of the rows labelled j; of a vector, the numbers: v[v gt 25.0]; and of a matrix by a mask of its own shape — what the matrix compared with a number answers — its numbers where it is true, in row order, as a vector: m[m gt 2.0] (numpy's boolean index; a mask of another shape stops). A mask of another length than the items stops. Of a frame it is the rows — df[df.age gt 50] is a frame of every column as it was — and of a frame's column its cells, a column of the same kind with its missing cells kept: df.name[df.age gt 50]; a column's .ok is a mask too, df[df.age.ok] (see Frames). A frame's index is a mask alone: a column is read as df.name, a cell as df.name[i]. A frame's column of bools is the mask it makes wherever a mask is taken — df[df.active], where[df.active, a, b], names[df.active], and negated or joined, not df.active, df.active and df.age gt 50 — as df.active eq true is, a missing cell false (it had been refused, and eq true written every time; negated it had been one bool, and joined it had not built compiled).

Design Note: a mask picks, and counts. Considered: filter[t, mask] for tensors and lists (rejected: a second verb where the index already picks — x[i] one row, x[mask] the rows); any[mask] and all[mask] (rejected: sum[mask] gt 0 and sum[mask] eq len[mask] say it, once a mask counts). Decision (Vijay, 2026-10-08): the index takes a mask, and a mask where numbers are asked is numbers — true 1, false 0 — so sum[v gt 2.0] counts, and math's where[mask, a, b] takes one as it takes numbers. The k-means centre, a loop with an index and a count, is one line.

Design Note: a mask picks a frame's rows. Considered: keeping filter[df, mask], the frame's own verb, beside the index (rejected: two ways to pick rows where the index already picks a list's items and a tensor's rows — the reason filter was rejected for those, above — and a frame's column had been the one collection a mask was refused for); pandas's index, where df[x] is a column, a list of columns or rows by what x is (rejected: a frame's index has one meaning, a mask — a column is df.name, columns select[df, names]); polars's filter alone, with no index for rows (rejected: every other collection is picked by its index). Decision (Vijay, 2026-10-16): df[mask] picks a frame's rows and df.name[mask] a column's cells, and filter is removed from the language: no builtin has the name, so a program may give it to a function of its own. A mask over the frame's own columns is computed in one pass (see Frames).

Out-of-bounds indices (positive or negative) are a runtime error.

Multidimensional Arrays

Nested brackets create multidimensional arrays. All inner arrays must be the same length.

m = [[1, 2, 3],
     [4, 5, 6]]              --- 2x3 array

m[0]                          --- [1, 2, 3] (first row)
m[1][2]                       --- 6

--- creation by shape
z = zeros[3, 4]               --- 3x4 of zeros
r = uniform[0.0, 1.0, 2, 3]   --- 2x3 random in [0, 1)

--- growing (immutable: returns a new tensor)
m2 = push[m, [7, 8, 9]]       --- append one row (shape-checked)
c = concat[m, m2, 0]          --- concatenate along an axis

--- errors: shape and type are the tensor's contract
push[m, [1, 2]]               --- error: row length 2 vs rows of length 3
push[m, 'x']                  --- error: arrays are homogeneous
[[1, 2], [3]]                 --- error: all inner arrays must be the same length

Iterating a 2-D array yields its rows; comparisons with a scalar broadcast to a boolean mask:

totals = for row in m
    sum[row]                  --- per-row sums

x = [1, 2, 3, 4]
mask = x gt 2                 --- [false, false, true, true]
big = (x gt 1) and (x lt 4)   --- masks combine element-wise

A tensor's rank is in its type, and a loop over a tensor walks rows or numbers — an index reads a row or a number — as the type decides, so both modes read a value the same way: a literal states its rank by its nesting, a constructor by its sizes, every builtin by what it answers (relu[m] is shaped like m, sum[m, 1] has one dimension fewer, flatten is a vector, transpose and * a matrix), a parameter by its calls, a record's field by what builds it, a gradient by its parameter. An item of a tensor of floats is a float, whole or not (type[x] of [2.0, 3.0]'s items is float; the interpreter had read 2.0 as the int 2). Where the rank is not known at a loop or an index — a parameter no call types, a piece of svd read by a position the program computes (parts[1] has its piece's shape, a vector of singular values; parts[i] has none) — the program is refused before it runs, with the fix: reshape[t, rows, columns] states a matrix, flatten[t] a vector. A tensor is written at the positions that read it, below.

A tensor is written where it lies, at the positions that read it. v[i] = x writes a vector's number and m[i][j] = x a matrix's — each position counted from the end when negative, and one past the end stops as reading there does. Fewer positions than axes name a part, as they do when read — m[i] is a matrix's row, c[i] a cube's matrix — and a part is written as a value of exactly its shape: m[i] = [7.0, 8.0], a list of numbers or a tensor, and c[i][j] = row. A value of another shape is refused before the program runs where both shapes are known, and stops as the program runs where one shows only then, in the same words. A number is no part: m[i] = x on a matrix is refused (and stops where the rank shows only as the program runs) — m[i][j] = x writes a number, m[i] = zeros[n] a row of zeros. A tensor held in a record's field, a list's item or a map's value is written the same way, where it is held: g.cells[i][j] = x, ms[k][i] = row, mats['a'][i] = row. (m[i] = x had written the matrix's flat number i while m[i] read the row; m[i][j] = x and any write through a field, an item or a key had stopped the interpreter and not built compiled; a row had been refused.)

Design Note: a part is written as it is read. Considered: refusing a row, the matrix built anew with concat, stack or reshape (the rule until 2026-10-16 — rejected: a row of the row's own shape has the read's one meaning, and building anew copies the whole matrix for each row written); a number spread over a row, as numpy broadcasts m[i] = 0.0 (rejected: m[i] = x would write a number or a row by the matrix's rank, the trap the flat write had been — m[i] = zeros[n] says it). Decision (Vijay, 2026-10-16): a part is written where its shape matches the place the positions name; a number written at fewer positions than axes is refused.

Design Note: rank is in the type. Considered: the interpreter deciding by the value, as it did (rows where the value had two dimensions, a whole number as an int), with the compiled program deciding by the type — the two walked the same matrix differently wherever the type knew no rank, and type[x] differed on a float tensor's items; a dynamic walk in the compiled program (rejected: the loop variable would need a type decided as the program runs, and the body with it); a vector assumed where the rank is unknown (rejected: a guess either way is the same bug with another sign). Decided (Vijay, 2026-10-03): the type decides, every builtin's answer has a rank, and where none is known the loop or index is refused before the program runs; both backends read a tensor through one runtime walk that checks the value's rank against the type's and stops with the reason, so a wrong shape rule can never make the modes differ silently. The rank and the sizes a builtin's answer has are declared on its row (2026-10-19; design rule 24), from a fixed vocabulary — an argument's own shape, the element-wise shape of several, a dimension per size written, each dimension stated, a reduction's axis dropped, a join's axis opened, the last two axes swapped, rows stacked, a decomposition's pieces by position — and read where the call is typed; the compiler holds no shape rule by name.

String Access

Strings support indexing just like arrays. Each index returns a single-character string. Negative indices count from the end.

word = 'hello'
word[0]                  --- 'h'
word[-1]                 --- 'o'
word[2]                  --- 'l'

Out-of-bounds indices are a runtime error.

len[s], slice[s, start, end], and indexing count characters, never bytes, in both execution modes: len['a—b'] is 3 and slice['a—b', 1, 2] is '—'.

Maps

Maps are created with the map[...] constructor — a variadic call with named arguments. [] is always an empty array.

items = []                               --- empty array
data = map[]                             --- empty map
counts = map[crow = 3, raven = 7]        --- map with entries
headers = map['content-type' = 'json']   --- quoted keys for special characters
by_id = map[1 = 'a', 2 = 'b']            --- keys written as ints key the map by ints

A map's keys are one kind: text, or ints. 2 and '2' are different keys, and a map holds one or the other, as it holds values of one kind. The literal's names and quoted keys are text; a key written as an int, map[2 = 'b'], keys the map by ints; map[keys, values] keys it by what the keys are (map[[1, 2, 3], ['a', 'b', 'c']], a tensor of ints too); an empty map[] is open, and its first set decides. A key of the other kind is refused before the program runs, in both modes — m['2'] of a map keyed by ints: "the map's keys are ints, not text: m[2] reads one, '2' is text" — as is a float (map[[1.5], …]: a map's keys are text or ints, not floats), and the dot form, which names a text key, is refused on a map keyed by ints (m.a: "a map keyed by ints is read by m[2]"). keys[m] answers the keys as they are, ints in order; in asks for a key of the map's kind. A parsed value's object has text keys, so a map decoded from JSON is keyed by text, and a map keyed by ints encodes its keys as text, as json.dumps does, since JSON's objects have no other kind.

scores = map[[7, 3], [0.5, 0.25]]
scores[3]                --- 0.25
keys[scores]             --- [3, 7]
3 in scores              --- true
scores['3']              --- refused: the map's keys are ints, not text

Design Note: a map's keys are typed. Considered: text keys only, a number used as a key read as its text, as JavaScript's objects and JSON do (rejected, Vijay, 2026-10-08: "2 is different from '2'", and a key put in as a number came back as text from keys[m]); keys of any kind in one map, as Lua's tables (rejected: the map's type would say nothing of its keys, and 2 beside '2' is a bug waiting). Decision (Vijay, 2026-10-08): one key kind per map, text or ints, as Python's dict and Go's map[K]V are used — the map's type carries it (Ty::Map(Key, value)), the compiled program's map is BTreeMap<String, _> or BTreeMap<i64, _> by it, the interpreter holds the two apart (Value::Map, Value::IntMap), and the eight map rows bridge both by hand, since one function cannot be instantiated twice in one closure. Floats are not keys, as in Go and for the same reason. JSON stays text-keyed both ways.

map[key = value, ...] desugars to a chain of set[] calls — it is ordinary Crow, not special syntax. For fully dynamic keys, build with set[]:

m = map[]
m = set[m, key_var, value]

Maps are homogeneous: every value must have the same type. Int and float unify (like arrays), and numbers held as a list unify with numbers held as a tensor: the map holds one form, and a value in the other is converted where it is set — map[a = [1, 2], c = []] holds lists, as a list pushed onto a list of rows is a row (it had stopped the interpreter at the tensor, expected a list, got array, and the compiled program had not built). Mixed named data belongs in a struct — and since the value type can be any type, including structs, the two compose:

struct Address [street, city, zip]

addresses = map[
    home = Address['12 Oak St', 'Austin', 78701],
    office = Address['500 Main St', 'Austin', 78702]
]

addresses.home.city                       --- 'Austin'
addresses = set[addresses, 'work', Address['9 Elm Rd', 'Dallas', 75001]]

for label in keys[addresses]
    a = addresses[label]
    log['`label`: `a.street`, `a.city`']

Mixed field types live inside the struct; dynamic keys live in the map. Adding a value of a different type is an error in both modes:

m = map[a = 1]
m = set[m, 'b', 'x']
--- error: a map holds values of one kind: the entry 'b' holds text, and the map's other values are an int —
--- a record (struct) holds fields of several kinds

Design Note: Maps Are Dictionaries, Not Records

What was considered: heterogeneous maps (map[name = 'crow', age = 3]) — mixed value types per key, as in Python dicts or Elixir maps.

Why it was rejected: sort the use cases by whether keys are known at compile time and whether values are mixed:

Homogeneous valuesMixed values
Keys known in codemap or struct — both finean anonymous struct in disguise
Keys are runtime datacounts, caches, headers — the true map usesopen-schema JSON — the Json type

The genuinely map-shaped uses (dynamic keys) are naturally homogeneous; the mixed-value uses have static keys — if every key gets its own type, the keys are code, and a struct gives them typo-checking and native compiled speed. The one dynamic-and-mixed case, open JSON, already has the Json type.

Every statically typed language lands here (Go's map[K]V vs struct, Rust's HashMap vs struct); every dynamic language that conflated them grew record types anyway (Python dataclasses, Ruby's Data.define, Elixir structs), and Julia's Dict{String, Any} shows the performance cliff heterogeneous maps cost a compiled language. Crow makes the idiom the rule: heterogeneity gets its shape from structs; keys get their freedom from maps; compose them.

Design Note: No Map Literals

What was removed: the bare map literal [name = 'crow'], which shared the array's delimiters and was distinguished only by the = inside.

Why: one bracket form with two meanings created a cascade of special cases — the empty map needed the special form map[], named arguments were ambiguous against map literals inside call brackets, and UI attribute maps forced double brackets (button[[click = inc], '+']). With the constructor: bare [...] always means an array, name = value inside call brackets always means a named argument, and map[] is just the zero-argument case of the one map constructor. The grammar has one rule per position. Precedent: Go writes map[string]int{...}, Julia uses Dict(...), Python's dict(k=v) is this exact form.

Writing the old literal produces a helpful error: map literals were removed — write map[key = value, ...] instead.

Map Access

Maps use bare names as keys. Access with dot notation or string key.

zips = map[home = 78701, office = 78702]
zips.home                --- 78701
zips['home']             --- 78701 (string key also works)

Bare-name keys are stored as strings internally. Use quoted string keys when the key is dynamic or contains special characters:

headers = map['content-type' = 'json']
key = 'home'
zips[key]                --- dynamic lookup

9. Structs

Structs are pure data — no methods. Define with struct, create with [].

What has fields. A record's are its declared fields, a frame's its columns (Frames), a parsed value's whatever it holds, read as the program runs (JSON), a map's its keys (m.name is m['name'], and a key the map lacks stops the program), a value that can fail's ok, reason, kind and trace, a tensor's shape, dtype and device, a moment's its calendar fields (Moments), a list's len. A field read of anything else — a number, text, a bool, bytes, a function, a handle — or of a field a tensor or a moment lacks, is refused before the program runs, in both modes, naming the kind and its fields where it has any: text has no field 'provider', numbers has no field 'id' — its fields are shape, dtype, device. The rule holds in every copy of a function (Calling): a guard that would skip the read as the program runs (type[c] ne 'map' or not c.id.ok, with c a tensor) does not make it one the program can write. (The interpreter had stopped at the read, or passed it under the guard, where the compiled program had not built.)

Declaration

struct Bird [name, speed]

Creation

b = Bird['crow', 50]

A field holds one kind, as a variable does — a parsed value given a field of a number, text, a bool or a moment is read as that kind, as one assigned to a variable is: its kind is what the program's records are made with, and a record made with another kind there is refused where it is made (Box's field 'v' holds a number, and this is text: a field holds one kind, as a variable does) — an int beside a float is a float, and numbers held as a list or as a tensor are one kind. (Box[2.5] beside Box['a'] had run interpreted and not built compiled.)

A field holds what it is given, a failed value included: Bird['crow', int[s]] is a bird whose speed may be an error, not an error itself, so a list gathered from such records is a list, and b.speed.ok asks the field.

Field Access

b.name                   --- 'crow'
b.speed                  --- 50

A field the record does not declare is no place to read or to write: b.color = 'black' is refused before the program runs, as b.color is.

A Record or None

A field may hold a record of its own kind, or 0 where there is none — a tree's children, a chain's next link:

struct Node [op, a, b]
leaf = Node['leaf', 0, 0]
t = Node['+', Node['*', leaf, leaf], leaf]

What holds a record or 0 is a record or none: such a field, a variable first given 0 and later a record (or the other way), a function's parameter handed either, a function's value answering either (fn child[n, side] answering n.a or n.b), and a list's items — a list of 0s an item of which is written a record, the empty buckets of a hash table (Vijay, 2026-10-29: they had stayed ints). It passes through all of them as it is, in both modes. Reading a field of none, or handing none to a function that takes a record and reads it, stops the program: cannot access field 'op' on this value. Whether a link holds none is asked by comparing it with the 0 that writes none — t.left eq 0, cur ne 0 — in both modes; a record always holds one, so leaf eq 0 is false. That is the one comparison of a record with a number: any other is refused, two values of two kinds not compared (Vijay, 2026-10-11: a leaf flag, or type[t] eq 'int', had been the only ways to tell a leaf).

fn size[t]
    if t eq 0
        return 0
    return 1 + size[t.a] + size[t.b]

type[] of none is int, as of the 0 it is written as, and of a record its name, in both modes (compiled, type[t.left] had said unknown, and of none had panicked). An int handed where a record or none is held — a field only ever given 0, handed to a function that also takes a record or none — is none when it is 0; another int stops the program: a record or 0 (none) is needed here, and this value is 7 (the lowered program had disagreed with itself, an internal error in both modes). A record's field is held the same way where it is made (Box[3] beside Box[P[1, 2]] stops at the first), and an int that can fail is refused where it is handed there: its failure would be the call's, and the check is made before it.

A record or none is shared by every value that holds it, never copied: t.left read, handed on or kept is the same record, so a persistent tree's insert builds only the path to its new leaf, and every version it was stays as it was. A write through it — t.left.key = 3 — is a write to the value written to: the record is copied first where another value still holds it, and that one keeps what it held. (Compiled, a link had been a box copied whole at every read: a treap's split copied every subtree it passed, a thousand times slower than Rust's.)

Structs with Functions

Structs have no methods. Pass structs to regular functions.

struct Bird [name, speed]

fn fly[bird]
    log['`bird.name` flies at `bird.speed`mph']

b = Bird['crow', 50]
fly[b]

10. Error Handling

Every value in Crow is a result. Fallible operations set ok to false with a reason, kind, and trace. Values auto-unwrap — you use them directly without worrying about the struct.

Fallible Builtins

These builtins can fail and return an error-value:

BuiltinWhen it fails
read[path]File doesn't exist or can't be read; a URL or an S3 object that does not answer (Files, URLs and S3)
write[path, content]File or S3 object can't be written; a URL, which is read, not written
mkdir[path]Directory can't be created
ls[path]Path doesn't exist or can't be read
rm[path]Path doesn't exist or can't be removed
