The interpreter could do it (measured: ~4-5% on a scalar-heavy loop), but the two modes must agree, so the feature would have been interpreter-only.

Why this design: freshness is tracked where data enters and checked before it is trusted — which is what every system that got burned by stale data already does: HTTP's Age header, ROS message stamps, dbt source freshness, stream-processing watermarks. The flow-through-computation case moves to crow check, which is the same answer compiled languages reached for taint tracking: runtime labels stayed in interpreted languages, while compiled ones (Java's Checker Framework, FlowDroid) moved propagation into static analysis. Deferred: declarative thresholds on the reader itself (dbt's warn_after shape), which would fit a future store declaration.

Design Note: Adopting External Ground Truth

A remote system's state cannot be made atomic by any language. Observations of it are delayed and transiently wrong — a position row reads flat for a moment while a fill settles, an order looks missing before it appears. The doctrine: never act on an external absence until it has been stably absent. A grace window is freshness inverted — where captured asks "is this recent enough to trust?", adopting an absence asks "has this been true long enough to believe?":

if flat[position] and now[] - first_seen_flat gt 15.0
    mark_closed[sym]

The authoritative source is the external system, not a cached row derived from it: derive closure from order status and fills, not from a position snapshot. No primitive can enforce this — it is a modeling rule, and the reason it is written down here is that every system that skipped it rediscovered it as an incident.

Design Note: A Moment Was a Number

Superseded on 2026-10-06 by "a moment is one kind" (Moments, above): a moment is a kind of its own, an instant and the offset it was written in. This note is kept for what it decided first and what still holds.

What was considered: the design before it — now[]/date[] returning component maps and elapsed[a, b] returning a duration map. It could not express a named timezone, parse a timestamp, add a duration, or compare two moments directly; worse, now[] built its components in machine-local time while date[] timestamped at UTC midnight, so mixed arithmetic was already silently wrong by hours.

What it decided: a moment as epoch seconds, so that comparison, subtraction and "ten seconds before the bell" were ordinary arithmetic on floats, with calendars and zones as named-argument functions. What still holds: a duration is a number of seconds, with no duration kind; zones are always explicit and the default is UTC — machine-local time is absent by design, since "correct on my laptop, wrong on the UTC server" is the classic deploy-day bug, and a language that makes the timezone explicit never has it. elapsed, date and today went with the component maps: a moment subtracted from a moment is the seconds between, moment[…] makes a date, and fmt[now[], style = 'date'] is today's.

12. Modules and Imports

Importing

Use use to import a module. The entire module is imported — no selective imports. Multiple modules can be imported on a single line with commas.

use math
use views.home
use web, ui

Dot-separated paths map to directories: use views.home loads views/home.co, and its exports are reached qualified through the last segment — home.render, home.message. Slash paths (use lib/utils → utils.helper[...]) work the same way. This is how you import view files, library modules, etc.

A module of the program's takes no standard module's name. A use of a standard module's name where a module of the program's of that name lies — a math.co in the project, or a package math under crow_modules/ — would name two modules, and is refused at the import, in both modes, naming the file and the two ways out: 'math' names two modules, the standard library's and math.co in the project — rename math.co and import it by its new name (my_math.co, `use my_math`), or move it out of the project to import the standard one; what the program calls through the import is that mistake's, and is not refused again (Names Are Never Ambiguous). The standard library's own sources import each other beside their own files, and are not the program's. (The import had reached the standard module silently, and the file's math.double[5] failed only as the program ran, interpreted — examples/multifile's math.co, since renamed arith.co.)

Comma-separated imports expand at parse time: use web, ui is equivalent to writing use web and use ui on separate lines.

Each file's imports are its own. A file sees the modules it imports and no other: a part's use frame serves the part, not the file that uses the part, so an entry that calls frame.new[…] or join[…] with no use frame of its own is refused for the import (frame.new[] is frame's: add `use frame` ), whatever its parts import — as every file is when it is checked alone. (The entry had reached a module through a part's import, and lost it when the part's import went; found in October 2026, writing the error corpus.) The program still links every module its parts import; only each file's names resolve against its own imports. What the part declares is reached as before, qualified: helpers.table_of[…]. A part that does not parse is refused by its own file and line (views/sim.co: line 60, col 5: …), as a part that fails its checks is; it had been dropped, and the entry told its name was undefined.

Aliased Imports: use X as y

Any import may take an alias. An aliased import is qualified-only under its alias — exactly Python's import pandas as pd: nothing enters your bare namespace except the alias itself.

use math as m

sum = 0                --- legal: math's names were never dumped
t = m.sum[xs]          --- the module is reached through the alias

use lib/scene as s     --- user modules too: s.mesh[...], s.group[...]

For the stdlib this doubles as the opt-out when you want a name the module owns (sum, mean, e). For packages and user modules — which are always qualified-only — the alias shortens long prefixes and resolves the otherwise-impossible case of two modules sharing a trailing name (use alice/stats as astats). as is contextual: it only has meaning after a use path, so a variable named as stays legal.

Accessing

After use, all module functions become top-level — no prefix needed.

use math

result = sqrt[16]    --- 4
x = zeros[3, 3]     --- 3x3 tensor of zeros

Conflict Resolution

When two imported modules export the same function name, bare calls are ambiguous. Crow throws an error and requires the module prefix to disambiguate.

use math
use frame

--- ERROR: 'stack' is ambiguous — provided by math and frame
t = stack[[a, b], 0]

--- Correct: use module prefix
t = math.stack[[a, b], 0]
both = frame.stack[top, bottom]

--- No conflict: only frame exports group
g = group[sales, 'region', ['amount', 'sum']]

Rules:

  • If only one module exports a name, bare calls work.
  • If multiple modules export the same name, bare calls error with a list of qualified alternatives.
  • Inside a view, an element name is the element: stack[...] is the layout container even with frame and math (which both export stack) imported. The prefix (frame.stack[a, b]) reaches the module function from inside a view.
  • A frame function given something other than a frame is the core builtin of the same name: with use frame, sort[xs] sorts the array and join[parts, sep] joins strings, while sort[df, 'col'] and join[a, b, 'key'] are the frame operations. The first argument decides.
  • The element-wise math functions (exp ln log2 log10 sign sin cos tan asin acos atan sinh cosh tanh) take a plain number as well as a tensor and return a number for a number.
  • Module-prefixed calls (module.fn[args]) always work regardless of conflicts. One of a name the module does not have is refused before the program runs, in both modes, the nearest of its names offered where one is a slip away (math has no 'meen' — did you mean 'mean'?), as every module's is. (A standard module the program imports was among the names its import makes, so that its constants read, and math.double[5] had been let by, typed as nothing known, to fail as the program ran, interpreted.)

User Modules: File Structure

A source file ends in .co; no other file is a Crow source. Each file is a module. The file path is the module path, rooted at the project: the nearest directory above the entry file that holds a crowfile, or the entry file's own directory when there is none. So views/sim.co reaches sim/clock.co with use sim.clock whichever file is the entry. A part is validated alone as it is expanded — its own names unprefixed, its imports inlined — so a name the part reserves by what it imports (derive progress beside use ui), or a handler nothing in the part wires, is refused whichever file is the entry, under the part's path: crow test part.co and crow run main.co see the same part. Any error found in a part — alone or in the whole program — names the part and its own line (sim/session.co: line 60: …), never a line of the entry. In the whole program a part keeps its own names: its facts and derivations carry its path, so a local or parameter of one of its functions, views, handlers or derivations is that body's own, whatever another file holds or imports under the same name — a local ch beside the entry's connection ch, a parameter secs beside the entry's derive secs, a list fills it builds beside the entry's derive fills (a derivation that calls the function reads none of them), a list rsi where another part imports chart bare, a derivation's local c or a handler's local r or parameter s beside the entry's top-level c, r and s. (The expansion gives such a local the part's path as a suffix — c__sim_session — which an error inside the part may show.)

Design Note: The Extension Is .co

What was considered: .crow (the name, four letters), .cw, .w, .cr, .o, .ro, .caw, .co.

Why .co: two letters that say themselves, the head of the name, and no language owns it — its only other users are a few 3D and colour-profile data formats that never meet an editor's language mode or GitHub's detection. .cr is Crystal's, .w is OpenEdge's and CWEB's, .o is every compiled object file on Unix, .cw cannot be said aloud, .ro reads as read-only. .crow was fine and unambiguous; it lost to brevity in a file tree of short part names (scan.co, nav.co). The Markdown fence language stays crow, since that names the language, not the file. Decided September 2026, while the tree was one repository and one app. .crow and .crowc were read for a release after the rename, with a note, so a tree could be renamed at leisure; that went in October 2026 (one extension, not two — design rule 1), with the editor association.

--- file: myapp/utils.co

fn double[x]
    return x * 2

--- file: main.co

use myapp/utils

result = utils.double[5]    --- 10

A module is a part. It may hold facts, derivations, handlers, views and test blocks, not only functions — the clock of a simulation, its tape, its blotter — and the page that composes them imports each one. The front end expands every module file once, whichever files import it: its names take the module's path as a prefix (sim/clock.co's head is sim_clock_head inside the program) and every importer's qualified spelling — clock.head, or an alias's — is rewritten to that name, in code and inside interpolation spans. A module reached through two importers is one copy with one state. A handler in one part may assign another part's fact by its qualified name (session.day = d), and it is checked like any fact write. A part's test blocks run with crow test sim/clock.co, with the parts it imports inlined; crow check main.co checks the whole app. A module's top-level statements other than declarations are not part of the program.

13. Standard Library

The modules that ship with Crow. Their exports are bare names in files that import them (use math), qualified-only under an alias (use math as m), and always reachable with the prefix (math.sum[...]) — see section 12.

A standard module written in Crow (src/modules/*.co; design rule 20) declares its functions by writing them: its top-level functions whose names begin with no underscore are its exports, and a function whose name begins with one underscore (_talk_of) is a helper, the module's own, which no program reaches. An export is inlined into the program that imports it and typed by its use, as any function of the program is — a copy per signature where programs hand it different kinds — its options are its parameters' defaults (chat[m, talk, system = '', tools = [], tokens = 1024, temperature = 1.0]), and a value the program holds crosses to it as itself. A misuse is refused before the program runs where the body first cannot take the kind — in the export's body or in a helper's, each call of a row held to the row's kinds under the module's own imports — the refusal naming the function the program called and the program's line (in relu: clip[] takes numbers, got an int; inside a helper, the export whose call made the copy that was refused — a helper two exports share is blamed on the one whose call it was refused under, not on the first export that reaches it (errors j80): in latest: lt compares numbers with numbers or text with text, not text with a number); a stop inside one names the program's line too. The builtin table still states the contract of a function the language cannot yet declare in Crow — an effect the session layer holds (read, write, request, mail, a socket's send and recv, the readers of files and URLs), a rest parameter (randn[2, 3], anova[a, b, c]), a driver's function the database runtime calls — each the subject of a later phase of docs/extending-crow.md.

Magpie — the log as a directory of batch files, with its Kafka and Redis doors — is a package, not the standard library — a repository of its own (github.com/agncrow/magpie; its packages' READMEs and REFERENCE.md) — written over sys and memory, as any package that needs them may be, and imported as a dependency is (use magpie, use magpie.kafka; section 18). Crow's gate runs its suite before a change to the compiler lands.

Design Note: what stays native, and why. Considered: the standard library wholly in Crow, Rust kept for the compiler alone. Decided (design rule 20; Vijay, 2026-10-04): native code stays where one of four reasons holds, and every crate the manifests name (Cargo.toml, crow_runtime/, crow_web/, crow_db/) is accounted for here — a crate added gets its line, with its reason.

  • A mechanism of the runtime, which no formula can be: the server and its pages (axum, tokio, tower, tokio-stream, futures-util) and what runs inside its request handling — multipart uploads (multer), CORS and static files (tower-http), the signed sign-in cookie and session ids (hmac, sha2, base64, uuid), a live page's fetch[] sources polled by the server (reqwest); the database handles — their pools, a handler's transaction, the race of a query against a stop (crow_db::driven); the drivers themselves are written in Crow (docs/db-in-crow-design.md); the seeded process-global random source (rand); the autograd tape; parallel loops (rayon); a record's link to a record of its kind, shared by every value that holds it and copied only where one of them writes through it (triomphe: one count and no weak one, so a node of two links and a flag keeps the 32-byte slot its box took).
  • A validated kernel: TLS (rustls, webpki-roots); the digests, the keyed digests and the password hash (sha2, sha1, md-5, hmac, argon2), randomness from the operating system (getrandom); regular expressions (regex); the byte search the CSV reader splits lines and fields with, vectorised (memchr); the formats a table travels in to and from other tools (arrow, for the web's Arrow responses and reading Parquet; parquet, for frame_of_parquet and save; serde_json; bytes) — never Magpie's own files, which are its own format, its own encodings and its own codec, with no library between the frame and the disk (Magpie's magpie-design.md, phase 2; Vijay, 2026-10-26: nothing third-party in Magpie); linear algebra, the FFT, sparse matrices and the GPU (faer, rustfft, sprs, wgpu, pollster, bytemuck — the GPU's linked only by a program that writes gpu[…]); time zones (chrono, chrono-tz — by the database chrono-tz carries, never the system's local zone, whose lookup links CoreFoundation); markdown (pulldown-cmark); a number written and read exactly (itoa, ryu), erf to the last bit (libm); whole numbers of any size, decimals to any digits with their roots, powers, logarithms and trigonometry correctly rounded, and fractions (dashu-int, dashu-float, dashu-ratio); the distributions drawn by rejection or a ziggurat — Poisson, Bernoulli, gamma, beta and exponential (rand_distr).
  • A C library: SQLite (rusqlite); the memory allocator of the interpreter and of every compiled program (mimalloc): the system allocator on macOS reads the clock in its free and frees a value another thread made slowly, and a program allocates for nearly every text and list it makes — revcomp and k-nucleotide run on every core spent half their time there, interpreted, and compiled binarytrees nine times its CPU.
  • The compiler's own tooling: the Jupyter kernel (zeromq, libc), the REPL (rustyline), the language server (lsp-server, lsp-types), crow check's SQL facts (sqlparser; the database module's query provider in crow_db reads statements with it too), the kernel's messages and the AST's operators read by their names (serde), the served harness's client (tungstenite, in the compiler alone).

Written in Crow on the sys layer, their crates gone: the HTTP client and every reader of a URL or an S3 object (ureq), tokens (jsonwebtoken), msgpack (rmp-serde), the WebSocket client (tungstenite, from the web runtime) and mail (lettre); and written in Crow by the package that needs them, their crates gone too: the gzip and snappy decoding of a Kafka producer's message sets (flate2, snap — an inflate by RFC 1951 with the gzip wrapper, and snappy's block format, the Magpie package's inflate.co and snappy.co, held to the formats' reference vectors); and, with no crate to drop, the statistics (math.stats) and the normal draws (randn and normal: Box–Muller over uniform draws). The database drivers too (klickhouse; tokio-postgres, deadpool-postgres and crow_db's tokio), measured first (2026-10-05, on one machine, three columns of a million rows from numbers and generate_series): the protocol written in Crow with the rows decoded straight into a frame by a kernel matches the native drivers or beats them, in both modes: ClickHouse over its HTTP interface — exchange in Crow, frame_of_rowbinary for the rows — read a million rows in 52 ms against klickhouse's 85 ms (two hundred thousand in 16 ms against 22 ms; five thousand in 2 ms against 1.4 ms, a connection opened per request), every column typed as the server names it; the Postgres wire protocol in Crow — the startup, a simple query, the answer read in large pieces and decoded by frame_of_postgres — read a million rows in 0.25 s against tokio-postgres's 0.24 s, the server's own time. The rows parsed by a parser written in Crow instead took 1.3 s compiled and 3.7 s interpreted (CSV through the frame_of_csv kernel, 77 ms): per-cell work in Crow pays the language's price per operation — a copy, an allocation, a Result — where a kernel pays per byte, which is design rule 20's line. The drivers are called from Rust — the schema modes at connect, a handler's transaction, a derivation's race of a query against a stop, fixture replay, the rehearsal, crow db diff — through a table of calls (crow_db::driven::Calls) a compiled program registers at its start and the interpreter answers from a child of its own; the compiler's describe asks a crow db describe process (docs/db-in-crow-design.md). What runs inside the Rust server's request handling moves only once the server can call Crow, or the design changes — a live page's sources polled by the program itself, with request.

math

The math module is built in — no file needed. It provides tensor creation, element-wise operations, reductions, shape operations, and linear algebra. After use math, all functions are called directly (e.g. zeros[3], not math.zeros[3]).

Tensor Creation

FunctionDescription
zeros[d1, d2, ...]Tensor of zeros with given shape
ones[d1, d2, ...]Tensor of ones with given shape
full[d1, d2, ..., value]Tensor filled with value (last arg)
eye[n]n×n identity matrix
onehot[targets, classes]A row per target, 1 in its class's column and 0 elsewhere: the targets a vector of whole numbers from 0 to classes − 1, and the classes 1 or more (a target that is no class stops the program: onehot: 7 is not a class of 3 (0 to 2))
diag[tensor]Extract diagonal from a 2D tensor
linspace[start, end, n]n evenly spaced values from start to end
arange[start, end, step]Values from start to end by step
randn[d1, d2, ...]Normal random values (mean=0, std=1); written in Crow (see Random Numbers)
tensor[array]Convert an array to a tensor

Element-wise Operations

FunctionDescription
exp[t]Element-wise exponential
ln[t]Element-wise natural log
log2[t]Element-wise base-2 logarithm
log10[t]Element-wise base-10 logarithm
sqrt[t]Element-wise square root
abs[t]Element-wise absolute value
sign[t]Element-wise sign (-1, 0, or 1)
floor[t]Element-wise floor
ceil[t]Element-wise ceiling
round[t]Element-wise round to nearest
clip[t, lo, hi]Clamp values to [lo, hi]
mod[t, divisor]Element-wise modulo
sin[t]Element-wise sine

Reductions

FunctionReturns
sum[t]Sum of all elements (float), added as numpy adds them — pairwise, so the error grows as log n and sum[x] is numpy.sum(x) to the last digit; of a list of ints, an int with every digit (sum[xs] % 10 of digits pushed one by one; Vijay, 2026-10-29) — a list written down is a vector, and a list whose ints can fail (int[c] of text) is read as numbers, so either sums to a float
mean[t]Mean of all elements (float): the sum over the count
min[t]Minimum element (float): NaN where the tensor holds one, as numpy's and IEEE 754's minimum answer and as sum carries one; of no numbers the program stops (min of nothing: an empty tensor has no smallest number), as numpy and Python raise — min[t, axis] of an empty axis too. (Until 2026-10-18 a NaN had been passed over and an empty tensor answered inf.)
max[t]Maximum element (float): NaN where the tensor holds one; of no numbers the program stops (max of nothing: an empty tensor has no largest number)
prod[t]Product of all elements (float)
argmax[t]Index of maximum element (int)
argmin[t]Index of minimum element (int)

All reductions except argmax/argmin accept an optional axis parameter: sum[t, axis] reduces along that axis and returns a tensor instead of a scalar.

Sorting & Selection

FunctionReturns
argsort[t]The indices that would sort the numbers, ascending: a list of ints, as range answers one, so xs[order[0]] reads the smallest (it had answered a tensor, whose items read as floats and indexed nothing)
topk[t, k]Top k largest values, sorted descending (NumArray)
unique[t]Unique values, sorted (NumArray)
where[cond, a, b]Element-wise: a where cond≠0, else b (NumArray); cond a mask too — where[v gt 15.0, v, 0.0 * v] — as a mask is numbers where numbers are asked (true 1, false 0; sum[v gt 15.0] counts)

Note: sort[t] is a core builtin (not in the math module) that works on both arrays and tensors.

Cumulative Ops

FunctionReturns
cumsum[t]Cumulative sum (NumArray, same shape)
cumprod[t]Cumulative product (NumArray, same shape)

Combining Ops

FunctionReturns
stack[tensors]Stack tensors along new axis 0 (NumArray)
stack[tensors, axis]Stack tensors along new axis (NumArray)

Note: concat[a, b, axis] is a core builtin (not in the math module) — one merge operation for all arrays and tensors. It replaced the former batch.

Note: slice[t, start, end] is a core builtin (not in the math module) that works on arrays, tensors, and strings.

Broadcasting

Element-wise operations on tensors with different shapes automatically broadcast following NumPy rules:

  • Dimensions are right-aligned
  • Size-1 dimensions expand to match the other tensor
  • Missing dimensions are treated as size 1
use math
a = reshape[linspace[1, 3, 3], 3, 1]   --- shape [3, 1]
b = reshape[linspace[10, 40, 4], 1, 4]  --- shape [1, 4]
c = a + b                                          --- shape [3, 4]

Broadcasting works with all element-wise operators: +, -, .*, ./, .^.

Incompatible shapes (e.g. [3] + [4]) are refused before the program runs where both shapes are known (see Tensor Arithmetic), and stop the program where it runs where they are not, with the same words in both modes.

Advanced Indexing

Tensors can be indexed with another tensor to gather elements by index:

a = linspace[10, 50, 5]
indices = linspace[0, 2, 3]
y = a[indices]                    --- gathers elements at indices 0, 1, 2

Negative indices are supported (wrap around from the end). The result tensor has the same shape as the index tensor.

Shape Operations

FunctionDescription
reshape[t, d1, d2, ...]Reshape to new dimensions
flatten[t]Flatten to 1D
transpose[t]Transpose (swap last two axes)

Random & Distributions

FunctionDescription
seed[n]Set the program's RNG seed: every later draw, tensors and the core rand[] alike, is repeatable and identical in both modes. Statements run in the order written, and a loop that draws runs in order (§14), so a seeded program draws the same numbers on every run. A page's (a program that holds state) declarations are set before its other top-level lines run, so its top-level seed[n] runs before them, wherever it is written: the facts' draws are seeded too (Vijay, 2026-10-29: they had been drawn unseeded)
randn[d1, d2, ...]Standard normal (mean=0, std=1): Box–Muller over two uniform draws a value, written in Crow (src/modules/math.co, design rule 20); a negative size stops the program
normal[mean, std, d1, d2, ...]Normal with custom mean/std: mean + std · randn, written in Crow; a negative std stops the program
uniform[low, high, d1, d2, ...]Uniform in [low, high): uniform[0.0, 1.0, 2, 3] is a 2×3 tensor of values in [0, 1)
poisson[lambda, d1, d2, ...]Poisson distribution
bernoulli[p, d1, d2, ...]Bernoulli (0.0 or 1.0)
gamma_dist[alpha, beta, d1, ...]Gamma distribution of shape α and rate β — the mean α/β, as the (α, β) of the textbooks, R, Stan and PyTorch: gamma_dist[1.0, λ, n] is exponential[λ, n]'s distribution (until 2026-10-18 β was a scale, the mean α·β)
beta_dist[alpha, beta, d1, ...]Beta distribution
exponential[lambda, d1, ...]Exponential distribution
shuffle[x]Random permutation of tensor elements
choice[x, n]Random sample of n elements (no replacement)
permutation[n]Random permutation of 0..n

rand is core's: rand[] is one float in [0, 1), and rand[n] and rand[min, max] are ints (see Core Builtins). A tensor of values in [0, 1) is uniform[0.0, 1.0, d1, …].

Design Note: no tensor rand. Considered: rand[d1, d2, …] for a tensor of uniform values in [0, 1), as NumPy spells it. The math module had it until March 2026, when its functions became callable without math. and core's rand kept the name; this table went on listing it, and its examples made ints (rand[2, 3] is 2 or 3). Rejected: rand[5] and rand[2, 3] are core's, an int below 5 and an int from 2 to 3, so the same call would mean two things, and use math would change what a program computes. Decided: uniform[0.0, 1.0, …] makes the tensor, the one way to.

fft2[m]2D FFT → returns [real, imag] tensors
conv[a, b]1D convolution (full), length = len(a)+len(b)-1; an empty signal or kernel stops the program, as numpy's refuses it (conv[[], []] had overflowed counting its answer)
conv2d[a, b]2D convolution (full); an empty signal or kernel stops the program, as conv's does
hamming[n]Hamming window of length n
hanning[n]Hanning (Hann) window of length n

FunctionDescription
gamma[x]Gamma function Γ(x), element-wise
erf[x]Error function erf(x), of a number or element-wise over a tensor (it had taken a tensor only)
fma[a, b, c]a × b + c rounded once: the fused multiply-add a compiled loop accumulates with (pandas's rolling kernels, scikit-learn's row norms, scipy's sparse products), so a sum held to one of them runs with it — total = fma[x, x, total]
bessel[x, n]Bessel function J_n(x), element-wise

Complex Numbers

Complex values are represented as [real_tensor, imag_tensor] pairs.

FunctionDescription
complex[re, im]Create complex value → [real, imag]
real[c]Extract real part tensor
imag[c]Extract imaginary part tensor
magnitude[c]Magnitude |c| = sqrt(re² + im²)
phase[c]Phase angle = atan2(im, re)
conjugate[c]Complex conjugate → [re, -im]

Sparse Arrays

Sparse matrices store only non-zero entries, using CSR (Compressed Sparse Row) format internally. They use the same operators as dense arrays — the runtime dispatches to sparse-optimized algorithms automatically.

FunctionDescription
sparse[dense_array]Convert dense 2D array to sparse
sparse[rows, cols, vals, shape]Create sparse from COO triplets (row indices, column indices, values, [nrows, ncols])
dense[s]Convert sparse matrix back to dense array
nnz[s]Number of stored non-zero entries
spmv[s, v]Sparse matrix × dense vector (the same product as s * v)
s * vSparse × dense matmul (vector or matrix)
s + s2Sparse + sparse addition
solve[s, b]Solve sparse linear system s·x = b
use math

--- dense to sparse
s = sparse[eye[3]]
log[s]                              --- sparse[3x3, nnz=3]

--- COO creation
s2 = sparse[[0, 1, 2], [0, 1, 2], [5, 6, 7], [3, 3]]

--- sparse matmul
r = s * [1, 2, 3]

--- sparse add
s3 = s + s2

--- back to dense
d = dense[s3]

--- sparse solve
x = solve[s2, [10, 12, 14]]

Data Loading

FunctionDescription
load[path]Load a table of numbers directly into a 2D tensor: a CSV file, or JSON records or Parquet, from a file, a URL or an S3 object (error-value on failure)

load[path] reads a CSV file with a header row and parses all values as floats into a 2D tensor (rows × columns). This is much faster than csv[path] (io module) for numeric data because it avoids creating intermediate string maps.

use math

data = load['data.csv']
log[data.shape]        --- [1000, 10]
log[sum[data]]         --- total of all values

Statistics (sub-module: math.stats)

Import with use math.stats. Functions become top-level after import.

FunctionDescription
median[x]Median of a 1D array
std[x]Population standard deviation
var[x]Population variance
percentile[x, p]p-th percentile (0-100) with linear interpolation
zscore[x]Z-score normalization: (x - mean) / std → returns array
corr[x, y]Pearson correlation coefficient between two arrays
cov[x, y]Population covariance between two arrays
ttest[x, y]Welch's two-sample t-test → returns map with t and p
linreg[x, y]Simple linear regression → returns map with slope, intercept, r2
chisq[observed, expected]Chi-square goodness-of-fit test → map with chi2, p
anova[g1, g2, ...]One-way ANOVA → map with f, p
kstest[data, dist_name]Kolmogorov-Smirnov test → map with d, p. Distributions: "normal", "uniform"
fit[data, dist_name]Distribution fitting (MLE) → map of parameters. Distributions: "normal" → {mu, sigma}, "exponential" → {lambda}, "uniform" → {a, b}, "poisson" → {lambda}

A statistic of no values is NaN. The series a function pairs up (corr, cov, linreg, chisq) are one length, percentile's p is from 0 to 100, anova compares two groups or more, and kstest and fit know the distributions above; anything else stops the program with the reason (see When a Program Stops). They had answered NaN, 0.5 or an empty map.

The module is written in Crow (src/modules/stats.co, design rule 20): each statistic is a formula over the math builtins — a median the middle of sort, a variance the mean of squared distances, a regression its sums of products — and the tests' p-values are the t, F and chi-square distributions written over the sys layer's betainc and gammainc, which stay validated kernels. Its functions are its declaration (section 13's opening; anova, whose arguments are the groups, keeps its row), typed by their use in both modes; its tests are the module's own (crow test src/modules/stats.co): each statistic at known values, and its laws over a spread of inputs (a median is the 50th percentile, a series' covariance with itself is its variance, a correlation is symmetric and within −1 and 1, a z-score keeps nothing of the scale, a line through its own points has r² 1).

use math
use math.stats

x = linspace[1, 10, 10]
log[median[x]]            --- 5.5
log[std[x]]               --- 2.872...
log[var[x]]               --- 8.25
log[percentile[x, 25]]    --- 3.25

z = zscore[x]             --- mean=0, std=1

y = linspace[2, 20, 10]
log[corr[x, y]]           --- 1.0 (perfect correlation)
log[cov[x, y]]            --- 16.5

result = ttest[x, y]
log[result.t]                   --- t-statistic
log[result.p]                   --- p-value

Neural Networks (sub-module: math.nn)

Import with use math.nn. Functions become top-level after import.

Activations:

FunctionDescription
relu[x]Rectified linear unit: max(0, x) element-wise (NumArray)
sigmoid[x]Sigmoid: 1/(1+exp(-x)) element-wise (NumArray)
softmax[x]Softmax: exp(x)/sum(exp(x)) with numerical stability (NumArray)
gelu[x]Gaussian error linear unit element-wise (NumArray)

Loss functions:

FunctionDescription
mse[pred, target]Mean squared error (Float)
crossentropy[logits, targets]Cross-entropy loss with log-sum-exp stability (Float): logits [classes] with one target, or [batch, classes] with a target per row, each a class from 0 up to classes − 1
bce[pred, target]Binary cross-entropy with clamping (Float)

Layers:

FunctionDescription
linear[x, w, b]Linear layer: x @ w + b (NumArray)

math.nn is written in Crow (src/modules/nn.co) over the math builtins, the first of the standard modules that are (design rule 20): each function is the formula the table above states — relu is clip[x, 0.0, inf], sigmoid is 1.0 / (1.0 + exp[-x]), crossentropy the mean over the rows of log(sum(exp(row))) − row[target] with the row's largest score taken out of the exponentials first — and each is its own declaration (section 13's opening): what it takes is what its formula takes (a tensor, which a list of numbers becomes where the formula computes with it), what it answers its formula's, how it fails its formula's words, on the program's line. The module is inlined into every program that imports it and runs in both modes as the program's own functions do; under grad its formulas are differentiated as any function is, so no rule of their own exists to disagree with them (Automatic Differentiation). Its tests are its own: crow test src/modules/nn.co runs the formulas at known values, their laws over a spread of inputs, and a gradient through every one against central differences.

Shapes mse cannot broadcast or linear cannot multiply (x and w matrices, x's columns as many as w's rows), two lengths given to bce, and a crossentropy target that is no class (or not a whole number, or one too few or too many for the rows of logits) stop the program with the reason, on the line of the call in the program (see When a Program Stops): linear: x has 3 columns and w 2 rows — x * w needs them equal, crossentropy: target 7 is not a class of 3 (0 to 2).

Design Note: a module written in Crow declares its functions by writing them. Considered (decided 2026-10-04, undone 2026-10-18): the builtin table stating every builtin's contract whether its body is Rust or Crow — a row per Crow-written function, the arguments read as the row's parameters take them (a list of numbers converted to a tensor), a misuse refused with the row's own words (relu[] takes numbers, got an int), a value JSON can hold handed to the function as its JSON text and read back with json[…]. Rejected now: the compiler then names every module's function (design rule 24), a module cannot be written without a row written in Rust beside it, and the row was a second signature that drifted from the function's. Decided (2026-10-18): a module's exported functions are its declaration (section 13's opening) — inlined where the program reaches them, by a bare name, the module's path, an alias, or as a value, and typed by their use, a copy per signature; a helper begins with an underscore. A misuse is refused where the formula first cannot take the kind, the refusal naming the function the program called and the program's line (in relu: clip[] takes numbers, got an int); the body's own stops name that line too, never a line of the module's source. A module's function is read in the module's own scope: the program's functions, imports, aliases and elements are invisible in it — the protocol's local lines, indexed lines[0], stays its local in a program with a lines of its own, and a program names its functions as it likes (parity 162) — and a module's functions are inlined only where the program reaches them: a program that uses math without load carries none of the protocol. A function that reads a value JSON can hold (sign's claims, msgpack's value) takes the value the program holds and reads it as its JSON inside (json[encode[claims]]). The rows left (an effect's, a rest parameter's, a driver's) keep the earlier rule until their phases; the IR verifier holds each one's function to its row.

use math
use math.nn

x = linspace[-2, 2, 5]
log[relu[x]]              --- [0, 0, 0, 1, 2]
log[sigmoid[x]]           --- [0.119..., 0.269..., 0.5, 0.731..., 0.881...]
log[softmax[x]]           --- probabilities summing to 1

pred = linspace[0.1, 0.9, 3]
target = linspace[0, 1, 3]
log[mse[pred, target]]    --- 0.00667

input = reshape[linspace[1, 4, 4], 2, 2]
w = eye[2]
b = ones[2]
log[linear[input, w, b]]  --- input + bias

Constants

ConstantDescription
piπ = 3.14159...
eEuler's number e = 2.71828...
infPositive infinity
nanNot a number

Properties

PropertyDescription
.shapeArray of dimension sizes
.lenIts length, as len[t] gives it: a vector's numbers, a matrix's rows
.dtypeData type ('float')
.deviceWhere the tensor lives ('cpu' or 'gpu')
.gradGradient status ('none' if no gradient)
use math

a = zeros[3, 3]
b = eye[3]
c = a * b
d = det[b]           --- 1.0

--- constants
log[pi]               --- 3.141592653589793

--- properties

log[a.dtype]               --- float
log[a.device]              --- cpu

io

Import with use io. Provides structured file parsing beyond the built-in read[path] and write[path, content].

FunctionDescription
csv[path]Parse CSV — a file, a URL or an S3 object — into array of maps (error-value on failure)
lines[path]Read a file, a URL or an S3 object as array of lines (error-value on failure); a file is read a block at a time (sys.file_lines), so its whole text is not held beside its lines

Note: Basic file I/O (read[path], write[path, content]) are builtins available without any import. The io module adds structured parsing only.

use io

write['/tmp/data.csv', 'name,age\nalice,30\nbob,25\n']

rows = csv['/tmp/data.csv']
log[len[rows]]         --- 2
log[rows[0].name]      --- alice
log[rows[0].age]       --- 30

ls = lines['/tmp/data.txt']
log[len[ls]]           --- number of lines
log[ls[0]]             --- first line

--- error handling
bad = csv['/tmp/missing.csv']
log[bad.ok]            --- false
log[bad.reason]        --- error message

doc

Import with use doc. Documentation from Markdown, rendered by the kernel so the interpreter and compiled programs produce the same pages. The Crow Book is built with it (docs/book/site.co).

FunctionDescription
markdown[text]Markdown to HTML: headings get anchor ids, links to .md files point at .html pages, fenced crow blocks are highlighted with Crow's own token rules and tagged data-mode (run, error)
chapters[summary]An mdBook-style summary (# Part headings, - [Title](path) items nested by indentation) as a frame with columns title path depth part
menu[chapters, current]Navigation markup for the chapters frame: a brand link, parts as headings, collapsible groups for chapters with sub-chapters (open around the current page), the current page marked, links relative to the current page
pager[chapters, current]Previous and next chapter links for the current page, in summary order
plain[text]The words of a chapter with markup removed and whitespace collapsed — what a search index holds
document[title, body]A complete HTML page: the kernel stylesheet (tokens, elements, responsive rules) plus the documentation rules, then the body

markdown, menu and pager make markup, which a view places as markup, so a rendered chapter or a menu can be a child of stack[...] or row[...]; every other string is text and is escaped (§ Views: Text is text). A view's tree handed to document[...] is its markup in both modes.

use web, ui, doc

view chapter[title, body, nav]
    return row[stack[nav, width = '240px', hidden_below = 'tablet'],
               stack[body, fill = 'true', class = 'doc'], gap = 'xl']

toc = doc.chapters[read['docs/book/src/SUMMARY.md']]
for i in range[len[toc]]
    path = toc.path[i]
    body = doc.markdown[read['docs/book/src/`path`']]
    page = doc.document[toc.title[i], chapter[toc.title[i], body, doc.menu[toc, path]]]
    html_path = replace[path, '.md', '.html']
    write['docs/book/crow-site/`html_path`', page]

bits

Import with use bits. An int's bits and a character's code, for the algorithms that work on them — a set of small numbers held as one int's bits, a hash, a parser that reads character codes. An int is a whole number of any size, read as two's complement with as many bits as it needs (as Python reads one): bitand[n, -1] is n, a negative number's high bits are ones, and a shift right rounds down. Every function works identically in both modes (crow_runtime::bits). An int stays in 64 bits where the width rules show it fits (Numbers That Keep Every Digit); a shift left by a count nothing bounds grows, so a mask meant to stay a width is cut to it with bitand after the shift.

FunctionDescription
bitand[a, b]The bits both have
bitor[a, b]The bits either has
bitxor[a, b]The bits one has and the other has not; bitxor[a, -1] flips every bit (-a - 1)
bitcount[a]How many ones the bits of abs[a] hold
shift[a, n]a times 2^n; for a negative n, a divided by 2^-n and rounded down (shift[-7, -1] is -4). A number past 2^32 bits stops
wrap[x]A whole number's low 64 bits as the int of those bits: a growing int wrapped to 64 (wrap[2 ^ 64 + 5] is 5, wrap[18446744073709551615] is -1), where wrapmul and its kin wrap as they compute
wrapmul[a, b], wrapadd[a, b], wrapsub[a, b]The product, the sum and the difference of two ints wrapped to 64 bits: the low 64 bits of the exact answer, as the int of those bits — a hash's multiply per byte, a linear congruence, a checksum, an offset from a base, which ints that grow have no other way to say (a product of two ints past 2^32 is exact, and growing). Each takes ints of 64 bits, and answers one
code[ch]The code of a one-character text, its Unicode scalar value (code['A'] is 65); any other length stops: code takes one character, got "ab" (2 characters)
char[n]The character whose code is n (char[233] is 'é'); a number that is no character's code stops
use bits

fn queens[n, mask, row, cols, d1, d2]
    if row eq n
        return 1
    count = 0
    free = bitand[bitxor[bitor[bitor[cols, d1], d2], -1], mask]
    while free ne 0
        bit = bitand[free, -free]                --- the lowest free column
        free = bitxor[free, bit]
        count = count + queens[n, mask, row + 1, bitor[cols, bit], bitand[shift[bitor[d1, bit], 1], mask], shift[bitor[d2, bit], -1]]
    return count

log[queens[8, shift[1, 8] - 1, 0, 0, 0, 0]]     --- 92

Design Note: bits by name, in a module. Considered: the operators most languages write — &, |, ^, <<, >> — or the functions in the core. Decided (Vijay, 2026-10-11): named functions in a module a program imports. ^ is already the power, and symbols are shift-key syntax whose cost needs a reason a few algorithms do not give; the core is frozen by the budget rule, and code is a variable's name in many programs, so the names are taken only where a program asks for them. Before the module, a program had no way to read a character's code at all (sys has one, and a program may not import sys).

memory

Import with use memory — meant for library code: the standard library, and a package that needs it. A record library code holds for the process under a name, shared by its threads — what a module keeps between calls (Magpie's sequencer: the rows put since the last flush, each key's last position and newest row). A program's own state is its variables, and a page's its facts, so a program seldom has a reason to import it; keeping it to library code is a suggestion, not a rule, and any program or package may import it (Vijay, 2026-10-29). A name holds one kind of record, the first it was given: a read, a revision or a write of another kind stops, naming both, in both modes (memory: 'tally' holds Other, not Count — a name holds one kind of record). Names are a tree — forgetting a name forgets the names beneath it (the name and a slash) — and a name that is a path, as canonical writes it, is forgotten when rm removes the path. The compiled program holds each record as its own type, the interpreter its values, under the same names and rules (crow_runtime::memory).

FunctionDescription
remember[name, record]The record held for the process under the name, replacing what was
recall[name, default]The record the name holds, or the default where it holds none; typed by the default
revise[name, default, f, …]f run under the name's lock, handed the record held (the default where none is) and the rest of the arguments; the record f answers is held and answered. The record is handed over, not copied, so a revision costs what f does; where f's answer can fail it is copied first, and a failure leaves it as it was and is the answer. Two threads revising one name take turns, and f reaching its own name again (a recall, a revision) is refused, where it would wait on itself; it reaches another name only after its own in name order — inside a revision of 'b', 'c' is revised, recalled or remembered and 'a' is refused alike, so that two revisions on two threads never each hold one name and wait on the other's (Vijay: made public again, two names are taken in name order)
forget[name]Nothing held under the name nor beneath it: a later read answers its default, and the name may take another kind
canonical[path]The path made absolute with its links resolved (as given where it cannot be): the name a path's record is kept under

recall reads (the held kind: a derivation may, a view may not); the others change what is held (held.write: a handler, a function or a test). A loop that touches memory runs in order, and nothing leaves the program, so a rehearsal records none.

use memory

struct Count [n]

fn bumped[c, k]
    return Count[c.n + k]

fn bump[k]
    return revise['visits', Count[0], bumped, k].n

log[bump[1]]                          --- 1
log[bump[2]]                          --- 3
log[recall['visits', Count[0]].n]     --- 3

Design Note: process memory holds records, by name. Considered: top-level variables a module's functions write (rejected: a function sees no top-level variable — No Ambient Mutability — and state its threads share needs a lock the language would then need as well); a store of any value (rejected: the compiled program holds a value as its type, and a name holding an int at one call and a list at the next would be checked at every read of every kind — a record is one type per program, so a read of another kind is one comparison, alike in both modes, and the record names what is kept); a lock and an unlock (rejected: a stop between them holds the lock for good) — revise runs a function under the lock instead. Considered for revise: copying the record for the function (rejected: the sequencer's record holds the pending rows, and a copy per put makes every put cost what is pending) — the record is handed over, copied only where the function's answer can fail; and what a module keeps per key is a name per key beneath the module's name (<dir>/#last/<key>), so no revision copies a map of every key. Decided (2026-10-28) for Magpie's sequencer (Magpie's magpie-in-crow.md, 2.4). The flush the sequencer asks for is later's — coalesced, waited for at exit — and run inline by the interpreter, so under crow run -i a put is durable when it answers: a legal schedule, and a slower one. Decided as well (Vijay, 2026-10-28): a form a derivation could call to keep a computed record (kept[name, f, …], for Magpie's footers) was declined: a program keeps nothing in the process behind its own variables, and Magpie's reads keep nothing between calls and are made cheap instead (Magpie's magpie-in-crow.md, 4.2). Process memory had been the standard library's alone, as sys was; keeping both to library code is a suggestion, not a rule (Vijay, 2026-10-29): a package that needs them imports them.

chart

Import with use chart. Technical indicators for the chart element, computed by the runtime (crow_runtime::indicators, over the kernels in crow_runtime::ta) so the interpreter and compiled programs draw the same numbers. Every function takes series — frame columns or arrays of numbers — and returns a series of the same length, NaN where the value is not yet defined (a 14-period RSI has no value on row 3), so a chart draws a gap instead of a guess. Write a result into the frame (df.name = series) and name the column in lines = [...] or panes = [...].

FunctionDescription
sma[values, n]Simple moving average over the last n rows
ema[values, n]Exponential moving average, smoothing 2/(n+1), seeded by the first n rows' mean
rsi[values, n]Wilder's relative strength index over n deltas (0–100), from the second row on: until n deltas have come, of the mean gain and the mean loss of those there are (the line shows from the open, as a scanner reading the first minutes needs); from the nth, each new delta smoothed in by (n−1)/n, as Wilder's is. 100 where there are gains and no losses, 0 where there are losses and no gains, 50 where there are neither (a flat series); a row that is no number is NaN and passed over
rsi[ts, values, n, secs]RSI on clock-aligned buckets of secs seconds over finer rows — a 14-period RSI on 3-minute candles drawn over 1-second bars: completed buckets feed the smoothing once, and rows inside the forming bucket see a provisional value from the latest close, so history never changes and the newest value moves with the tape
macd[values, fast, slow, signal]A frame with columns line (fast EMA − slow EMA), signal (its EMA), hist (their difference)
vwap[price, vol]Cumulative volume-weighted average price from the first row
vwap[ts, price, vol, secs]VWAP over the trailing secs seconds (rows with ts >= t − secs)
rolling_sum[ts, values, secs]Sum over the trailing secs seconds

A trailing window — rolling_sum, vwap over seconds, rsi on buckets — reads its times in order, each at least the one before, and its seconds are 0 or more: a time that goes back stops the program, saying where (rolling_sum: the times go back at row 1 (1 after 3)), as a negative window does. | cvd[close, vol] | Cumulative volume delta: volume counted positive when the close rose, negative when it fell |

ts is a column of moments (seconds). A window is 1 row or more, a trailing window 0 seconds or more and a bucket more than 0, and the series an indicator reads row by row are one length; anything else stops the program with the reason (see When a Program Stops), as does a wrong number of arguments, naming the counts the function takes (rsi[] takes 2 or 4 arguments, got 3). A negative window had been a huge one compiled and one row interpreted, and a shorter series cut the others.

use chart, frame

bars = frame.new[]
bars.ts = [0.0, 60.0, 120.0, 180.0, 240.0, 300.0]
bars.c = [10.0, 11.0, 10.5, 11.5, 12.0, 11.0]
bars.vol = [100.0, 200.0, 150.0, 300.0, 250.0, 100.0]
bars.sma3 = sma[bars.c, 3]
bars.rsi3 = rsi[bars.c, 3]
bars.vwap = vwap[bars.c, bars.vol]
log[bars.sma3[5]]      --- 11.5
log[bars.rsi3[5]]      --- 50
log[bars.vwap[1]]      --- 10.666666666666666

In a view, the columns become panes with reference levels:

chart[bars, candles = ['o', 'hi', 'lo', 'c'], lines = ['vwap'], bars_y = 'vol',
      panes = [['rsi3'], ['cvd']], levels = map[rsi3 = [30, 70], cvd = [0]],
      points = fills, seek = jump, marker = clock, tz = 'America/New_York']

sys

Two operations over functions, for the standard modules (section 7 of docs/extending-crow.md): describe[f] answers a function's description as a parsed value — name, doc (the comment written above it), params (each name, takes — the word errors use for its kind: an int, a number, text, a bool, a list of ints, … — and default, or null), answers — and apply[f, args] calls the function with its arguments read by name from a parsed value or a map, each by the parameter's kind, and answers the value encoded as text: an argument missing or of another kind is an error value of kind call, the function's own failure its value. Each takes a function written, or one of a group (a list literal of functions: Specialization), and is lowered to what inference knows — a dispatch the compiler writes over every function the value can be — so both modes agree by construction. A parameter nothing types is refused with the fix (weather's parameter 'city' is typed by nothing — read it in the body, or call weather[…] once with a value of its kind); one handed a parsed value, or typed by nothing, takes what the body reads it as, or its default's kind; one of a kind no name can hand (a frame, a function, a handle) is refused where it is applied. A program outside the standard library does not call them (sys is the library's own).

Design Note: functions as values for native rows, described and called by name. Considered: a tool written by hand beside the function (rejected in llm's note); a list of function values of one signature only, each a function pointer compiled (rejected: a value's name cannot be recovered compiled, so describe could not say what it was handed, and tools of several signatures — the common case — could not share a list); a new expression that chooses by a name at run time (rejected: a dispatch written once by the compiler over the members, which it knows, is the same thing without a construct). Decided (2026-10-19): a native row declares the function it takes with its signature (Kind::FnOf: any's predicate over the walked items, later's deferred work handed the rest, stream's feed handed put, route's handler the request, transact's body the connection, a chart's seek an event), and inference, the checks, the width pass and the classification read the one declaration; a list literal of functions is a group, held by name; describe and apply are sys's, lowered to a dispatch the compiler writes.

Import with use sys — meant for library code: the layer the standard library's own Crow-written modules stand on, and a package that needs a kernel's speed — bytes, files, and a connection to a host read and written as bytes. A program serves with serve[…], reads with request[…], read[…] and socket[…], and connects with connect[…], which are built on this layer (design rule 20); keeping sys to library code is a suggestion, not a rule, and any program or package may import it (Vijay, 2026-10-29).

FunctionDescription
bytes[text]The text's UTF-8 as bytes. A bytes value shows as <n bytes>, has a len, and compares with eq and ne to another; type[b] is bytes
text[bytes]The text the bytes spell in UTF-8; an error value of kind 'parse' where they spell none
slice[bytes, from, to]The bytes from from to to (excluded), counted from the end when negative; a span outside stops
find[bytes, needle]Where the needle first occurs, or -1
concat[a, b]The two in order
byte[bytes, i]The byte at i as an int, counted from the end when negative; outside stops
bytes[n]n zero bytes: a buffer a kernel written in Crow writes into by position
set[bytes, i, v]The byte at i replaced by v (0 to 255), counted from the end when negative; outside stops. b = set[b, i, v] writes where the bytes lie, as a list's set does
push[bytes, v]The byte appended (0 to 255); b = push[b, v] grows the bytes where they lie, and a = concat[a, b] appends b where a lies
open[host, port, tls, seconds]A connection to the host's port — each address the host's name resolves to tried in turn (localhost is ::1 and 127.0.0.1, and a server may listen on one) — behind TLS when tls is true (the system's roots); each read and write gives up after seconds (none when 0). An error value of kind 'sys' when none can be made: a host that does not resolve, a port nobody listens on, a certificate that does not fit
listen[host, port]A socket listening on the host's port, as a stream that is accepted on (port 0 for one the system picks; address says which); an error value of kind 'sys' where the port cannot be bound. It is neither read nor written: send and recv on it are refused, naming it a listener
accept[listener, seconds]The next connection that arrives, as a stream of its own named by the peer's address, waiting at most the seconds (as long as it takes when 0); an error value of kind 'sys' where none arrives in time, the stream is no listener, or it is closed. The wait ends early with a stop of the work it is in, as a read's does
address[stream]The address a stream is named by: a listener's own (127.0.0.1:54321), an accepted connection's peer, an opened connection's host and port
start[command]The process the command names, started under this program — sh -c runs the command, as shell runs one — with its standard input and output piped and its standard error the program's own, as a socket: stream_of[p] is written (the process's input) and read (its output, recv waiting at most its seconds, nothing once the output closed), and the socket keeps what a read took past a message for the next (leftover, unread). One process per command on each thread of the program, kept while the thread lives: a start of a command whose process runs on this thread answers the same socket, one whose process ended, or was closed, starts it again — per thread, since a served program's handlers run on threads of their own, and two exchanges with one process would take each other's lines. close[stream_of[p]] ends it as the MCP stdio transport says a client ends its server: the input closed, its exit waited for (1.5 s), then TERM to its process group (sh and what it runs), then KILL. An error value of kind 'sys' where the command is empty or the shell cannot start; a command the shell does not find starts, says so on standard error and ends — a read then answers nothing, a write the process ended. Made as open makes a connection, no effect of its own: its send and recv are
send[stream, bytes]The bytes written, all of them; the count. An error value of kind 'sys' on a stream that is closed or broke
recv[stream, most, seconds]Up to most bytes as they arrive — none when the other side closed — waiting at most seconds (the connection's own wait when 0). The wait ends with a stop — of the work it is in (a derivation nobody waits for any more) or of the program — with the reason cancelled: nobody waits for this query any more, which a driver answers by telling its server. An error value of kind 'sys': no data from host:port in 2 s, the stream is closed
close[stream]The connection let go; every call after is refused
concat[chunks]A list of bytes joined in order, as one (what a connection read, piece by piece), as concat[[a, b, c]] joins pieces written down
file[path]A file's bytes, as they are; an error value of kind 'sys' where it cannot be read
file_lines[path]A file's lines, read a block at a time — split at each line break, a \r before one dropped, a last line's trailing \r too, no empty line after the last break — without the whole text held beside them; an error value of kind 'sys' where it cannot be read or a line is not UTF-8
store[path, bytes]The bytes written to a file, as they are; an error value of kind 'sys' where it cannot be written
random[n]n bytes from the operating system's randomness
hex[bytes]The bytes as lowercase hex, two digits each
text[bytes, fill]The text the bytes spell, each run that spells none replaced by fill ('�', as a browser shows one) — never an error
hash[bytes, algo]The digest as lowercase hex: 'sha256', 'sha512', 'crc32' (eight digits, the checksum gzip and Kafka carry), 'sha1' or 'md5'
digest[bytes, algo]The digest as bytes: 'crc32' (four bytes), 'sha1' (a socket's accept key), 'sha256', 'sha512' or 'md5'
hmac[key, message, algo]The keyed digest as bytes: 'sha256' (an S3 signature's chain) or 'sha512'
reply[status, body, headers]A request's answer from its parts: the record request answers, made by the client written in Crow
replaying[], recording[], rehearsing[]Whether a replay answers the requests (crow test --fixtures); whether a recording keeps what they answer (crow check --record-fixtures); whether a rehearsal stands in for them (crow check)
replayed[method, url, body]The request's answer from its fixture — the replay's project's, else the recording's, else the rehearsal's — or the miss: an error value of kind 'http', noted for the run's report in a replay
recorded[method, url, body, status, text, headers]In a recording, what the request was answered, kept as its fixture under .crow/fixtures; nothing otherwise. An error value of kind 'sys' where it cannot be written
answer[status, phrase, headers, body]A server's answer from its parts, its body as bytes: the record exchange and fetch answer — status, phrase, headers (names in lower case) and body
csv_rows[text]CSV text as rows, each a map from its column's name to its text; an error value of kind 'io' where it does not parse
frame_of_csv[text], frame_of_csv_bytes[bytes], frame_of_json[text], frame_of_parquet[bytes], frame_of_rowbinary[bytes], frame_of_postgres[bytes]A table as a frame, from CSV text (or a CSV file's bytes read where they are, which load uses: no copy of the text), JSON records, Parquet bytes, ClickHouse's RowBinaryWithNamesAndTypes, or the messages Postgres answers a query with (its row description, rows, completion and ready-for-query). Each column is typed as the server names it: integers an int column (a ClickHouse UInt64 past an int cell's 64 bits refuses the whole, naming its column — CAST it to String to keep its digits; it had wrapped round to a negative number), floats, decimals and numerics floats (a decimal the float nearest its value, every digit of it read — a 256-bit decimal's high bits had been dropped), booleans bools, dates and timestamps a datetime column (a fraction of a second kept to the millisecond, a zone's offset kept), a NULL a missing cell, ClickHouse's enums by name and its UUIDs, IPs, 128- and 256-bit integers (their exact digits and sign) and arrays as text, anything else text; a ClickHouse type no cell holds, or a Postgres cell sent in the binary format, refuses the whole; a Postgres error is the reason in the server's words, and bytes that end before the ready-for-query "end early" (the driver reads more); an error value of kind 'frame'
frame_csv[frame], frame_json[frame], frame_parquet[frame]A frame as CSV text, as JSON records, or as Parquet bytes
tensor_of_frame[frame]A frame's cells as a matrix, a row each; an error value of kind 'load' where a cell is no number
base64[bytes]The bytes as base64, the standard alphabet padded with = (HTTP's Basic authorization, SMTP's AUTH PLAIN)
base64url[bytes]The bytes as base64 in the URL-safe alphabet, unpadded, as a token's parts are written
unbase64[text]Base64's bytes, either alphabet, padded or not; an error value of kind 'parse' where the text is no base64
same[a, b]Whether two byte strings are equal, decided in time that does not depend on where they differ (a signature checked against the one computed)
pack[n, width, little = false]An int as width bytes, big-endian, or little-endian with little — 1, 2, 4 or 8, the low bytes of its two's complement; another width stops
unpack[bytes, at, width, little = false, signed = true]The int the width bytes at at spell (little-endian with little; unsigned with signed false, where 8 bytes past 2^63 are refused), big-endian two's complement — 1, 2, 4 or 8 — the counterpart of pack; a span outside the bytes, or another width, stops
ieee[x, width = 8, little = false]A number as the 8 bytes of its IEEE 754 double, big-endian — or the 4 of its single precision with width 4, little-endian with little
terms[text]The words of a text — each run of letters and digits, Unicode's, lowercased — in order, as a search index and a query split them
float_bytes[floats, width, little], float_values[bytes, at, n, width, little]A list of floats as their bits one after another, 8 bytes each or 4 of single precision, little-endian where little is true, and n floats read back from the bytes at at: a chunk's worth at once, where ieee writes and reads one
ieee[bytes, at, width, little]The float the width bytes at at spell — 8 of a double, 4 of single precision — little-endian where little is true; outside stops. (Its width and byte order are positions, since the writer's options may be given by position: ieee[x, 4])
binary[bytes, media]A response of the bytes, as the media type names them (msgpack's)
mask[bytes, key]The bytes XORed with the 4-byte key, cycling: a client's frame masked, a masked frame read

secure[stream, host]A plain connection put behind TLS from here on (SMTP's STARTTLS), the certificate checked against the host's name; the same stream for every value that holds it, or an error value of kind 'sys'
civil[t]A moment's civil date and time, UTC, as a list: the year, month and day, the hour, minute and second, and the day of the week (0 is Sunday); written in Crow
betainc[x, a, b]The regularized incomplete beta function I_x(a, b): the t and F distributions are written over it

note[stream, key, text], noted[stream, key]A note kept on a connection under a key, read back (empty when there is none; empty text lets the key go): a query's id and a backend's process and key — what a cancel needs later — how long the server keeps the connection, the statements prepared on it
machine[], process[], alive[pid]This machine's name (letters, digits, dots and dashes, or local), this process's number, and whether a process of this machine is running (a number that is none counts as running)

copy_of_frame[frame]The frame's rows in Postgres's COPY text format: a tab between cells, a line per row, \N for a missing cell, a backslash, a tab, a line break and a return escaped; a whole float without its decimals, a truth as t or f, a moment as it is written
rowbinary_of_frame[frame, types]A frame as ClickHouse's RowBinary, each column encoded as the table's type for it names (one per frame column, in the frame's order): what INSERT … FORMAT RowBinary takes after the statement; a missing cell NULL where the type holds one, else the type's own nothing; a decimal as its digits at the type's scale, a half away from zero (a float as the shortest text that reads back as it); a cell a type cannot hold — a number past its range, a fraction where a whole number is taken — is an error value of kind 'frame' naming the column (a number had been cut to the type's width: 300 written as a UInt8 had been 44)
shaped[t, dims]The tensor's values in the shape the list of sizes names: reshape given its sizes as a list, as a Crow function's rest arrives

put[path, bytes]The bytes written to an S3 object, signed; a URL is refused — it is read, not written

ints_of[column, from, to]An int column's values of rows from to to (excluded), or a moment column's milliseconds; a missing cell reads as 0 in every one of these (0.0, false, empty text, zeros for a vector), the same in both modes, and present_of says which
floats_of[column, from, to], bools_of[column, from, to]A float column's values (a vector column's rows laid flat), a bool column's
millis_of[column, from, to], zones_of[column, from, to]A moment column's milliseconds and its offsets in minutes
vectors_of[column, from, to], vector_width[column]A vector column's rows laid flat, and how many numbers each holds
text_bytes_of[column, from, to], text_ends_of[column, from, to]A text column's cells' UTF-8 one after another (a missing cell empty), and where each cell ends in it
present_of[column, from, to], missing_in[column, from, to]Which cells are present as a bitmap's bytes (bit i of byte i / 8 for cell from + i; no bytes where every cell is), and how many are missing
ints_column[ints, present], floats_column[floats, present], bools_column[bits, n, present], moments_column[millis, zones, present], vectors_column[width, floats, present], text_column[bytes, ends, present], text_dict_column[bytes, ends, codes, present]A column from its parts, present the bitmap present_of answers or no bytes for every cell; written onto a frame with df.name = column. Refused where the parts disagree: more offsets than milliseconds, numbers that fill no row, bytes that spell no text, an end outside them

A stream is one connection however many values hold it, as a socket is; type[s] is stream. A socket is a stream and what it read past the last message — a WebSocket's connection after its handshake, or a process started under the program (start), whose output comes line by line; type[p] is socket, and it shows as <socket /bin/cat>. Every function works identically in both modes (crow_runtime::sys). The HTTP protocol is written on this layer, in src/modules/sys.co, as functions of sys itself: a URL taken apart, a connection opened plain or behind TLS, the request line, headers and body sent, the answer's head read to its blank line and its body by its length, by its chunks, or until the server closes (exchange), redirects followed and a reader's options applied (fetch), an S3 object signed as AWS's Signature Version 4 says and read or written by path at an endpoint (fetch, put). On the protocol stand the HTTP client — request (Requests), in src/modules/core.co — and every reader of a URL or an S3 object: read and write (core.co), use io's csv and lines (io.co), use frame's load and save (frame.co) and use math's load (math.co), each a few lines over the layer's parsers (csv_rows, frame_of_csv, tensor_of_frame). A program never sees the layer: it sees request and read, effects the session layer holds, whose rows in the builtin table are the contracts the Crow bodies keep until effects are declared on the layer's own rows (math.nn, Design Note "a module written in Crow declares its functions by writing them"; docs/extending-crow.md, phase 3).

use sys

b = bytes['héllo']
log[len[b]]                      --- 6 (two bytes for é)
log[text[slice[b, 3, 6]]]        --- llo
log[find[b, bytes['llo']]]       --- 3
bad = text[slice[b, 1, 2]]       --- half of é: not text
log[bad.kind]                    --- parse
s = open['127.0.0.1', 1, false, 0.2]
log[s.kind]                      --- sys: nobody listens on port 1

Design Note: the standard library's layer. Considered: writing the standard library's modules in Rust only, as they were (rejected: a module both backends share is a bug both share, which no parity or fuzz run can see; the week of 2026-10-03 found eleven bugs where the two backends differ and none in the kernels they share, because nothing can look there); giving every program bytes, sockets and threads (rejected: a program would gain the bug classes the language exists to exclude — data races, half-read buffers, protocol state spread over user code — and a second way to serve and connect beside serve and connect). Decided (Vijay, 2026-10-03): one sys module, the smallest native layer the rest of the library can be written on, importable by the compiler's own modules alone; a program sees one way, and every module written on sys is compiled and run twice, fuzzed, and takes laws. Then (Vijay, 2026-10-29): importable by any program or package — keeping it to the standard library is a suggestion, not a rule — so a package that needs bytes and files at a kernel's speed is written on it outside the standard library, and the reference still teaches serve and connect as a program's one way to serve and connect.

frame

Import with use frame. Column-oriented DataFrames for tabular data. See docs/frame-module.md for design details.

Column references: strings for names, expressions for computation. Naming a column is a string; computing on a column is a normal Crow expression over df.col. Every line is plain Crow — there is no special evaluation inside frame calls.

FunctionDescription
new[], new[col = values, ...]The empty frame (frame.new[], since new reads best qualified), or a frame written down: each named argument a column, in order — frame.new[sym = ['AAPL', 'MSFT'], qty = [100, 50.5]] — its values a list, a tensor or one value repeated, as a column written takes them (each named argument is a column write, df.sym = …, typed column by column; until 2026-10-09 only with could write a frame down); a matrix, or a list of vectors, makes a column of vectors (below)
load[path]Load a table → frame, in the format its extension names — CSV, JSON records (columns in name order), Parquet — from a file, a URL or an S3 object (error-value on failure; Files, URLs and S3). A CSV column of whole numbers is ints, of numbers floats, of true/false (or True/FALSE, as pandas writes them) bools, of ISO 8601 moments a moment column, anything else text; an empty cell is missing; a cell in quotes may hold a comma or a quote written twice (RFC 4180), though not a line break
save[frame, path]Write frame → a file or an S3 object, as CSV, JSON records or Parquet by its extension (CSV otherwise); a text cell holding a comma, a quote or a line break is written in quotes, as load reads it; a failure is an error value of kind 'frame', as load's is (compiled, it had stopped the program)
select[frame, cols]Pick columns: select[df, ['name', 'age']]
an index by a mask, df[mask]The rows where the mask is true, as a list's items are: df[df.age gt 50]; a column of bools is its own mask: df[df.active]; a column's cells the same way: df.name[df.age gt 50]
sort[frame, col], sort[frame, [cols]]Sort ascending: sort[df, 'age']. Descending: sort[df, 'age', desc = true]. By several keys, sort[df, ['symbol', 'qty']]: by the first, ties by the next; desc = true turns every key, desc = [false, true] one each. Stable — rows whose cells are equal keep their order — with a missing cell, or a float that is not a number, last either way
group[frame, col, aggs...]Group + aggregate: group[df, 'dept', ['salary', 'mean'], ['age', 'max']]; by several keys, group[df, ['dept', 'city'], ['salary', 'mean']] — one row per combination of the keys' cells, in the order first seen, each key column as it was (a row with a missing key cell is in no group). Each aggregate is named col_how and is in the kind it has: count the present cells, an int; sum an int over ints or bools and a float over floats, 0 where a group has no present cell; mean a float; min and max a cell of the column's own kind (text by character code, moments by their instant), missing where a group has no present cell. A missing cell is skipped, as SQL's aggregates skip NULL; a sum or mean over text stops the program
pivot[frame, rows, cols, values]The long frame made wide: a row per cell of rows, a column per cell of cols — named by the cell's text, in ascending order — holding the values cell of the row that has the pair, and a missing cell where no row does. A pair two rows share stops the program: aggregate with group first
window[frame, col, how, n = 0, by = [], span = 0, on = '']A column computed over each row's partition — the rows sharing the by columns' cells (one name or a list; the whole frame without) — in the frame's order: an aggregate (every word group takes) over the trailing n rows, over every row so far when n is 0, or, with span = seconds and on = column, over the rows whose on cell lies within the span before the row's own (a column of moments, or of numbers in the span's units; the partition in ascending order of it, or the program stops: sort first) — (t - span, t], as pandas and polars close a time window; a sum, min or max over ints is an int, every digit (a sum past 64 bits is refused), a mean, median, std, var or percentile a float, a count or distinct an int, first and last the column's own cells; 'lag' and 'lead' the cell n rows back or ahead, missing where there is none; 'rank' the cell's rank in its partition, ascending, equal cells sharing a rank and the next rank skipping (none of the three takes a span). Write it into the frame: df.avg3 = window[df, 'close', 'mean', n = 3, by = 'symbol'], df.vwap_5m = window[df, 'px', 'mean', span = 300, on = 'at', by = 'symbol']
join[f1, f2, key], join[f1, f2, [keys]]Join on a key column, or on several: join[df1, df2, 'id'], join[df1, df2, ['symbol', 'day']]. Its kind: join[df1, df2, 'id', 'left'] — 'inner' (the default), 'left', 'right', 'outer'; 'semi' the left's rows that have a match and 'anti' those that have none, the left's columns alone; 'asof' each left row with the right row whose last key is the latest at or before the left's, the other keys matching exactly — a column of moments, or of numbers — missing where there is none (a left join by time; the right's clock is the frame's own order, sorted as needed)
stack[f1, f2], stack[frames]Stack frames vertically: two, or a list of them in order (the parts a file is read in, a loop's frames) — the empty frame of an empty list; columns that differ in name or kind stop the program
head[frame, n] / tail[frame, n]First/last n rows (returns a frame)
fill[frame, col, value], fill[frame, col, how = 'forward' | 'backward']The column's missing cells given value, of the column's kind; or each given the nearest present cell before it ('forward') or after it ('backward'), one with none staying missing
unique[frame, col], unique[frame, [cols]]The column's distinct present cells, first seen first, in the column's own kind (an int column's are ints): unique[df, 'symbol']. With a list of columns, a frame: the first row of each distinct combination of their cells, every column as it was, a missing cell a value of its own
rename[frame, old, new]Rename a column: rename[df, 'salary', 'pay']
unpivot[frame, keys, cols]The wide frame made long, the reverse of pivot: a row per key row and column, the key columns (one name or a list, [] for none) repeated, variable the column's name and value its cell — the columns' cells one kind, ints beside floats floats
complete[frame], complete[frame, cols]The rows whose every cell is present, or every cell of the named columns (one name or a list): the rows a mask of .ok over all of them keeps
sample[frame, n]n of the rows, drawn without replacement by the seeded generator (see Random Numbers), kept in the frame's order; n beyond the rows stops the program
where[mask, a, b]A column that takes a's cell where the mask is true and b's elsewhere — each a column, a list or one value for every row, both of one kind (ints beside floats floats); a missing cell stays missing; a column of bools is its own mask. (math.where is the same word over tensors.)
bucket[column, seconds]Each moment's bucket start (see Moments, bucket[t, seconds]), a column of moments: after df.hour = bucket[df.at, 3600], group[df, ['symbol', 'hour'], ['px', 'first'], ['px', 'max'], ['px', 'min'], ['px', 'last'], ['qty', 'sum']] is the hourly bars
