If no routes are registered, serve[] shows a default welcome page so the user always sees something; once a route or a page is, an address none of them answers is a 404, no page at /cards (the welcome page had answered it).

If the port is already in use, the server prints a friendly error: error: port 3000 is already in use.

FunctionDescription
serve[port]Start the server on the given port (implicit app)
page[path, view]Add a LiveView page rendered by view
route[path, handler]Add a route; handler is a named fn handler[req] that receives the request and returns a response (implicit app; route[path, method, handler] for other methods; limit = 60 answers each client at most 60 times a minute)

A route's handler is handed the request, and nothing else: it is a function, so no module variable is visible inside it (Function Scope), and no parameter can carry one in, since the route calls it with the request alone. What it needs it opens itself — conn = connect[env['DATABASE_URL']] — and every connect to one URL in a program is one handle, so the handlers that connect share one pool (### db). The request's req.method, req.path and req.body are text, and req.headers (their names in lower case), req.query and req.params are maps of text, so /hello/:name reads its name as req.params.name and /find?q=crow its query as req.query.q — decoded, so ?q=hello%20world is hello world (a query had reached the program still encoded). A form's fields are req.form, a map of text, from an urlencoded body or a multipart one, and a multipart body's files are req.files, a list of maps of text as a page's picked file is — field (the form's name for it), name, type, size and path, where the server keeps a copy under .crow/uploads/ while the handler runs and removes it once the handler has answered (cp[f.path, …] keeps one). A body bigger than the app takes — 10 MB, or serve[…, uploads = N] megabytes, the limit a page's uploads keep — is answered 413 Payload Too Large before the handler sees it (a body had been read whatever its size), and a multipart body that does not parse 400 Bad Request.

serve[port, cors = ['https://app.example.com'], files = 'public'] sets the server's options. cors names the sites whose pages may call the app — '*' any — and answers their preflights; a page on any other site is told nothing, so its browser refuses the answer. files is a directory whose files answer the addresses no route or page does — /logo.png is public/logo.png, / its index.html — and a file it does not hold is the app's 404, as is one whose name, or a directory's on its path, starts with a dot: .env, .git/, .crow/, however the address spells the dot (files = '.' had handed out .crow/keys/, whose key seals the sign-in cookies); .well-known, where a certificate authority looks, is served. A route's limit = n answers each client at most n times a minute, a client being its address (a proxy's on this machine, the first address its X-Forwarded-For names); a request over it is 429 Too Many Requests, its Retry-After the seconds to wait. All three are the server's, the same in both modes. A route that names a method answers only it, and another method at its path is 405 Method Not Allowed. Both modes serve routes and make every response below through the same functions, so they answer alike, to the byte of a msgpack or an arrow body. A handler whose value fails — int[req.query.n] of a query that is no number, a frame that did not load — answers 500 and an id, the request could not be answered — the server's log has why, as 3f9a1c07, and the server's log names the handler and the reason beside it, crow: flow stopped [3f9a1c07]: cannot convert 'x' to int; so does one that stops (boom stopped [91c0d2e4]: line 5: index 9 is out of range for an array of 2 items). The client is told no more, since a reason names what the program reaches — a database's address, a file's path (it had been told the reason; Vijay, 2026-10-01) — in both modes, and the server goes on. A response HTTP cannot carry — a status outside 100 to 999, a header whose value holds a line break or another control character, as one a handler built from what a request sent may — answers 500 and an id as a failed handler does, the log saying why beside it (a response could not be sent [5e07aa1b]: the header 'x-name' holds a line break or another control character); text beyond ASCII in a header's value is sent as its bytes. (It had answered internal error, saying nothing, and refused text beyond ASCII the same way.) A route's handler that cannot wait on anything — no query, no read, no log, no loop, nothing it calls that does one — is answered on the server's own thread as its request is read (a hop to another thread and back had cost more than answering text['Hello, World!']); any other runs off the server's threads, as a page's does, so a slow one delays only its own request, on at most four threads a core (64 at least) at once: a request past them waits its turn, as one past a database's pool waits for a connection. Both modes decide which handlers cannot wait by the same rule. A route and its responses are web's: a program that calls one without use web is refused before it runs. (The interpreter had served no route at all, and then none of msgpack, arrow and stream; a compiled route that read a param or named a method had not built, nor one whose handler's value can fail, and the compiled stream had not built either; a compiled handler that stopped had dropped the connection, saying nothing.)

Responses

FunctionDescription
text[s]Plain text response
html[s]HTML response
json[map]JSON response from a map; from a record, an object of its fields; from a list, an array — json[rows] of a list of maps or of records answers an array of objects. Each value is written as encode writes it: a value that failed is null, an int past 64 bits the text of its digits
json[frame]JSON response from a frame: its records, a list of objects (a missing cell null, a moment its ISO text) — what a page's fetch of the route reads back as a frame. A list of frames answers an array of each one's records
json[parsed]JSON response of a parsed value, as it is: json[json[req.body]] echoes a body, json[json[text]] serves JSON the program holds as text (json[text] alone parses)
respond[status, body]Response with custom status code
redirect[url]HTTP redirect response
msgpack[value]MessagePack binary response: the value as JSON holds it, packed (application/msgpack) — written in Crow (src/modules/web.co), each piece in its smallest form
arrow[frame]The frame as an Arrow IPC stream (application/vnd.apache.arrow.stream), which pandas, polars and every Arrow reader take
bytes[data]Raw binary response (application/octet-stream)
stream[feed]A text response sent as feed puts it, chunk by chunk; stream[feed, a, …] hands feed the rest

A response's body is text, its status HTTP's. text, html, redirect, bytes and respond's body take text — a value of another kind is written into text first, text['`n`'], and refused before the program runs; a parsed value is read as text, as any builtin reads one. respond's status is an int from 100 to 999: one written in the program is refused (respond[] takes an HTTP status, from 100 to 999, got 1000), and one computed as it runs stops the handler in the same words — never moved to a status near it. arrow takes a frame. A route's handler returns a response: one that returns another value — json[req.body] parses the text — is refused before the program runs. A handler that stops, or whose response cannot be sent, answers 500 with the failure's id, its reason in the server's log, in both modes.

A stream is sent as its feed puts it. stream[feed, a, …] answers at once, and when the response is sent it calls feed[put, a, …] on a thread of its own. Each put[text] sends that chunk to the reader as it is put, and answers whether the reader still listens: false once they have gone, so a long feed can stop early. The arguments after the feed are the values they have when the handler answers, which is how a feed reads the request. A feed puts text — a value is written into text first, put['`row`\n'] — and a put handed anything else is refused before the program runs; the chunks go out in the order they are put, a loop's included. A feed that fails or stops ends the body where it is, and the log says why. The body is text/plain, sent chunked.

use web

fn numbered[put, n]
    for i in range[n]
        if not put['line `i`\n']
            return

fn flow[req]
    return stream[numbered, int[req.query.n]]

route['/flow', flow]
serve[3000]

Design Note: a stream's feed. Considered: a feed that returns a list the runtime sends (it holds the whole body, which a stream exists not to); a channel value a handler writes to (a new kind of value for one builtin); send[out, chunk] on a handle (WebSocket's send would name two things); and chunks of any value, sent as JSON lines. Decided: the feed is a named function, as Crow has no lambdas, handed put, a function value the program already knows how to call; stream[feed, a, …] hands it what it needs from the request, as later[f, a, …] does. Text chunks keep one way to write a value (interpolation), and put answering whether the reader listens lets a feed stop with the return it already has.

A response is what a route returns. A page shows elements and text, so a view that places one — row[text['3 items']] — is refused in both modes: place the text itself, or p[…] or span[…].

Auth (use web.auth)

FunctionDescription
sign[claims, secret]A token — a JSON Web Token signed with HS256 — carrying the claims (a map, or a record), issued now (iat) and expiring in an hour (exp) unless the claims say when
verify[token, secret]The claims a token carries, as a parsed value (str[claims.user]), when it was signed with this secret and has not expired

A token is how an API, which has no page session, says who calls it: sign gives one to a client that signed in, and each request carries it back for verify to read. A refusal is an error value of kind 'auth', its reason saying why: the token has expired, the token's signature does not match, not a token. A secret shorter than 32 characters signs nothing (a token's secret is 32 characters or more), as the sign-in cookie's CROW_SECRET keeps the same floor; keep it out of the source, in the environment (env['TOKEN_SECRET']). A page keeps sign_in, which needs no token. Both modes sign and verify through the same functions. (Neither had built them: the compiled program named a type the web runtime did not have, and the interpreter had no such builtins.) They are written in Crow (src/modules/auth.co) on the sys layer: the header and the claims as base64url, the signature an HMAC-SHA256 of the two, compared in constant time; the claims reach sign as the JSON text they encode to (Design Note "a builtin written in Crow keeps its row"). Their tests are the module's own (crow test src/modules/auth.co), one of them a token another library signed, read with its secret.

WebSocket (use web.ws)

FunctionDescription
socket[url]A connection to a WebSocket server, ws:// or wss://
send[s, text]Send a text message; answers empty text
recv[s] / recv[s, secs]The next message, as text — waiting for it, or at most secs seconds
close[s]Close the connection, for every value that holds it

A socket is a live feed a program reads — a market's trades, a service's events — in a script, a job, or a handler's background work. Each failure is the call's error value, of kind 'socket': a server that does not answer is socket's (cannot open a socket to …), a wait that passes its seconds is recv's (no message in 5 s) — one that ends while a message is arriving leaves what came of it for the next recv, which reads the message whole (the message's head had been lost, and the next read took its payload for a frame) — and a server that closed answers closed, after which every call says the socket is closed. A binary message reads as text, and a ping is answered and passed over. A socket is one connection however many values hold it — a function handed it, a loop — and close closes it for all of them. Both modes talk through the same client, which is written in Crow (src/modules/ws.co) on the sys layer: the handshake is the protocol's (sys.upgrade), and the framing — a message sent masked, the next read whole across its fragments, a ping answered, a close answered and the connection let go — a formula over send, recv, pack and mask. socket and close are its functions, their declaration their own (section 13's opening); send and recv are effects and keep their rows (docs/extending-crow.md, phase 3); the contract above holds in both modes, and its tests are the module's own (crow test src/modules/ws.co, the RFC's masked example among them), and tests/socket.rs runs it against a server in both modes.

use web, web.ws

feed = socket['wss://feed.example.com/stocks']
send[feed, '{"action": "subscribe", "params": "T.AAPL"}']
while true
    m = recv[feed, 30]
    if not m.ok
        log['the feed stopped: `m.reason`']
        break
    log[m]

Design Note: a socket's name. Considered: connect[url], the client's first name, which the database's connect already is — one name two ways, told apart by the URL's scheme; and dial, open and ws, which name a mechanism, a verb many things do, or the protocol's initials. Decided: socket, the thing a program holds, as smtp names a mailer and connect a database; send, recv and close act on it.

LiveView / Pages

FunctionDescription
page[path, view]Add a LiveView page (server-rendered with live updates via WebSocket); :name in path is a fact in its address (Core, above)
download[value, name]Hand the reader's browser a file when the handler commits: a frame as CSV, a string as text, a picked file's bytes
title[text]The page's title, which the browser's tab and history show: a view places it anywhere in the page, reading the page's state like any element (title['Card card']); a render that changes it changes the tab
meta[name = 'description', content = text]A tag in the page's head: its attributes pass through as HTML (property = 'og:title' for a link preview)

A page's head is what its render places. title and meta take no room on the page: a served page carries them in its head from its first render, and a later render that changes them changes the head — the last title the render places is the page's (a layout's placed before the page's own gives way to it), and a page that places none is titled Crow App. In a test they are elements of the render, where elements[a, 'title'] finds them; the layout engine gives them no box.

Design Note: a page's address and its head. Considered: a path's names as the view's parameters (view card[id]), as Express and Rails hand a route its parameters; and a page's title as an argument of page (page['/card/:card', card, title = 'Card']). Decided: a path's name is a fact. A page's state is its facts and the address already carried facts (url = […]), so one rule holds the path and the query string, a handler that changes the fact moves the address, and nothing new reaches a view. A title is an element the render places, so it reads the page's state (a card's own name) and changes with it, where an argument of page would be text fixed when the page is registered. A route keeps req.params: a route's handler is handed its request, and a page is handed none.

A page and its session. The page request renders the reader's session once, and the page's socket adopts that session — its facts, its derivations and the render already on screen — so a page loads with one render. A session outlives its connection. When the socket closes — a laptop sleeps, the network drops, a rebuild restarts nothing but the page — the server keeps the session for ten minutes, and the page, which holds the session's token for its tab, resumes it on reconnect: the same facts, a replay still where it was, sent its render at once. A waiting session keeps its facts and its derivations' last values, so it comes back without a flash of loading; at most a thousand sessions wait at once. The token is a credential and is treated as one: it travels only over the socket, it is good once, and every resume replaces it, so a copied token resumes nothing after its tab has. Reloading the tab resumes it too, and so does coming back to it with the browser's back button; a second tab is a session of its own. Following a link opens the session of the address it names, even to the page the tab is on: /?day=2 from /?day=1 starts from day = 2 (the tab's session had been resumed, and the address followed it back to day = 1). A socket that brings no live token — a session past its ten minutes, a server that restarted — starts a fresh session from the facts its address carries and is sent its render at once: the page never keeps showing a session the server no longer holds. serve[…, keep = true] keeps sessions across a restart as well: each session's facts are written to .crow/sessions/ as they change, and a tab that comes back to a restarted program finds its session there for the same ten minutes (a kept session had been removed before it was read, and the tab started afresh); a fact whose name or type changed since starts from its declared value, which the log says. The directory ignores itself in version control. While the socket is down the page dims, says reconnecting… and takes no input, so a click is never silently lost. Events that arrive while a render runs are applied together and answered by one render: a slider dragged across its range is one render, not one per step. A render sends what changed, not the page: the server reads the render as the browser builds it — a chart's marks and a table's rows included — compares it with the render the page holds, matching elements by id and then by position as the page does, and sends the changes (a text, an attribute, an element added, gone or replaced) when they are smaller than the page, the whole page when they are not. A moving play-head on a chart is a few attributes, not the drawing again, and a row added to a list whose items carry ids is one row. Each change names the element it expects to find; a page that meets one it cannot place — its shape changed under it — asks for the whole page back, and a field being typed in keeps the reader's draft. Markup the browser would have to repair (a block inside a paragraph, a row outside a table, a tag closed by its parent's end tag) is sent as whole pages, and the server log says which repair, once. Handlers and renders run on worker threads, never on the threads that carry the sockets, so a session's slow work delays only its own page. A clicked element is marked data-crow-busy the moment it is clicked, before the server answers, and the mark clears when the answer to that click arrives — the reader sees the click taken in the same frame, with no program code in the browser.

A page sends only what it was offered. A session accepts a click only when a render it sent carried that handler with exactly those arguments (click = set_speed[10.0] offers set_speed with 10.0, a table row its key), a bound input or select only when a render drew it — a select, a radio or a tab only with a value it drew among its options — and a form only when a render drew it. A helper no element names, a fact no input binds, and the button of a closed modal or a hidden element are refused — a confirmation's second step is accepted only after its first opened it. A refusal changes nothing and is logged: crow live: refused 'name' — the page did not offer it. The socket opens only from the app's own page (a page on another site is refused before it can send anything), a message on it is at most 4 MB — an event is a click or a field's text, and a file comes by a request of its own, under the uploads limit — so a bigger one closes the socket, which the page opens again (the bound had been 64 MB), and a served app listens on this machine only; set CROW_HOST=0.0.0.0 where the network must reach it — a container, a server behind a proxy.

Both modes serve pages. crow run file.co compiles and serves; crow run -i file.co serves from the interpreter at once, with no Rust compile between an edit and the page — the live runtime keeps the sessions (their facts, the commit, the notice, the questions, the address, the reconnects) in both, and only who renders the views and runs the handlers differs. One difference is of timing, not of meaning: the interpreter computes a derivation where it is read, so a page served by it waits for its queries, where the compiled page shows the last value and catches up. crow test --served drives every page in tests/served/, and every app under examples/ with a main.events script beside its main.co (served from a fresh copy, so its database starts empty in each mode), with the same events in both modes and compares the pages they settle on.

Mail (use web.mail)

FunctionDescription
smtp[host, port, user, pass]Create an SMTP mailer; its messages come from user
mail[mailer, to, subject, body]Send a plain-text message: held to a handler's commit, so a handler that stops sends nothing; r = mail[…] sends at once and r.ok says whether the server took it

mail is an effect like notice and download: in a handler, or a function a handler calls, a message whose answer is not used waits for the commit, and a stop drops it; used (sent = mail[…]), it is sent when asked, and a failure is the answer's reason. Outside a handler it is sent at once. A view or a derivation sends none, itself or through a function it calls, and is refused if it tries: it runs whenever the page needs it. A mailer is made once for the whole program, as a connection is, and prints as its host, never its password. In a test nothing is sent: mails[a] is the list of what the session sent, each a map of to, subject and body, and crow test --compiled records the compiled program's the same way.

The sending is written in Crow (src/modules/mail.co) on the sys layer, as RFC 5321 says: the greeting, EHLO, the connection secured with STARTTLS where the server offers it (port 465 is TLS from the start; a password travels only encrypted, so a remote server that offers neither is refused — a local relay on the loopback address is not), AUTH PLAIN, the envelope, the message's head (From, To, a Subject beyond ASCII as an encoded word, Date) and its body with its dots stuffed (a body beyond ASCII as base64), QUIT. A recipient the server refuses, an address that is none (name@host), a server that does not answer or answers anything but what the step wants are the reasons (mail to bob@example.com failed: the server answered 550 no such user at RCPT TO). The session layer holds a handler's message to its commit in both modes and hands it to the row's function to send; a script sends it at once. Its tests are the module's own (crow test src/modules/mail.co), and tests/smtp_server.rs runs it against a server in both modes.

Client (use web.client)

A program reads a URL with read and load (Files, URLs and S3), sends any other request with request (Requests), and a page pulls remote data with fetch[url] / poll[url, seconds] (reactive, see above). web.client is no module: the client is the language's own, written in Crow.

Design Note: one process. Considered: sessions any process can resume from a shared store, and a table's version shared across processes, so that an app could run on several machines as a Phoenix cluster does. Decided (session-layer-design.md §6; measured 2026-10-02): an app is one process — its sessions, its derivations' values and its tables' versions live in it — and how far one goes is measured, not guessed: a connected tab costs about 140 KB while it is open, a parked session a few kilobytes, and a trivial route answers tens of thousands of requests a second on a laptop (the book, Polish and Deployment, "How far one process goes"; tests/capacity.rs measures it on any machine). An app that outgrows one process runs several behind a proxy that keeps each tab on its process, sharing the database; a write one process makes reaches another's derivations by their fresh window, as an external source's does. Sessions across processes wait for an app that needs them.

Design Note: what web is. Considered: the modules the web framework had gathered — a task queue (web.queue, push), templates (templates, render), an embedded SQLite handle (web.db's sqlite), session stores (sessions[]), Server-Sent Events and WebSocket servers (sse, ws), an LRU cache (cache), generated API docs (web.docs), and CORS, static files, rate limits and uploads as calls on an app value the language no longer has (cors[app], static_dir[app, …], limit[max, window], uploads[req]). Decided (Vijay, 2026-09-30): each was a second way to do what the language already does, or a stand-in the interpreter answered with a placeholder (render gave [rendered: name]), and each is gone. Background work is later[f, …]; a page is a view, not a template; a database is db.connect; a reader's session is a page's session, with sign_in for who they are; a server reaches a page through its live session and a client through stream; what a program computes once and reads often is a derivation. use web serves pages and routes, and its modules are web.auth, web.mail and web.ws; another is refused by name.

db

The db module provides database connectivity. Import with use db. Supports SQLite, PostgreSQL, and ClickHouse via URL-scheme dispatch. See docs/db-module.md for full architecture details.

Connection

use db

--- SQLite
conn = connect['sqlite://data.db']

--- PostgreSQL (default pool: 10 connections)
conn = connect['postgres://user:pass@host/mydb']

--- ClickHouse (its HTTP interface: port 8123 when none is given)
conn = connect['clickhouse://default@localhost:8123/default']

--- Custom pool size
conn = connect['postgres://user:pass@host/mydb', 20]
FunctionDescription
connect[url]Open a database connection (pool) with default pool size, checking its database holds the tables the program declares on it (The schema a program declares, below)
connect[url, pool_size]Open a database connection (pool) with custom pool size; a pool size of 0 is the default
describe[conn, sql]The columns a statement answers, without running it: a frame of name and type (int, float, str, bool, datetime, with ? after a kind where the column can hold NULL); a ? placeholder stands in as NULL, and a list parameter (IN ?) as a list of one. What the compiler asks at build time (Typed cells, below), asked by the program: the same answer, a computed column typed and a column that can hold NULL marked, in both modes (it had answered the database's word alone — no ? on SQLite or Postgres, and on SQLite unknown for a computed column no row typed and an error for IN ?)

The ClickHouse driver is written in Crow (src/modules/clickhouse.co, design rule 20, docs/db-in-crow-design.md), over its HTTP interface: clickhouse://user:pass@host:port/database names that interface, port 8123 when none is given; the native protocol's port, 9000, is no longer spoken, and a URL that names it is read as the HTTP port, which the log says once. Each statement is sent with an id (crow-<machine>-<process>-<draw>), its rows come back as RowBinary and are decoded by the frame_of_rowbinary kernel, typed as the server names each column; an insert goes as RowBinary encoded by the table's types; a statement a stop abandoned is killed by its id (tests/query_cancel.rs); at connect, the statements a dead run of this machine left running are swept. The server says how long it keeps a connection open (its Keep-Alive header), and the driver notes it: an idle connection past that is let go before the server closes it, not found closed under the next statement. The handle, its pool of kept connections (four, since ClickHouse runs one query on every core already), the reconnect and the cancel are crow_db's, which calls the driver through a table of calls the program registers (the compiled program's generated functions; the interpreter's child over the program's functions). A program never imports the driver: use db carries the drivers its connection URLs name.

The Postgres driver is written in Crow too (src/modules/postgres.co), speaking the wire protocol over sys: a connection started and authenticated — trust, a clear password, md5, or SCRAM-SHA-256 as RFC 7677 says, its proof held to the RFC's own example in the module's tests — and its backend's process and key noted for a cancel; a statement without parameters as a simple query, one with parameters through the extended protocol, each parameter typed by its kind (text, int8, float8, bool; a cast like ?::date is a cast from text) and bound as text; the answer decoded by the frame_of_postgres kernel, typed as the server names each column; a command's count from its completion; insert as COPY … FROM STDIN in the text format (copy_of_frame); describe from Parse and Describe without an Execute; a statement a stop abandoned cancelled by a CancelRequest on a connection of its own. tokio-postgres and deadpool are gone; the pool (ten), a handler's transaction on one connection, transact, the advisory lock around reconciling a schema and the reconnect are crow_db's.

A connection is not the program's lifetime. When the server closes it — a restart drops every one — the next query reopens it and runs, and the server log says so; a program told to stop stops the statements it had in flight on their servers (ClickHouse by the statement's id, Postgres by a CancelRequest) and waits for that, three seconds at most, before it ends: a restarted app does not leave its predecessor's queries running; Postgres and ClickHouse connections are pooled, so queries from different sessions run side by side, and sqlite is a file. Every connect to one Postgres or ClickHouse URL in a program is one handle: parts that each connect to the same server share its pool.

A query nobody waits for is stopped. A background recompute whose session has gone, or whose inputs moved while it ran, stops its query on the server (ClickHouse and Postgres); so does every query in flight when a served program is told to stop — crow stop, a rebuild, Ctrl-C. A program killed outright cannot stop anything; the next run that connects to a ClickHouse server on the same machine stops the queries the dead run left behind, and the log says so. Each ClickHouse statement carries a leading comment naming the machine, the process and a number, which is how it is found again.

Statements

FunctionDescription
query[conn, sql]Run a statement; the rows it answers, as a frame
query[conn, sql, params]The same, with a list of bind values for its ?s

One verb runs every statement. query answers a frame: the rows a SELECT answers, the rows a RETURNING clause reads back, and an empty frame for a statement that answers none — a CREATE, an UPDATE, a DELETE. No count of rows affected is answered: a write that must say what it touched reads it back with RETURNING.

The statement says whether it reads or writes, not the name. A literal statement is read by the compiler: a SELECT (without INTO), VALUES, SHOW, EXPLAIN, DESCRIBE and a PRAGMA asked are reads (db.read); anything else is a write (db.write), RETURNING included, so a derivation may hold a literal read and a handler's writes are held to its commit (Effects Have Kinds). A statement built as the program runs — a string joined from pieces, a parameter — writes for all the compiler can tell: a derivation that hands one is refused, naming the fix, which is the literal with ? for its parameters.

use db
conn = connect['postgres://user:pass@host/mydb']

users = query[conn, 'SELECT * FROM users']
log[len[users]]
filtered = users[users.age gt 25]

query[conn, 'CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (id INT, name TEXT)']
gone = query[conn, 'DELETE FROM users WHERE age < ? RETURNING id', [18]]
log[len[gone]]

Design Note: one statement verb. Considered: exec beside query, two names carrying what the compiler needs before the program runs — the answer's kind (a count or a frame) and the effect's (a write or a read) — which every typed client has (Go's Exec and Query, sqlx, JDBC); a flag on the call. Rejected: the statement already says both, and the compiler reads literal statements anyway; the count was read in one program of the corpus; and a SELECT through exec failed on SQLite with the library's words while Postgres and ClickHouse ran it and threw the rows away. Decided (Vijay, 2026-10-19): one verb, query, a frame for every statement, the literal's first word deciding its kind, a computed statement a write.

List parameters. An array bound as a parameter stands for a list: its ? becomes (?, ?, …) with one placeholder per element, on every backend. Write WHERE id IN ? and pass [[1, 3]]; an empty list matches nothing. A list goes after IN and nowhere else: handed to another ? — x = ANY(?) — it would be sent as a row where the database wants an array, so a literal statement that does so is refused, in both modes, before it runs. The compiler asks a database about IN ? as the list of one it is sent as, IN (?).

A parameter that failed is the statement's answer. A bind value that failed — [float[entry]] where the entry is no number, or an item of a list parameter — is what query answers, with its reason, kind and trace, and the statement is not run, as any call handed a failed argument answers it (Auto-Propagation); a transaction whose function meets one is rolled back. In a handler, a write handed one stops the handler, as every effect held to its commit does (Errors in Conditions), and a read answers it. (The interpreter had stopped — cannot bind done as SQL param — where the compiled program answered the failure; compiled, a handler's read had stopped too, and a list parameter holding an item that failed had not built.)

Typed cells in compiled programs. The compiler asks the program's databases what each literal query returns — a prepare on Postgres, DESCRIBE on ClickHouse — and types every column: rows.price[0] is a float, rows.n[0] an int, in the compiled program as it always was in the interpreter. A column that can hold NULL has fallible cells, in both modes: a NULL one is an error value of reason missing (see frame). The compiler reads which can from the statement and the tables' declarations — a column read from a table can hold NULL unless it is declared NOT NULL (or is SQLite's INTEGER PRIMARY KEY); every column of the side of an outer join a row can have no match on can; an expression can where what it reads can, except count, coalesce with an argument that cannot, IS NULL and a literal, and an aggregate over no rows (no GROUP BY) can; a function it does not know can; and a column the WHERE clause compares or asks IS NOT NULL of cannot, in the rows it keeps. ClickHouse says it itself, by a Nullable type. A statement it cannot read that way can hold NULL in every column. Connections bound to a literal URL (pg = db.connect['postgres://…']) are asked in declaration order; ? placeholders and interpolated spans stand in as NULL. A database that cannot be reached at compile time is skipped. What each database answers is kept in the project's .crow/schema — a file per statement and the connection it was asked on, holding the statement and its columns' types, a column that can hold NULL marked ? (float?) — and when no database answers a statement its cells are typed from that record, and the build says so once; with no record they are dynamic, as below. A project may keep .crow/schema in version control, so a machine without the databases — CI, a laptop without the data — builds the program typed as it was. The frames the standard modules build are typed as they are built: doc.chapters' title, path and part are text and its depth an int, and chart.macd's columns are floats. Frames derived through select, a mask, sort, head, tail, fill, stack, and join keep their column types; group gives its key columns as they were and a float per aggregate (group[df, 'dept', ['salary', 'mean']] has dept and salary_mean); pivot gives its rows column as it was, the rest as they show; a window is typed by what it computes (frame). A frame handed to a function keeps its column types there — each call's, the function a copy per set of columns (see frame, A frame's type is its columns) — so a function reads its cells as the numbers or text they are. A cell of a column whose type nothing tells the compiler — a file loaded as it runs, a query no database answered and no record holds — is dynamic, as a value json[] parsed is, in both modes: int[c], float[c] and str[c] read it as one type (and fail as they do on text), arithmetic or a comparison with a number reads it as a number, and giving one to a variable that holds another type reads it as that type, as a parsed value's field is read (Parsed values: read as its use implies) — a cell that is not what the use reads stops the program and says where (a parsed value holds text 'abc' where the program reads a number (met in mul at line 6)) — and a missing one is JSON's null, an error value of reason null. (Compiled, such a cell was read as text while the interpreter read the number: the same line meant two things.) On sqlite, a computed column (count(*), round(...)) is typed by stepping the SELECT once with null parameters; when no row comes back — WHERE session_id = ?, an empty table — it is typed from the statement itself, by the declared types of the columns it reads and SQLite's rules (round, avg and arithmetic on a REAL are floats, count and comparisons ints, || and datetime text; a parameter or NULL alone stays text). The cells of a RETURNING clause — query[book, 'INSERT ... RETURNING id', [...]], the way to read back a row the database numbered — are typed from the table's declared columns; asking never runs a write, and never creates a sqlite file that is not there yet. Every command asks the same way — crow run, crow run -i, crow test, crow check and the app's book — so a program refused for its columns' types (a local given an int cell, then text) is refused by all of them. Set CROW_NO_DESCRIBE=1 to build without asking: no database answers then, so the records type the cells, and nothing is written.

Bulk Insert

FunctionDescription
insert[conn, table, frame]Bulk insert a frame into a table using native fast paths: the count of rows written, or, when the database refuses them — a constraint they break, a column the table lacks — an error value of kind 'db' with the database's reason, as a refused statement is (compiled, it had stopped on a Rust unwrap)

Uses backend-specific protocols for maximum performance:

  • PostgreSQL: COPY … FROM STDIN, the text format
  • ClickHouse: RowBinary over the HTTP interface, encoded by the table's types (DESCRIBE first)
  • SQLite: Batched prepared statements in a transaction
use db
conn = connect['clickhouse://localhost/default']
insert[conn, 'events', events_frame]

Transactions

FunctionDescription
transact[conn, fn]The function run inside a transaction: its value, committed; or its error, rolled back

One connection is checked out, BEGIN run on it, and the function handed it as its one parameter; its statements through that parameter are the transaction's. transact answers what the function answers. A function that fails — its value an error (return error['no', 'db'], or a failed value it returns), or a stop inside it — rolls the transaction back, and transact answers that error (a stop stops, as a stop does); a function that succeeds commits, and a commit the server refuses answers an error of kind 'db' with the server's words. Inside a handler, whose writes through a connection are one transaction already (A handler is a transaction), transact is a savepoint on it: released when the function succeeds, rolled back to when it fails, the handler's other writes untouched. The same in both modes (parity 167). No begin/commit/rollback builtins — one way to do transactions.

use db
conn = connect['postgres://user:pass@host/mydb']

fn transfer[t]
  query[t, 'UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE id = 1']
  query[t, 'UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance + 100 WHERE id = 2']

transact[conn, transfer]

Migrations

Migrations are the tool's, not a program's: crow db apply <dir> <url> runs the .sql files of a directory not yet applied on the database, in name order, recording each in a _crow_migrations table, and says how many were new. It is the apply half of crow db diff, which drafts the statements a schema change needs (below).

The schema a program declares

A program's tables are the ones its own statements make. Every literal CREATE TABLE a program hands a connection — query[book, 'CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS cards (…)'], directly or through a function the connection is handed to (setup[book]) — declares that table, with its columns, on that connection. A table the program also drops there (DROP TABLE IF EXISTS scratch) is one it remakes each run, and declares nothing. A connection the program declares no table on is one it does not own: nothing below reads or changes it, and the tables and columns a database has beyond the declared ones are its own.

Opening a connection checks that the tables the database has hold what the program declares, in one of three modes, the same in both modes of running. A declared table the database does not have yet is not a difference: the program's own statement makes it as it runs, so a fresh database starts as it always has.

ModeAt connect
reconcileWhat a table lacks is made: a declared column it does not have is added (ALTER TABLE … ADD COLUMN, as the statement declares it), under the database's lock (Postgres: an advisory lock, in one transaction; SQLite: an immediate transaction), so two processes starting at once make it once. A column SQLite cannot add in place — a key, or NOT NULL without a default — is made by making a table with no rows anew. A difference reconciling cannot make without losing data or guessing — a column of another kind than declared, a NOT NULL column without a default for a table that has rows — stops the program with each difference and the statements that would make it, and nothing is made.
verifyNothing is changed. The database's catalog is read (reading it needs no right beyond reading), and every declared column of every table the database has must be there, of the declared kind; a difference stops the program at connect with each difference and the statements that would mend it.
trustNothing is read, and every start says so: schema not verified — schema = 'trust'.

A column's kind is the kind Crow reads its cells as (int, float, str, bool, datetime): TEXT and VARCHAR(20) are one kind, INTEGER and TEXT two. A column declared without a type is of any kind.

A program run from its source — crow run, crow run -i, crow test — reconciles: it is being developed, against a database of its own. A program crow build makes verifies: it runs where the database is someone else's to change. The crowfile's schema = 'verify' (or 'reconcile', 'trust') says otherwise for both, and is what a built program starts with; the environment's CROW_SCHEMA says otherwise for one run (CROW_SCHEMA=trust ./app).

crow db diff [file.co] prints, for each connection a program declares tables on and opens with a url it writes, the SQL that would make its database hold what the program declares: the CREATE TABLE of each declared table it lacks, the ADD COLUMN statements reconciling would run and, commented out, the changes reconciling will not make (a column of another kind, a NOT NULL column a table with rows cannot take without a default) — a draft for a migration to review. It only reads the catalog; a SQLite file that is not there is not made.

$ crow db diff app.co
-- sqlite://board.db (book)
-- table 'cards' lacks column 'due'
ALTER TABLE "cards" ADD COLUMN due TEXT;
-- table 'labels' is missing
CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS labels (card INTEGER, name TEXT);

Design Note: schema modes. Considered: migrations alone, applied by hand, which leave a table the program changed — a new column — to fail at the first query that reads it, in development and in production alike; making the schema everywhere (Prisma's db push, Django's old syncdb), which alters production databases the program's user has no right to alter and must not; and a check only in production. Decided (Vijay, July 2026; approved September 2026): a program's CREATE statements declare its tables; development makes what is missing under a lock, a built program checks it at connect, and crow db diff drafts the migration for what making would lose (as Terraform's plan drafts before an apply, and Prisma's db push in development meets migrate deploy in production). At connect a missing table is left to the program's own CREATE, which runs moments later: checking it there stopped every built program on its first run against a fresh database, and making it there broke a program whose own CREATE TABLE (without IF NOT EXISTS) then found it made — the drift worth stopping for is the table that exists with other columns, which CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS silently keeps.

Pool Defaults

BackendDefault Pool SizeNotes
SQLite1File-based, concurrent writes lock
PostgreSQL10Standard for web/data apps; the driver written in Crow keeps the connections it opened
ClickHouse4The HTTP interface, kept alive; opened as queries need them. One query already runs on every core of the server, so more at once contend rather than help

llm

Import with use llm. A language model as a connection: one way to ask a model, the same in both modes, over two wire formats — Anthropic's Messages API, and the OpenAI-compatible chat completions that Ollama, vLLM, OpenRouter and LiteLLM serve too — so a program written against a local model runs unchanged against a hosted one. Written in Crow on the sys layer (design rule 20; src/modules/llm.co), its functions its declaration (section 13's opening: reach, ask, asked, said, told, chat, follow, next, reply, embed, act); crow test src/modules/llm.co runs its own tests, and tests/llm_server.rs drives it against a server speaking both formats in both modes, the agent loop against runs that server scripts. The design is docs/llm-design.md and, for the loop, docs/agents-design.md.

use llm

m = reach['anthropic://claude-sonnet-5-5']
talk = ask['What is the capital of Mongolia?']
r = chat[m, talk, system = 'Answer in one word.']
log[r.text]                              --- Ulaanbaatar
log[r.usage.input]                       --- 21
FunctionDescription
reach[url]A model as a connection: anthropic://<model> or openai://<host>[:port]/<model> (any server speaking the OpenAI chat completions: Ollama at openai://localhost:11434/llama3.2, vLLM, OpenRouter, LiteLLM). The key is read from the environment when a call is made — ANTHROPIC_API_KEY or OPENAI_API_KEY, or the variable ?key=NAME names — and is in no value; a local host (localhost, 127.0.0.1) needs none and is reached over http, any other over https. Answers the model's description, a parsed value: m.provider, m.model, m.base, m.key (the variable's name). An error value of kind 'llm': a URL of neither scheme, no model named, the key's variable unset for a remote host
ask[text]A conversation of one user turn: a parsed value, a list of turns
asked[talk, text]The conversation with another user turn added
said[talk, reply]The conversation with the model's reply added — its text and its tool calls — as the next request needs it
told[talk, call, text, failed = false]The conversation with a tool's result added: what the program computed for one of the reply's calls. failed = true marks the result a failure the model is to hear as one: the turn says so, Anthropic's block carries is_error, and the OpenAI-compatible turn its text alone, since that wire has no flag
chat[m, talk]The model's reply: a parsed value with text, calls (each id, name and args, the arguments as the model wrote them, read as the program's use implies), usage (input and output tokens) and stop — end, tool (the model waits for its calls' results), length (its tokens reached) or other. By name: system = text, tools = [f, g] (the functions themselves, see below), tokens = n (1024), temperature = t (1.0). An error value of kind 'llm' when no answer came, when the server's status is no success (its message quoted: the model's server answered 401: invalid x-api-key) or when its answer is no JSON
follow[m, talk]The same request, the reply followed as the model writes it: a socket the pieces are read from; the same options and errors as chat
next[s]The next piece of the reply's text, as the model writes it; empty text once the reply has ended. A tool call the model streams is gathered into reply, not shown. An error value of kind 'llm' when the connection breaks
reply[s]The whole reply the socket has carried so far, in chat's shape — complete once next has answered empty text
embed[m, texts]The texts' embeddings: a matrix, a row per text, from an OpenAI-compatible embeddings endpoint (openai://host/<embedding model>); an Anthropic connection is refused, since it serves none
act[m, talk, tools = [], allow = [], rounds = 8, system = '', tokens = 1024, temperature = 1.0, budget = 0, keep = 0, compact = 0, skills = '', servers = []]The agent loop: the model runs its tools until it answers or a limit stops it. Each reply's calls are answered in order: the program's gates first (allow = [g, h], each a function of the call — a parsed value with the call's name, args and id — answering a bool; every one must allow the call, and a gate that takes another count of parameters or answers another kind is refused with why before any model is asked), then the tool of the call's name, applied to its arguments read by each parameter's kind (apply, sys); a refusal, a name no tool has, an argument missing or of another kind and a tool's own failure are each told to the model as a failed result that says why, so every call is answered. At most rounds model calls and at most budget tokens in all (0 for none): a limit stops the run after the reply's calls are answered, so the conversation ends well-formed and can be continued. keep = n cuts the tool results of rounds older than the last n to a stub naming their size ([a result of 2400 characters, cut], the turn marked cut), compact = n asks the model for a summary once a reply has read n tokens or more and goes on from the summary alone (a step of what compact), skills = folder names a folder of skills (below), and servers = [s] adds the tools each MCP server offers (use mcp), called on the server. Answers a record: text (the model's last words), stop (end, length, other, rounds, budget), rounds, usage (input and output tokens in all, a parsed value as a reply's), talk (the conversation as it ended) and steps, a frame with a row per model call, tool call and compaction — step, what (chat, tool or compact), name (the model's or the tool's), input, output, ms, and how (a chat's stop; a tool's ok, failed, arguments, refused or unknown). An error value of kind 'llm' as chat's, for rounds below one, for a gate that is none, for a skill that breaks the standard's rules, and for a tool's name two of the lists share

chat, follow, next, embed and act are effects: a route's handler that calls one runs off the server's threads (Routes), and crow check sees the call.

Tools are the functions. tools = [weather, search] hands the functions themselves — a group (Specialization) — and the module writes each one's schema from its description (describe[t], sys): its name, its parameters by name with the kinds inference found — an int integer, a float number, text string, a bool boolean, a list an array of its items' kind, a map, a record or a parsed value an object — and, as the description, the comment written above the function. A parameter's kind is what the program's calls of the function hand it, or, where the program never calls it (a tool only the model calls), what its body reads it as: handed to a builtin whose rows take one kind there, upper[city] reads it as text; read by its fields, order.lines and order.id, it is a parsed value — an object in the schema, handed as the model wrote it. (Only a call had typed one, and a tool read in its body was refused with the advice to read it there; the field read came 2026-10-20, when a gate that read the call's fields was refused the same way, its body doing what the advice said.) A function whose parameter nothing types is refused before the program runs, with the fix (weather's parameter 'city' is typed by nothing — read it in the body, or call weather[…] once with a value of its kind), and so is a tools that is not a list of functions written. The program makes the calls: the reply's calls name a tool and its arguments, the program dispatches on the name, reads the arguments as its use implies and hands each result back with told.

--- the current weather in a city, in °C
fn weather[city]
    r = request['https://wttr.in/`city`?format=j1']
    return str[json[r.body].current_condition[0].temp_C]

talk = ask['How warm is it in Lisbon right now?']
r = chat[m, talk, tools = [weather]]
while r.stop eq 'tool'
    talk = said[talk, r]
    for call in r.calls
        if call.name eq 'weather'
            talk = told[talk, call, weather[call.args.city]]
    r = chat[m, talk, tools = [weather]]
log[r.text]

The loop is the module's. Written by hand, that loop has three gaps every program would close again: no limit, a call the if does not handle left unanswered (the next request is refused), a tool that fails ending the run with nothing told to the model. act[m, talk, tools = [weather]] is the loop written once, in Crow, in the module: the dispatch is apply over the group (sys), which the compiler writes for any list of functions, so every call the model can cause is to a member of the list the program wrote, crow check sees each member called with its effects, and the limits, the failed results and the record of the steps are one code for every program (docs/agents-design.md). A tool's failure, a wrong argument and an unknown name are told to the model, each as a failed result whose text says why, since a model that hears why can try another way; told[…, failed = true] tells one by hand. The program's say between the model's decision and the tool's run is the gate: allow = [g] names functions of the call, each asked before a tool runs, and a call one of them refuses is told to the model as a failed result (remove is not allowed by the program) rather than run — the if the hand-written loop had, kept where the loop is the module's.

--- the weather may be asked for Lisbon alone
fn only_lisbon[call]
    if str[call.name] ne 'weather'
        return true
    return str[call.args.city] eq 'Lisbon'

done = act[m, ask['Is it warmer in Lisbon or Porto?'], tools = [weather], allow = [only_lisbon], rounds = 8]
log[done.text]            --- the answer
log[done.stop]            --- end, or the limit that stopped the run
log[done.steps]           --- a frame: every model call and tool call, in order

A subagent is a tool that runs the loop. A tool is a function the model calls, and a tool whose body runs act with a tool list of its own is a subagent: the parent model calls research[question], research runs an inner loop — its own model, prompt, tools, gates and limits — and answers what the inner run found. Nothing of it is new: a function sees no top-level name (Function Scope), so the inner loop reaches its model itself; the loop is copied per tool list (Specialization), the inner copy reached through the outer copy's dispatch of research by name; and the tool's parameter is typed as any tool's is — trim[question] reads the question as text, where ask[question] takes any value and types nothing. The inner run's record is the tool's to read: inner.text answered is what the parent hears, as any tool's result, and a run that failed fails the tool — return inner.text of a failed run answers its failure — so the parent model hears a failed result that says why (cannot reach the model at …, told as a failure, how failed in the steps), or the tool reads inner.reason and answers in words of its own, failed or not. The parent's steps frame shows the delegation as one tool row named research, its ms covering the whole inner run; the inner run's steps are the tool's own, read from inner.steps and logged or kept where the tool decides (decided 2026-10-20: a subagent needs nothing new, and the delegating run is held in both modes — tests/llm_server.rs, parity 277).

--- what a smaller agent finds out about a question, with the weather as
--- its only tool
fn research[question]
    small = reach['openai://localhost:11434/llama3.2']
    inner = act[small, ask[trim[question]], tools = [weather], rounds = 4]
    return inner.text

done = act[m, ask['Is it warm in Lisbon today?'], tools = [research]]
log[done.text]            --- what the parent answered, from what research found
log[done.steps.name]      --- the parent's steps: its model, research, its model

Skills are know-how loaded on demand. skills = 'skills/' names a folder in the Agent Skills format (agentskills.io): each skill a folder holding a SKILL.md — YAML frontmatter with its name (1 to 64 characters, lowercase letters, digits and single hyphens, neither first nor last, matching the folder) and its description (1 to 1024 characters), then the instructions — and, beside it, the files the instructions refer to. The loop reads every skill's frontmatter once, names each with its description in the system prompt, and offers the model two tools of the module's own: skill[name] answers a skill's instructions, skill_file[name, path] a file inside the skill (a path that leaves it is refused), so a hundred skills cost the model a line each until one is used. A skill that breaks a rule is the call's error value, naming the skill and the rule, before any model is asked; a program's tool named skill or skill_file is refused. Scripts are not run: running one is shell, which waits on the gate.

A reply followed is a loop the program writes. A route forwards the pieces as it reads them with the response stream[feed], a page keeps them in a fact a view shows, a script logs them:

s = follow[m, ask['Tell me a story in three sentences.']]
piece = next[s]
while piece.ok and len[piece] gt 0
    log[piece]
    piece = next[s]
whole = reply[s]

A conversation is a parsed value — a list of turns, each its role (user, assistant, tool) and text, a model's turn with its calls, a tool's with the id of the call it answers — because a map holds one kind and a turn holds several, and JSON is the language's shape for that; the builders keep every turn in the one shape both wire formats are written from, so a conversation started against one provider continues against the other. One reply shape the same way: what one provider answers and the other does not is not there until both mean it.

Design Note: a model as a connection, named reach. Considered: the model named on every call, as most SDKs take a model string (rejected: the URL parsed and the key's presence checked at every call, and a program's calls not read as calls on one model); connect, as db makes its handle (rejected: connect is db's own form, and a program that imports both — an agent that keeps its notes in a database — would write every call qualified); model (rejected: the name most programs give the thing they fit). Decided: reach[url], a handle made once; the connection holds no secret, since the key is read from the environment as each call is made, so what it holds is description and a parsed value is readable where a handle kind would be opaque (m.model in a log line), with no kind to thread through the compiler (design rule 21).

Design Note: tools are the functions, the schema the compiler's. Considered: a tool written by hand — a name, a description and a schema beside the function (rejected: the signature said twice, and the two drift); the module calling the tool for the program and sending the result back by itself, as agent SDKs do (rejected: a call the program never wrote, an effect crow check could not see, and a model deciding what runs with no if of the program's between); a description handed beside the tool, describe = map[weather = '…'] (rejected: one thing in two places — the comment above the function is the sentence the code already carries). Decided: tools = [f, g], the compiler writes each schema from the signature it inferred and the function's comment, and the program dispatches.

Design Note: who runs the tools. Considered: the program writes the loop, as before (kept, and the only way until 2026-10-20; as the only way rejected: three gaps written again in each program); the lowering expanding act into a loop over hidden rows of llm's, the dispatch written by the compiler per listed tool (rejected: the compiler holds the language and names no module, design rule 24); the module dispatching by name at run time over a table from names to function values (rejected: Crow has no such value — a list of functions is a group whose members are known before the program runs — and the checker could not tell which functions will run). Decided (2026-10-20): the module writes the loop in Crow, and the dispatch is apply over the group, which the compiler writes for any group. It answers this section's objections to a module calling the program's functions: every call the model can cause is to a member of a list the program wrote, the dispatch is over those members alone, and crow check sees each one called, with its effects. A gate the program hands the loop — allow = [g], functions of the call answering a bool, every one asked before a tool runs — needed one rule of inference, since a gate's parameter is a parsed value its body reads by its fields, which had typed nothing (a parameter was typed by a call of it, or by a single-kind builtin read): a parameter read by its fields that nothing else types is a parsed value (Vijay, 2026-10-20). Considered for the gate: a plain function parameter (rejected: a function taking a function is lowered once, so one act would serve one tool list per program, and no subagent could have tools of its own); a required gate (rejected: a program with tools that reach nothing writes nothing). A run's record is a frame, one row per step, so traces go to ClickHouse and to charts with no conversion; its columns avoid kind, ok, reason and trace, which are every value's own.

Design Note: a reply followed is a socket read piece by piece, not a feed. Considered: a function handed to follow and called for each piece, as a response stream[feed] hands its feed a put (rejected: a builtin's row takes no function value — one handed to a row is typed by nothing — and a loop the program writes can stop, show, keep or forward a piece where a callback only receives it). Decided: follow answers a socket, next the pieces, reply the whole; the loop is the program's.

Not in this round, each a later note: images and documents in a turn, a conversation the server keeps (prompt caching, stored responses), batches, and embed for a provider that serves no OpenAI-compatible endpoint. A model's answer is kept and replayed as any request's is (Requests: crow check --record-fixtures, crow test --fixtures), so an agent's test runs without the model.

mcp

Import with use mcp. Tools from other programs, and a Crow program as one: the Model Context Protocol, revision 2026-07-28 — stateless, every request carrying its protocol version and the client's capabilities in _meta — over both of its transports: Streamable HTTP, one POST per message, and stdio, a process the program starts and talks to one message per line. Written in Crow on request and the sys layer (design rule 20; src/modules/mcp.co), its functions its declaration (server, discover, catalog, tool, answer, listen); crow test src/modules/mcp.co runs its own tests, and tests/mcp.rs drives a Crow client against a stand-in server, a stand-in client against a Crow server, and a Crow client against a Crow server over stdio, in both modes. The design is docs/agents-design.md.

use mcp

s = server['http://localhost:8000/mcp']
log[catalog[s][0].name]                      --- the first tool the server offers
log[tool[s, 'weather', map[city = 'Lisbon']]] --- the text its result carries
files = server[command = 'npx -y @modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem /tmp']
log[catalog[files][0].name]                  --- the same calls, the server a process
FunctionDescription
server[url = '', token = '', command = '']An MCP server as a connection: the URL of its MCP endpoint (http:// or https://), and the bearer token its requests carry, if any — or, by name, the command that runs it over its standard streams (the stdio transport), as shell[cmd] takes a command: server[command = 'npx -y @modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem /tmp']. A parsed value: s.url, s.token, s.command. An error value of kind 'mcp' for a URL that names no endpoint, for a URL beside a command, for a token with a command (stdio takes its credentials from the environment, as the protocol says), and for nothing named
discover[s]What the server says of itself (server/discover): supportedVersions, capabilities, its instructions for a model, and who it is under _meta; a parsed value
catalog[s]The tools the server offers (tools/list): a parsed value list, each its name, description and inputSchema. A response streamed as events is read to its last message
tool[s, name, given]A tool called with its arguments — a map, or a parsed value (tools/call) — answering the text its result's content carries. A result the server marks an error (isError) is an error value of kind 'mcp' saying what it said, as is a tool the server does not have, a server that answers an error, and one nobody answers (over stdio: a process that ended, or wrote a line that is no message, cannot reach the MCP server /bin/cat: the process ended)
answer[req, tools = [f, g], name = 'crow', instructions = '']The request a POST route was handed, answered as an MCP server over the functions themselves: server/discover with the versions, the capabilities and the instructions; tools/list with each tool's schema from its description (describe, sys — the comment above the function, its parameters by name with the kinds inference found); tools/call with the tool of the name applied to its arguments (apply), its value's text the result, a wrong argument or the tool's own failure a result marked an error, a name no tool has the protocol's Unknown tool error. A notification is accepted with 202; a body that is no JSON, a request missing its _meta or the headers the transport requires (MCP-Protocol-Version, Mcp-Method, Mcp-Name), and one of another protocol version are refused with 400 and the JSON-RPC error the protocol names (-32700, -32602, -32020, -32022); an unknown method with 404 and -32601

| listen[tools = [f, g], name = 'crow', instructions = ''] | The program as an MCP server over its own standard streams: every line of its standard input a message, answered on its standard output exactly as answer answers a route's request — the same server/discover, tools/list and tools/call, the same errors in the message (there is no status and no header over stdio: the body is the source of truth) — a notification answered with nothing, each answer written whole before the next line is read, until the input ends (an empty line ends it too: a message is never empty). Answers empty text. A program serving so writes nothing else to its standard output; its standard error is its own (log goes to standard output, so a serving program keeps its notes for standard error or a file) |

discover, catalog and tool reach the network through request, or a process through the layer's send and recv, and crow check sees the call. A program becomes an MCP server with one route:

use web, mcp

--- the current weather in a city, in °C
fn weather[city]
    r = request['https://wttr.in/`trim[city]`?format=j1']
    return str[json[r.body].current_condition[0].temp_C]

fn served[req]
    return answer[req, tools = [weather], name = 'weather-server']

route['/mcp', 'POST', served]
serve[8000]

The stdio transport: a server is a process the program talks to. server[command = 'crow run tools.co'] names a server by the command that runs it. The process starts at the first message — start[command] (sys): sh -c runs the command with its standard input and output piped, as the protocol's client launches its server — and is kept while the thread that started it lives, one process per command on each thread (a served program's handlers, each on a thread of its own, each talk to a process of their own, so no two exchanges interleave), started again at the next message if it ended, as the transport says a client restarts a server that exited. Each request is one line on its input, with the _meta every request carries (the revision mirrors nothing into headers over stdio: there is no header layer), and the response the line on its output carrying the request's id — the ids count up on the process, so a late answer to an earlier request is passed over, as are the notifications a server writes before its response; a line that is no JSON message is refused with its words, since the transport forbids a server anything else on its output. The server's standard error is the program's own: the transport lets a server log there, and a client may forward it, which this one does. Ten minutes is the most a message waits, as over HTTP. A Crow program is such a server with listen[tools = [f, g]] in place of the route: the same answers, the same errors, over its standard streams; a legacy client's initialize is refused naming the revision spoken, as the revision asks of a modern-only server. The client is modern-only: it does not probe with server/discover before another request, and discover is the program's to call (the revision recommends probing to fail deterministically against a legacy server, which here fails with the server's own error instead).

use mcp

--- the current weather in a city, in °C
fn weather[city]
    r = request['https://wttr.in/`trim[city]`?format=j1']
    return str[json[r.body].current_condition[0].temp_C]

listen[tools = [weather], name = 'weather-server']    --- crow run weather.co, as a client's command

Design Note: a module of its own, stateless as the protocol is. Considered: the client inside use llm, since an agent is who calls a server's tools (rejected: a server needs no model, and use llm stays the one place a program reaches a model); the connection as a handle kind (rejected, as reach's was: it holds a URL and a token's text, which a parsed value shows); the earlier revisions' initialize handshake and Mcp-Session-Id (rejected: the 2026-07-28 revision removed sessions, and a client sends one request and reads one answer, which is what request does; a legacy server answers 400 with no modern error, which the client names). Decided (2026-10-20): use mcp, the client on request with the headers the transport mirrors from the body, the server a function a POST route calls, the schema writer the same shape use llm writes for a model (a helper is a module's own, so each module writes it).

Design Note: a process is a socket, and a server is named by its command. The stdio transport (2026-10-20, docs/agents-design.md) needed a process held open. Considered, for the handle: a handle kind of its own, declared beside sys's rows (rejected: a kind is a Value variant, a Kind, an Arg and an arm in every rule that meets them — a compiler change, where the compiler is frozen — and a process is read and written as bytes, which is what a stream is); a stream (start[command] answering what open answers, rejected: a message is a line, and what a read takes past the line's end must stay with the process for the next read, which is what a socket holds beside its stream — so a process is a socket, as a WebSocket's connection is: type[p] is socket, send, recv, leftover, unread and close serve it, and nothing was added but start). Considered, for the surface: the transport inside the URL (server['stdio: npx …'], rejected: a parse hidden in text, and a URL that is none); a second export naming a command (command['npx …'], rejected: server is the one way to name a server, the transport an option); a program and a list of arguments, as the SDKs' configurations are written (server[command = 'npx', args = ['-y', …]], rejected: a second way to write a command beside shell's, and a list option with a [] default makes a copy of the function whose items have no kind) — decided: server[command = text], the command as shell takes one, the shell's own quoting. Considered, for the handle's place: a record answered by server, holding the socket (rejected: act reads its servers as their JSON, since a list option's default [] has items of no kind, and a handle is no JSON); a process per message, the protocol being stateless (rejected: a server such as npx … takes seconds to start, and the transport says a client restarts a server only when it exits) — decided: the process is the runtime's, one per command on each thread, found and started by start at every message (per program had let two handlers of a served program write to one process at once and read each other's lines), so server[…] stays a parsed value and the loop's servers = [s] is unchanged. Considered, for when it starts: at server[…] (rejected: server would be an effect for one transport and none for the other, and the shell starts sh -c nosuch as readily as a server, so nothing fails earlier) — decided: at the first message, where crow check sees the call. Its standard error is inherited: the transport lets a server log there and a client forward it, and a server's reason for ending is then where the program's own stops are. What the revision says of stdio (https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/transports/stdio, read 2026-10-20): messages are newline-delimited JSON-RPC with no embedded newlines; the server may write anything to standard error and must write nothing but messages to standard output; request metadata is carried in the body's _meta alone, "there is no header layer"; the client ends the server by closing its input, waiting for it to exit, then forcing it, TERM before KILL on POSIX — and a server should exit promptly when its input closes; a client should restart a server that exits unexpectedly; a dual-era client probes with server/discover and falls back to initialize on any error that is not a recognized modern one, and a modern-only server should name the versions it speaks in its answer to initialize. The runtime's close and the restart follow it; the fallback is not built, since the client speaks one revision.

14. Parallelism

Crow is parallel where the work is: a for loop runs its iterations on every core, a frame or tensor operation uses every core inside itself, and a database query is parallel on its server. No explicit threading, async/await, or channels needed.

Statements Run in Order

Statements execute in the order they are written, in both modes. Two statements that look independent may still share something no variable names — the random generator, the clock, the output, a file, a table — and the order written is the order that happens:

seed[1]
a = rand[]                   --- the first number of the seeded sequence
b = rand[]                   --- the second
write['out.csv', text]
df = frame.load['out.csv']   --- reads what the line above wrote

Work that should overlap is written as a for over the pieces whose body only computes: its rounds run across the cores (below). A loop that reads, writes, requests or queries keeps its order, as statements do.

Design Note: Statements Run in Order (a reversal)

What shipped first: the runtime grouped independent assignments — statements sharing no variable — into waves and ran each wave in parallel, in both modes, so a = fetch['url1'] and b = fetch['url2'] overlapped with nothing written.

Evidence: a seeded script drawing three numbers in three independent statements gave five different results in thirty runs; a write followed by a load of the same file was one wave, so the read could run before the write. The analysis saw variables only. The random generator, the clock, the output stream, files, tables, and every function that touches them were invisible to it, and a miss was a timing-dependent bug that no test catches reliably.

What was considered: making the analysis exact by ending a wave at every statement that is not pure. That is a few days of work, and it leaves nothing worth running in parallel: what remains is pure arithmetic and the frame and tensor operations, which are parallel inside already. Keeping "unordered by default" with an explicit sequencing form was also considered; it asks every programmer to know which builtins carry hidden state, the opposite of never thinking about parallelism.

Why this design: the programmer never thinks about order, because the order is the one written, as in every language they already know. Parallelism stays where the time goes — for, the kernels, the database — with nothing to write. crow test --bench is the measure of what the change costs, and the benchmark programs did not slow down when the waves went.

For Loops

A for loop runs its iterations in parallel when nothing its body does can show the order they ran in, and in order when something can. Both modes decide the same way, once, from the program; a collecting for keeps its results in input order either way.

A loop runs in order when its body:

  • writes a variable from outside the loop, or a fact;
  • reaches an effect of any kind (Effects Have Kinds): output, files, the database, the network, the clock, later, notice, download, a sign-in;
  • draws from the random generator (rand, and randn, shuffle and the other draws of math — the kind random), so a seeded program draws the same numbers on every run;
  • renders a view or a paged table, which a page numbers in the order it draws them;
  • asserts, or asks whether background work is running;
  • can leave the function: a return, or an error handed back from a condition, where the first failure in input order decides;
  • calls a function that does any of these, however deep. A function handed to a builtin (any[xs, check]) counts as called, and a call through a variable counts as a call of any function the program holds as a value.

Everything else runs in parallel on every core: arithmetic, text, lists, maps, records, frames and tensors, reads of facts and derivations, elements, and calls to functions that do only these.

for x in items
    process[x]         --- parallel when process only computes
total = 0
for x in items
    total = total + x  --- in order: writes the outer 'total'
for x in items
    log[x]             --- in order: output is an effect

cpu[1] runs every loop that starts after it on one core (Resource Control).

Design Note: When a Loop Runs in Parallel

What shipped first: each mode decided for itself. The interpreter ran a loop in order as soon as its body called a function the program defined; the compiled program ran it in parallel unless the function reached an effect. Neither looked at the random generator, rendering, or a way out of the function, so a seeded loop of rand[] drew different numbers on every run, and two views rendering at once could mix up the page's marks.
