Why this design: "no side effects, then parallel" is the rule a reader can predict, and it is decided once, where every function's reach is known, so the modes cannot drift: the IR carries the answer (crow build --ir shows parallel on each loop that has it) and both backends only read it. The cost is a loop over a function that logs or draws running on one core, and the idiom that keeps it parallel is the one the book teaches anyway: compute in the loop, print after.

While Loops

while loops run sequentially.

while x gt 0
    x = x - 1

Resource Control

By default, Crow uses all available CPU cores. Use cpu or gpu to control resources; running the program as several processes is crow launch's (Distributed Support).

cpu[4]                   --- 4 CPU cores
gpu[]                    --- all GPUs on this machine
gpu[2]                   --- 2 GPUs on this machine

cpu[n] bounds every parallel loop that starts after it runs — in the code after it and in every function that code calls, whichever was written first — until another cpu[…] changes it; written in a function, it holds until the function returns, and the caller's count is back. A loop nested in a bounded loop runs on the same cores. A count below 1 stops the program (cpu[] requires a positive integer). Both modes read the count where a loop starts, so the same loops are bounded in both. (The compiled program had applied cpu[1] while it wrote the program, so a loop in a function written above the call ran on every core, and cpu[4] bounded nothing; the interpreter had sized one pool for the whole process at the first cpu[…], and ignored every later one.)

Function-level

fn heavy_work[]
    cpu[4]
    for x in items
        process[x]           --- 4 cores

fn gpu_work[]
    gpu[]
    for x in items
        compute[x]           --- all GPUs

Global

Set program-wide defaults in the crowfile (whole numbers of 1 or more). Both modes take them, for the entry and for a job alike. Any function can override locally.

--- crowfile

cpu = 4
gpu = 1

--- file: main.co

fn task_a[]
    for x in items
        process[x]           --- 4 cores (from crowfile)

fn task_b[]
    cpu[8]                   --- override: 8 cores for this function
    for x in items
        heavy[x]

GPU Support

gpu[n] specifies intent — "use n GPUs." gpu[] uses all available GPUs. The language does not expose any GPU vendor API. The compiler decides what is GPU-eligible (numeric operations on arrays) and what falls back to CPU (string ops, I/O, map lookups). A compiled program links the GPU only where it writes gpu[…]: the runtime's GPU support (wgpu) and the system frameworks it brings — Metal, QuartzCore, Foundation — had been linked by every program, and their loading had been four of a small program's seven megabytes; without it every tensor operation runs on the CPU, as it does where no GPU answers.

Implementation

When gpu[] or gpu[n] is called, a global GPU-enabled flag is set. This has two effects:

  1. Tensor auto-placement: Tensor creation functions (zeros, ones, full, arange, linspace, eye, randn, uniform and the other distributions) automatically place data on GPU memory when the flag is set and a GPU is available.

  2. Op auto-dispatch: Element-wise operations (+, -, *, /, neg, abs, exp, log, sqrt, sin, cos) and scalar operations automatically dispatch to GPU compute shaders (WGSL via wgpu) when both operands are on GPU: a number added to, taken from or multiplied with a tensor there, and a number divided by one, run there too — by one function both modes call, so they answer the same digits (the compiled program had subtracted and divided on the CPU where the interpreter did both on the GPU). Reductions (sum) also dispatch to GPU. If tensors are on CPU, the CPU path is used as fallback, and so is an operation with no shader (a tensor divided by a number, a power) and a view of a tensor on the GPU — a slice, a transpose — which is read through its own offset and strides from the device's numbers (a kernel takes a whole tensor as one row-major run: transpose[m] had been read as m). A number no f32 holds stays off the GPU.

GPU uses f32 internally (wgpu limitation). CrowTensor uses f64. Conversion happens transparently during GPU upload/download, with ~7 significant digits of precision on GPU vs ~15 on CPU.

Automatic Differentiation (grad)

grad[f] takes a function and returns a function of the same parameters that computes the gradient of f: its derivative with respect to each parameter. A function of numbers is differentiated forward, with dual numbers; a function of tensors backward, over a tape its run records. The grad builtin works identically in both modes.

Numbers

When f takes one number and returns a number, grad[f] returns a function that computes the derivative:

fn f[x]
  x * x

df = grad[f]
log[df[3]]       --- 6 (derivative of x^2 at x=3 is 2*3=6)

When f takes several numbers, grad[f] returns a function that computes the gradient (a list of partial derivatives):

fn g[x, y]
  x * x + y * y

dg = grad[g]
log[dg[3, 4]]    --- [6, 8] (∂g/∂x=2x=6, ∂g/∂y=2y=8)

All arithmetic operations (+, -, *, /, ^) and sqrt, abs, exp, ln, log2, log10, sin, cos, tan, asin, acos, atan, sinh, cosh, tanh, erf, floor, ceil, round and sign are differentiable (the last four have derivative 0) — every function of a number the math module has (gamma, factorial, choose and bessel take tensors, and atan2, of two numbers or of tensors, is not differentiated), and % is refused. (The dual numbers had carried arithmetic, sqrt, abs and the roundings only, so a function with sin or exp in it stopped as it ran.) Forward mode carries a value and a derivative in each number; for N parameters, N passes compute the gradient. A helper f calls — a function of numbers answering one — is differentiated too, through its own run on the dual numbers, in both modes (compiled, the helper had only its float version, and the program had not built). A function handed to grad is a function of floats: its parameters are floats wherever it is called, so an int handed to it is a float, as it is to any float parameter, and the builtins its body calls are the ones for floats. (Its parameters had no type, grad[f][3] left them so even where df = grad[f] did not, and a compiled program that called such a function with an int had not built.)

Tensors

When a call of the gradient hands f tensors, f is a function of tensors and grad[f] computes the gradient of the number f answers with respect to each tensor parameter — one tensor for one parameter, a list of tensors, one per parameter in order, for several:

use math, math.nn

fn loss[w, b, x, y]
    p = sigmoid[x * w + b]
    return bce[flatten[p], y]

g = grad[loss]
grads = g[w, b, x, y]        --- [∂loss/∂w, ∂loss/∂b, ∂loss/∂x, ∂loss/∂y]
w = w - grads[0] * 0.1

f runs once on traced tensors, each remembering how it was computed from the parameters, and the tape is walked backwards from f's number, so every parameter's gradient comes from one run however many parameters there are (reverse mode: what training needs). Each operation computes its value as the operator does, so f's number is the same whether traced or not, to the bit. The operations a function grad differentiates may use: +, -, * (a number, or the matrix product), /, .*, ./, .^, ^, negation; sum, mean, max and min, whole or along an axis (an extreme's gradient reaches the first element holding it); exp, ln, log2, log10, sqrt, sin, cos, tan, asin, acos, atan, sinh, cosh, tanh, abs, erf, and clip (its bounds numbers that take no gradient; the gradient passes strictly inside them); floor, ceil, round and sign, constants of the tape as they are for numbers; transpose, reshape, flatten; len, .shape and onehot, read and never differentiated; the constructors (zeros, ones, full, eye, randn, tensor, linspace), which make constants; and every function of math.nn, each a formula over these (relu is clip, sigmoid is exp, the losses are ln, mean and onehot), differentiated as a helper is. A helper f calls is differentiated too, through its own run. A value f reads that is no parameter — a fact, a tensor from outside — is a constant, with no gradient. A loop in f runs in order, since one tape records the run. log inside f prints the value. f answers a number (a tensor of one element counts); the gradient of a parameter the answer does not depend on is zeros of its shape. A shape that does not meet stops the program where it runs, with the operator's words, as it does untraced.

What is refused before the program runs, in both modes: an operation on a traced value that is not in the list (grad: loss calls \prod`, which is not differentiable), an index into a tensor or a loop over one inside f, a comparison of tensors (a comparison of two numbers decides as it would, with no gradient through the decision), a parameter that is a record or a list, parameters of two kinds (numbers beside tensors: all numbers, or all tensors), and a second derivative, which is not built: gradcalled inside a functiongrad differentiates, over numbers as over tensors, or inside a function it reaches (grad: df calls grad, and a function grad differentiates cannot), and gradhanded a gradient —grad[grad[f]], or grad[g] of a variable that holds one (grad[] takes a function the program writes, and this is a gradient — a gradient is not differentiated again: a second derivative is not built). (Over numbers the first had run the inner gradient on the dual numbers' values alone, and answered 0 where the second derivative of x³ at 2 is 12; the second had stopped as it ran, interpreted, and not built compiled.) A function that computes a derivative and is differentiated by nothing — fn slope[x]answeringgrad[cube][x]— runs as any function does. A functiongraddifferentiates is typed by the calls of its gradient:g[w, b, x, y]typesloss's parameters as those tensors, as a direct call would — a value that can fail by its payload, as a call hands it, so a gradient held in a variable (dloss = grad[loss]) applied to a table loadread is a function of tensors (it had been typed a function of a value that can fail in one pass and of a tensor in the next: "cannot reassign 'dloss'"). A gradient applied to a value that failed answers that failure, the leftmost, and never runs, as any call does, held or written inline. (Interpreted, the failure had been handed in —takes numbers or tensors, got done` — and compiled the call had stopped or answered it by whether the failure was the first argument.)

Design Note: one grad. Considered: a backward on tensors that remember their history, as PyTorch's requires_grad and .backward() do (rejected: a tensor would carry a history the program never wrote, and a value's meaning would depend on where it came from); a separate gradient[f] for tensors beside grad[f] for numbers (rejected: two names for one operation, decided by the parameters' kinds that the call already shows). Decided (2026-10-02): one grad, forward over numbers and backward over tensors, the kind decided by what its gradient is handed; the list of differentiable operations is the list, and anything outside it is refused before the program runs rather than silently a constant.

Distributed Support

Running a program as several processes is the launch's, not the program's:

crow launch <n> <file.co>

builds the binary with crow build, then runs n processes of it, each with CROW_RANK (0 to n − 1), CROW_WORLD_SIZE, CROW_MASTER_ADDR and CROW_MASTER_PORT in its environment, all on 127.0.0.1 from base port 29500, their output interleaved. A process reads its rank as it reads any variable of its environment — my_rank = int[env['CROW_RANK', '0']] (not rank, which is use math's) — and takes its share of the work by it.

crow launch 4 train.co     --- build + run on 4 processes

Design Note: the process count is the launch's. Considered: nodes[n] in the program, as cpu[n] and gpu[] are. Decided (Vijay, 2026-10-11): the count is given once, where the processes are started — the program had said it beside the launch's own count, and the core name took nodes from every program (graph code reached for it and wrote all_nodes instead). nodes is no name of the language, so a program may use it as it likes. The runtime's ring operations (crow_runtime::distributed: a mean across the ranks, a broadcast, a barrier) are not written as builtins yet.

Backend Detection

The runtime reaches the GPU through wgpu, which takes the interface the machine has when the program first asks: Metal on Apple Silicon, Vulkan on Linux (NVIDIA's and AMD's cards included). Nothing in the program or the crowfile names one, and where no adapter answers the work runs on the CPU.

Decoupled from Language Version

New GPU interfaces, or a new wgpu, are runtime updates (crow upgrade), not language changes. The .co source files never change. This follows the same model as LLVM — Rust and Swift don't change when LLVM adds a new target.

The language specifies intent. The toolchain handles implementation.

Effects Have Kinds

An effect is what a call does beyond answering its value: it reaches outside the program's values. Each builtin's row declares its kinds, and a function written in Crow — a program's, or a standard module's — has the union of what its body reaches, through every call, a function it hands over as a value included (any[xs, check]); nothing is written in a program. The kinds:

KindWhat a call of it doesAmong the builtins
consolewrites outputlog
filesreads a file, a directory or a table from a pathread, ls, exists, csv, lines, load
files.writewrites, makes, moves or removes a filewrite, mkdir, rm, mv, cp, save
networksends or receives over the networkrequest, send, recv, fetch, poll, a socket's
db.readreads a database, or opens a connection to oneconnect, query
db.writewrites a databasequery of a statement that writes, insert, transact
mailsends mailmail
clockreads the clocknow
randomdraws from the seeded generatorrand, seed, randn, normal, shuffle, choice, the other draws
inputreads what the reader typesinput
processreads the process's arguments or environmentargs, env
exitends the processexit
shellruns a commandshell
sessionacts for the sessionnotice, sign_in, sign_out, download, later
waitswaits: on the reader, a socket, the clock, a commandinput, sleep, shell, recv
servingdeclares what the server servesroute, page, serve

A builtin may have several: shell runs a command and waits, input reads and waits. Every rule the language states about effects is stated by kind, once:

Where a call standsWhat it may reach
a viewconsole, clock (One moment per event: a render's now[] is the event's moment) and random, and nothing else: a view shows the page's state, and runs whenever the page needs it
a derivationconsole, files, network, db.read, clock, random, process: it reads, and changes nothing, since it runs whenever the page needs it
a handler, or a function a handler callsanything; session, db.write and mail are held to its commit (A handler is a transaction), and a failed argument of a held effect stops the handler, so an error never reaches past the session
a function, the top levelanything
a test blockanything; files, network, shell, mail and the databases answer from fixtures where a recording kept them (Verification)
a route's handleranything; one that reaches no effect is answered on the server's own thread (Routes)

A call a context cannot hold is refused where it is written, in one wording per kind whatever the builtin — write[] writes a file — a view shows the page's state and does nothing else, since it runs whenever the page needs it (it may write to the console, and read the clock): call it from a handler, or a function a handler calls — and where the call belongs is its kind's: a read a derivation may make (files, network, db.read, process) is a derivation's, which the view reads — query[] reads a database — a view shows …: read it in a derivation the view reads — `derive x` above the view, returning it — and a write, or any other kind, a handler's (a call of several kinds goes where the one a derivation cannot hold says: recv, which waits). (Until 2026-10-21 a read had been sent to a handler too, where the book and this section teach a derivation for it.) One the context reaches through a function it calls is refused naming that function. The other rules, by kind: a loop whose body reaches an effect of any kind keeps its order (For Loops); a value read by an effect of the kinds files, network, db.read, input, process or shell carries the moment it was read (captured); a rehearsal records a files.write or a shell with its arguments instead of running it, and a network request runs through its own fixtures (Verification); a function calling a session or a mail effect acts for the session, and is a handler; crow check's reach names what each file reaches by kind.

Design Note: effect kinds, not names. Before 2026-10-19 each rule was a list of builtin names in the compiler, and the lists disagreed with each other (one held names that no longer existed; the readers were listed twice with different contents). Considered: console output as no effect at all (rejected: output has an order, and a loop that logs keeps it — For Loops — so both modes decide alike); later as a kind of its own (rejected: it acts for the session as a notice does, held to the commit); every effect held in a handler, a failed argument stopping it (the rule until then; rejected: a read with a failed argument answers the failure as any call does, and only what acts past the session — its state, a database, a mailbox — must never see one); files as one kind (rejected: a derivation may read a file and may not write one, and a rehearsal records writes and runs reads); a query in a view (the rule until then, which the book taught as "query at render time"; rejected: a derivation is the place a page reads a table — recomputed when the table is written or a tick passes, off the render — and a view is a function of the state it is given). Decided: the sixteen kinds above, declared on each builtin's row, each context's rule stated once in the compiler's one table; a draw is no effect on the world (the generator is the program's own), so it orders a loop and makes a function impure to crow check, and a view may draw; assert and running are no effects, but their order shows, so a loop over them keeps its order; running and captured answer by the run, so a function crow check probes as pure cannot call them.

15. Background Work: later

A live Crow app is one loop serving every connected client; a script's poll loop is one loop feeding everything downstream. Slow work on the loop freezes everyone behind it. later is the one verb that moves work off the loop:

fn load_book[]
    book = query[pg, 'SELECT symbol, pnl FROM trades']
    status = 'updated'

fn refresh[]
    status = 'loading'
    later[load_book]
FormMeaning
later[f]run f off the current loop; returns immediately
later[f, a, b]scripts/servers: capture a, b by value, pass to f — in a page's handler refused, in both modes: a page's work reads the facts it needs
running[f]is a schedule of f still in flight? (spinners, disabled buttons); for a derivation, is a recompute of it in flight?

Semantics, in order of importance:

  1. State is the only mailbox. In live apps the fn reads and writes reactive state; its writes are applied on the loop between renders, one schedule at a time, as one — state never mutates concurrently, and landing writes re-render the page. In scripts (function-scope isolation applies) the fn communicates through its arguments and the database.
  2. Coalesced, never stacked. A second later[f] while f is in flight runs f once more when it ends — however many arrive meanwhile — from the state as it then stands, so the work's last run reads every write made before it. A script's work coalesces the same way (the latest schedule's work runs once more; it had been skipped until 2026-10-28). Double-clicks and overlapping polls cannot stack, and none is lost: a schedule skipped while in flight (until 2026-10-18) had left a page with the earlier run's answer — a refresh asked after a filter changed showed the old filter's rows — where the interpreter, running each inline, showed the new.
  3. A failure stops it. A failing query inside the fn stops it as it stops a handler: nothing it wrote lands, the state keeps its last value, and the notice says why. A script's work that stops prints its stop (runtime error: line …) and the script goes on, in both modes: the interpreter, running the work inline, had ended the program, and the compiled program's thread had ended in silence.
  4. Nothing vanishes at exit. A script waits for in-flight work before ending.
  5. The interpreter runs the work inline. later promises "eventually, atomically" — immediate is a legal schedule, so crow run -i and crow test are deterministic.

later is for responsiveness; for is for throughput. Crunching data is parallel already — later exists so an interaction (a click, a tick) never waits on slow work: remote queries, shell calls, model loads.

later is in-process and best-effort: if the process dies mid-flight, the work is gone. Work that must survive a crash (placing an order) belongs in a durable queue the fn drains — see the claim-semantics proposal in discovered-features.md.

Periodic Freshness and the Loop Budget

serve[3000, refresh = 5, budget = 50]
Named argMeaning
refresh = Nre-render every connected session every N seconds. A derivation that reads now[], or a table the program never writes once its fresh window has passed, recomputes on the tick (Derivations), so pages stay current with zero user code. Missed ticks are skipped, never stacked — a slow render coalesces.
budget = Mlog any handler or render that takes more than M ms, with its name — the loop ships its own heartbeat.

There is no every[], no timer builtin, and no way to write periodic user code. Periodicity is a property you declare on serve, not a loop you write.

Design Note: Periodicity Is Declared, Not Written

Evidence: in the same production system that motivated later, a 1s status poll needed a hand-written in-flight guard so slow rounds couldn't stack out-of-order updates, and a 3s news poll starved a 60s universe refresh through a shared rate gate — four symptoms, one root cause: user code owned the clock. Every hand-rolled poll loop eventually needs the same three fixes (overlap guard, backoff, shutdown), and every codebase writes them badly once first.

What was considered: an every[N] { … } block (rejected: it is the poll loop with nicer clothes — overlap, starvation, and shutdown become the user's problem again); a timer builtin (rejected: same, smaller).

Why this design: the runtime owns the clock, so no-overlap (missed-tick skipping) and lifecycle come built in, and there is nothing to hand-roll badly. The while + sleep poll shape remains for scripts — crow check steers UI-shaped uses toward refresh (the stale-loop-read finding). Deferred: per-source refresh intervals, backoff policy for failing render queries.

Design Note: The Loop Never Blocks

Evidence: a production trading scanner froze in premarket because a ~700ms model load and synchronous database reads ran directly on its event loop — every websocket and poll stalled behind them. The fix (offload to threads) exists in every ecosystem, and in every ecosystem it is opt-in discipline: Python's to_thread, JS's workers, Go's go, Swift's detached tasks. Discipline failed there and fails everywhere.

What was considered: async/await (rejected: function colors infect every caller); a bare spawn verb with return values (rejected: Go's leak and race classes arrive with it); nothing (rejected: crow check can detect blocking handlers but the language must be able to express the cure).

Why this design: Elixir/LiveView's mailbox model — background work communicates by messages the loop applies serially — is the only surveyed approach with no colors, no locks, and no shared-state races, and Crow's reactive state is already exactly such a mailbox. Coalescing is built in because the overlap guard is the first thing every production system bolts on — and a guard that skips loses the last ask: a run already in flight read the state before it, so its answer is the stale one (until 2026-10-18 a schedule in flight was skipped; a skip and the interpreter's inline runs could end with different facts, where a coalesced run ends where the inline ones do). The interpreter's inline schedule keeps the parity principle: both modes satisfy the same contract, tests see the simplest legal schedule. Deferred deliberately: automatic offload of slow handlers (the no-verb endgame — needs the decision-span machinery), cancel-and-restart for typeahead-shaped work, a render latency budget on serve, and durable jobs. Delivered with the session layer (session-layer-design.md §3.4): a derivation that queries is computed off the render and a superseded one is stopped — the automatic offload, for derivations, and the cancel-and-restart; handlers follow in the layer's next phase.

Design Note: Effects Commit When the Handler Ends

What was considered: leaving effects immediate (the rule before the session layer); making every effect deferred, including reads (rejected: RETURNING, a shell status, a query the handler branches on all need an answer, and a handler that cannot read is not a handler); two-phase commit across stores (rejected: three databases with three transaction models cannot promise it, and pretending would be the worst kind of promise).

Why this design: one rule, decidable from the text, that a reader can apply without the runtime: needed the answer, it ran; did not, it waits. Elm's commands and Redux's thunks return effects as values but make the handler write them as data; here the handler's text is unchanged and the runtime does the holding. Design: core-design.md §1.

Design Note: A Fact Never Holds an Error

What was considered: errors landing in facts (the rule before, and later's); a per-fact choice.

Why this design: a fact is what the reader acts on. An error in a fact is a page that lost its data to a string, and a handler that half-succeeded. Stopping keeps the last good value and tells the reader why; derivations, which are beliefs, keep holding errors, so a page with one failed part is still a page. Design: core-design.md §2.

Design Note: One Moment Per Event

What was considered: a wall-clock read per call (the rule before); a frozen clock for the whole process (rejected: refresh and derivations over now[] need it to move); a clock capability only (deferred: capabilities bind what a part needs; the per-event moment is what the binding delivers).

Why this design: an event is one thing that happened at one time. Two readings inside it are two answers to one question. Tests set it with the verb that sets any input, because it is one. Design: core-design.md §3.

Design Note: Handlers and later

What was considered: dropping later once handlers run off the loop (rejected: a thirty-second import would hold every other click of the session); a second kind of handler (rejected: two ways to handle an event).

Why this design: a programmer never chooses where a handler runs; the runtime does, invisibly. The only choice left is whether the session's other clicks should wait for this work, and later[f] is the one way to say they should not. Design: session-layer-design.md §3.3.

Design Note: Text Is Text

What was considered: keeping the old rule — a string that begins with < and ends with > is markup — which made composing views free (a view returned a string, and a string that looked like markup was placed as markup); rejected: whatever a reader types or a database holds can look like markup, and a page placed it as markup, so one reader's text ran as script in every other reader's page. A raw[s] for markup built by hand (deferred: nothing in the corpus builds markup from strings; if it is needed, it gets a name that says what it does).

Why this design: markup has a source — a view, an element, doc — and only what came from one is markup. The interpreter carries the source with the value; the compiler carries it in the type (markup, a string wherever a string is read), and compiles a view once for markup and once for text when it is given both, so the two modes place every child alike. Reading a string never makes it markup.

Design Note: One Widget Element

What was considered: growing the vocabulary for each thing a page might draw (rejected: a map, an editor, a chart library — every one a language change, and never enough); script tags or inline JavaScript in views (rejected: two ways to put code on the page, and text a reader wrote could become one of them); widgets that write facts directly (rejected: the page would write state no handler checked).

Why this design: one element and one module shape. The module draws; everything it tells the program arrives as an event the page offered, to a handler, like a click — so the session's rules (one event at a time, a transaction, a notice) hold for widgets too. Design: session-layer-design.md §4.4.

Design Note: Files Are Inputs and Effects

What was considered: an upload element and an event carrying the file's bytes (rejected: a second way to bind a value, and a socket that carries megabytes between clicks); a file record as a struct the runtime declares (rejected: the reader's map of strings is already the shape a record the runtime fills takes, and a program declares upload = map[] as it declares any fact); download as a response a handler returns (rejected: handlers return nothing to the page; everything a handler does to the page is an effect held to its commit).

Why this design: a file field is a field whose value is a map; the bytes travel beside the socket, by a one-time ticket, as a sign-in's cookie does, and are limited before they are sent. A download is an effect like notice: a handler that stops hands the reader nothing.

Design Note: The Runtime Says Who the Reader Is

What was considered: a reader fact the program declares and a login handler assigns (rejected: anything a handler can assign, a bug can assign, and nothing would keep a bound input or a stray event away from it); handing the program the cookie, or web.auth's sign and verify, to build sessions itself (rejected: every app would rebuild the same security-critical plumbing, and most would get one part wrong); a record type for reader (rejected: the runtime reads it from a cookie before the program runs and cannot know the program's struct; maps are the one shape that crosses that line, and the values are strings because identity is — an id, a name, a role); a built-in users table and login page (rejected: who may sign in, and how, is the app's; only the password hash is not, so it is hash[pw, 'password']); a sign-in carried over the socket (rejected: a socket cannot set an HttpOnly cookie, and a cookie script can read is one a script can steal — the page fetches it from the server with a one-time ticket instead).

Why this design: the reader is a fact the runtime provides, like the clock: read everywhere, written by two effects, and checked by ordinary code. Signing out ends the sign-in everywhere because that is what a person means on a shared computer. Design: session-layer-design.md §4.1.

16. Verification: test Blocks

Verification is part of writing a function, not a separate discipline. A test block states what a function does at points you chose, and what it can never do anywhere:

fn fee[notional]
    if notional lt 0
        return error['negative notional', 'validation']
    return max[1.0, notional * 0.001]
test[n]
    fee[50000.0] eq 50.0                --- example: exact fact, checked once
    fee[100.0] eq 1.0                   --- the floor decision
    fee[-5.0].kind eq 'validation'      --- the error path
    if n gte 0.0
        fee[n] gte 1.0                  --- law: n is generated, many values
FormMeans
test after a fnthat function's examples and laws
test name[…]a standalone scenario, any file, any position
test[a, b]params are generated inputs — boundaries first
no paramsruns once

A param's type is what its use in the body gives it: the parameter of the function it is handed to (as the test's other calls of that function type it, when nothing else does), or a number, when it meets one in arithmetic.

A bare boolean line is a truth to check. assert is for steps inside scenarios, and reports both operands on failure. Preconditions are plain ifs, and a bare boolean line under one is a truth too, checked where the precondition holds. A failure names the generated input it failed at only where the failing line reads one — a law, a line reading a name made from one, or a line under a precondition that does ((at n = -1)); an example fails alike at every input, and names none. Bindings are plain assignments. A test body is ordinary Crow. A block that reaches no truth and no assert — in its body or in a function it calls — fails with asserts nothing: a test that cannot fail tests nothing.

A test block sees declarations (functions, views, structs, imports) and two kinds of top-level assignment: literal values — a number, text, a bool, a list or a map of them, a frame built from them, a moment of them (moment[0.0], moment['2026-01-01']) — which are reactive-state defaults, and connections (db.connect[...]), which are resources the derivations and handlers under test need. Computed top-level values are invisible, as inside any function.

Live apps are tested through a headless session — app[view] opens one, click[a, handler] runs a handler on it, a.name reads reactive state, html[a] renders the view to a string, and elements[a, selector] returns the rendered elements that match a selector, in document order:

test refresh_flow[]
    a = app[render]
    click[a, go]
    assert[a.status eq 'updated']
    assert['count: 1' in html[a]]

A named test is a scenario and reads the program back: the facts and derivations, as the harness's clicks left them — assert[session_id gt 0], assert[len[rows] gt 0]. Each session holds its own facts, as each served page does: the first app[…] takes the facts as the test has them, and every later one starts from where the first started. A bare fact name reads the session a verb acted on last, so a one-session test reads its session directly; a.name reads any session's — a fact, or a derivation as that session sees it (len[bob.cards]). An attached test (test[p] after a fn) sees what a caller of that fn sees and nothing of the program's state; its inputs are generated.

HarnessMeaning
app[view]a headless session rendering view against its own copy of the program's reactive state
click[a, handler]run handler on the session as one transaction (deferred calls: click[a, set_speed[10.0]], whose arguments may be numbers, text, lists, maps, tensors, frames or records, in both modes); when it stops, click evaluates to the error and the session is as it was
html[a]the rendered view as one HTML string, as the browser builds it (each element's attributes in name order), the same text in both modes
elements[a, sel]matching elements as records — tag, text, class, and every attribute (href, name, …) as keys; sel is tag, .class, tag.class, or a space-separated descendant chain ('div.controls button')
change[a, 'var', value]what typing into a bound field does: set the reactive variable, so the next render and handler see it. A part's fact is named as the code reads it: change[a, 'session.day', '2026-09-04']
change[a, 'now', t]set the session's clock: now[] in its handlers, renders and derivations is t until the test moves it again
change[a, 'reader', record]a reader who signed in: the session's reader is record (map[] signs them out), as if a handler had called sign_in
downloads[a]what the session's handlers handed the reader with download, in order: maps of name and text (a frame's CSV, a string, a file's contents)
app[view, width = 390, wide = true]a session at a viewport width (default 1280) — responsive rules are testable at every named width; wide = true lays the page out across the whole window, as serve[..., wide = true] serves it, instead of the reading column
box[a, sel]the layout engine's boxes for matching elements: x y width height overflow, plus tag class path
style[a, sel]resolved tokens: background color size weight
fits[a, width]true when nothing overflows at that viewport width

Structure is a truth like any other: len[elements[a, 'div.stats .card']] eq 2 says there are two cards in the stats panel; elements[a, 'button'][0].text eq 'Reset' pins the first button; an element that must sit inside a panel is selected through it. Layout that only CSS decides (pixels, columns) is not a tree property and is not what a unit test checks.

Background work scheduled with later[] runs inline in tests, so a handler's eventual state is observable on the next line — no waiting, no flakiness. Two sessions are two independent pages, which is how event interleavings are tested deterministically: one session's handler never touches another's facts, and a table one writes is the table the other reads, as in the served program (session-layer-design.md §3.1).

Verification never creates methods. Test bodies call production functions — that is what verification is — but no function exists in the program's namespace because verification needed it. Anything a truth needs beyond calling the program lives inside the block.

test blocks never run in production: crow build strips them, crow run ignores them. crow test runs them; crow check runs them plus everything automatic — determinism contracts, edge cases, database hazards. crow test --compiled runs them with every app they open (app[view]) compiled: the block runs in the interpreter, and its harness verbs reach the compiled program, started fresh for each run that opens one, so both modes answer the same test. A test reads a fact or a derivation of the compiled program by name, a record's fields included (stats.items.names), and the page it renders is the settled one: a render that read a value still being computed waits for it and renders again, as the interpreter computes it before it answers. A handler a test clicks with arguments is a handler, as one clicked by name is, and its arguments type the parameters nothing in the program types.

Design Note: Verification Is Part of the Function

What was considered: a conventional test framework (files by naming convention, assert helpers, fixtures); separate keywords for examples and laws (verify + rule, or where + prop_); helper functions holding multi-line laws.

Why this design: every alternative created a boundary the language then had to police — test files that drift from code, two words with identical semantics, "is this function logic or test scaffolding?". One keyword whose position carries the distinction (attached vs named) and whose params carry quantification needs no policing: a test block cannot drift from the function it sits under, and a helper function for verification is rejected by the checker. The runtime/verification split is drawn by the vocabulary itself: assert is a builtin that ships; test is a keyword that never executes in a build. Deferred: compiled-mode execution of test blocks (today they run in the interpreter) and richer input generation for structs and frames.

17. Type System

Crow is statically typed with inference. No type annotations are needed — the compiler infers concrete types from how values are used.

Handles

A handle is a value a module's native functions make and take, and the language holds opaque: a database connection and a transaction's (db.connect, transact's body), the layer's socket, stream and mailer (sys, web.mail's smtp), a route's response (respond, text, json, …), the server's app, a test's app session (app[…]). Every handle is one kind to the language, by one rule: it is not indexed, not walked, not JSON (encode refuses it), not in a mask, moved where a value would be copied, equal to no other (a eq b of two handles is refused where it is written: a mailer is not compared: a handle is its own, equal to no other — compare what it holds), and shown as its word (<mailer>, as bytes show). A handle is declared once, beside its module's native rows, with its name, the word a message uses for it, the type the compiled program holds it as and that type's default, and whether a session may hold it: a fact of a connection or a mailer is the process's, one value for every session (db.connect at the top level), while a response or a socket is the session's. The types the language defines — tensors, frames and columns, moments, parsed values, bytes, sparse matrices — are not handles: each has operators or readers of its own.

Design Note: one handle category. Considered: a type variant per handle in the compiler (the rule before 2026-10-19: eight variants, and every rule about handles a list of them — the clone rule, the display rule, the comparison, the fields a value has — which drifted); sparse matrices as a handle (rejected: + and * over them are the language's operators, so it stays a type); handles comparable by identity (rejected: no program needs it, and a connection re-made is the same database). Decided: one type and one class for every handle, each declared beside its rows; the interpreter keeps a value per runtime type, read through the declaration.

Rules

  • Every variable has exactly one type throughout its scope
  • Types are inferred from assignment: x = 5 makes x an int
  • Function parameter and return types are inferred from call sites
  • If the compiler cannot determine a type, the program is invalid

Call-Site Inference (No Generics Needed)

Crow has no generics, no type parameters, and no type annotations — but you can still write functions that work across different types. The compiler infers concrete types from each call site, effectively specializing the function for the types used.

fn keep[items, f]
    result = for x in items
        if f[x]
            x
    return result

fn is_even[x]
    return x % 2 eq 0

fn is_positive[x]
    return x gt 0

evens = keep[[1, 2, 3, 4], is_even]         --- items: array[int], f: fn[int] -> bool
positives = keep[[-1, 0, 3], is_positive]    --- same types, different function

This works because keep[[1, 2, 3, 4], is_even] tells the compiler that items is an array of ints and f is a function taking an int and returning a bool. No annotations needed — the call site is the type information.

When one function is called with different type signatures, the compiler monomorphizes it — generating one specialized native version per signature, invisibly:

fn first[arr]
    return arr[0]

log[first[[1, 2, 3]]]      --- compiles a tensor version
log[first[['a', 'b']]]     --- compiles a string-array version

A parameter nothing in the program types — a function only tests call — takes the type every test call agrees on, and stays unknown where they disagree, so a test never makes the program refuse what it did not. A part's tests type its functions the same way in a program that imports the part, whose build leaves the part's tests out: a part's function has the same types whichever file is the entry. (Such a function had its calls run by name, chosen as the program ran.) A tensor's shape is not a signature: a function handed tensors of different shapes is one function, whose parameter has the dimensions its calls agree on (see Tensor Arithmetic).

Specialization propagates through call chains and works with recursion and struct arguments. A version is what its function is: a view's versions are views, and a handler's are handlers, moving state as the handler does. (A handler's version was made a plain function, so its fact writes landed in locals the page never saw — scan.focus handed a symbol from a query and one from a click.) Two documented limits: a function used at multiple signatures cannot itself be passed as a value (a function value has one type — wrap it in a single-type function) — though a function handed to a builtin that calls it with the call's own arguments is called by name, not held: revise[name, default, f, x] calls f[held, x] and later[f, a] calls f[a], so each such call reaches the version its arguments make, as a direct call does (it had been refused as a value of several kinds, and a page calling magpie's batch with two frames typed apart did not start) — and a generic function with function-typed parameters can be reused across predicates of the same element type but not across element types (specializing the predicate per clone is future work) — a call that hands such a function an argument of another kind than its other calls is refused where it is written (`apply` takes a function, so one `apply` serves every call, and its 'x' is a frame's column here where another call makes it a number; it had been an internal error). A vector and a matrix are two signatures (a list of numbers and a tensor of one axis are one): first[xs] beside first[m] is a version for each, where one version had typed the list as a matrix. So are a list of ints and a list of records: an int is a record's none only handed alone (a record or none), and one version for both had taken the ints as records or none, an internal error. A function value has the one type its calls give it: a function used at several kinds is refused where it is handed as a value ('pass' is used at several kinds, and is handed as a value here), and a call through a function value of another kind than its parameter is refused where it is written (`g[…]` hands text to a function value whose parameter is an int); a parsed value handed through one is handed as it is (it had been read as a position, and stopped). A function value nothing calls — compared, put into text, held in a list no loop calls through — is refused where it is handed, since nothing types its parameters; a call through a variable that holds it types the parameters it gives, even one of the wrong count (whose count is the one mistake), and so does a call through a record's field that holds it — called there (c.f[c.x]), read into a local and called, or handed where a function is taken — or through an item of a list it was put in (fs = push[fs, sq], then g = fs[1], fs[0][5] or a loop over fs; it had been refused as a function nothing calls). A list literal of functions written — [double, triple], tools = [weather, search] — is a group: each function is held by its name (compiled, the value is the name as text), a loop over the list types every function by its calls through the variable, and a call through the variable (g[2]) is a dispatch the compiler writes over the members, which must take the arguments and answer one kind that a position can hold (an int, a number, a bool, text, a list or a map); members of several signatures are described and called by name alone (describe, apply, the standard modules' two operations over functions: section 13, sys), and a positional call through one is refused (`t[…]` calls one of weather, search by position: they take or answer different kinds … — call it by name). A group's function is no function value: a builtin that takes a function (any, later) does not take one (any[] takes a list and a function, got numbers and a function called by name).

Execution Modes

ModeCommandRole
Transpilercrow run file.coPrimary. Compiles to native Rust. This is the authority on valid Crow programs.
Interpretercrow run -i file.coDev tool. Runs code directly for quick iteration. Slower, but convenient.
REPLcrowInteractive. Explore and test code interactively. Uses the interpreter.
Dev servercrow dev [file.co]The edit loop. Serves the program with the interpreter, and reloads it as its files change.

crow dev serves the program with the interpreter and reloads it as its files change. A change is checked first, by the gate crow run -i passes. A program that passes replaces the one serving, and every tab reconnects and resumes its session, drawn by the new code: crow dev keeps sessions as serve[…, keep = true] does. A change that breaks the program leaves the last good one serving, and every open page shows the refusal, with its file and line, until a change mends it; a click on it uncovers the last good page. A change to what a page's shell carries (css/app.css, a widget's module) reloads the tabs, and a program that serves nothing runs again. The program crow dev started stops with it, and nothing of it reaches a build: its routes and its part of the page exist only under crow dev, taken only from the crow dev that started the program.

The dev panel. Under crow dev, Ctrl+Shift+D opens a panel over the page: the session's facts, live; each event (a click by its handler, an input by its fact) with the facts it changed, before and after, and its render's time and size — a handler that stopped with its reason and the id its reader was told (apply stopped [3f9a1c07]: cannot convert 'x' to float); and the renders' timing, last, average and slowest. The panel watches and changes nothing: a value is tried through the page.

Design Note: the dev panel watches. Considered: editing a fact and resetting the session from the panel (ui-framework.md, Dev Tools). Rejected: it writes facts that no input and no handler wrote, so the page would show a state the program cannot reach and a bug found there might be the panel's. A value is tried through the page, and a test sets one with change[a, …].

The compiler's front end is the source of truth for what a valid program is: it infers a concrete type for every value. Both modes run the same front end (parse → expand → lower → infer → validate) and then the same intermediate form, which the interpreter walks and the Rust backend prints, so they reject the same invalid programs with the same messages.

The parity promise: both modes are required to produce identical output. This is enforced mechanically — crow test --parity runs tests/parity/ through both modes and diffs stdout, and crow test --book does the same for every code block in the book. A disagreement between the modes is a bug in Crow, never a semantics to learn. Interpret while exploring (instant start, the REPL, crow test); compile when shipping (crow build produces the binary without running it). crow run builds in the dev profile — the runtime crates at optimization level 1, the program at 0, no LTO — so the edit loop stays short; crow run --opt 1 file.co optimizes the program too (0 to 3, the runtime crates following above 1) for a run that needs the speed, and crow build is the shipped binary, level 3 with LTO. The level is a trade the program decides: a program whose own loops are the work runs many times faster at 1 (fannkuch: 3.85 s at 0, 0.32 s at 1, 0.12 s from crow build), and a large app rebuilds many times slower (finch's 10,541 lines of generated Rust: 3 s of compile after an edit at 0, 14 s at 1; 4 s and 26 s from scratch) — so 0 is the default, and crow dev builds nothing.

No none

Crow has no null, nil, or none value. Every variable always holds a concrete value. Missing or failed data is represented through the error-value system (ok, reason, kind, trace) — see Error Handling.

18. Project Configuration

Project Structure

A Crow program can be a single file or a full project. No restructuring is needed as a project grows.

Single File (No Project)

Any .co file is a valid program:

crow run hello.co

Project Structure

A project is a directory with a crowfile at the root. Create one with crow new:

crow new myapp

Full project structure (all optional except crowfile and main.co):

myapp/
  crowfile                    --- project identity + config
  main.co                   --- default entry point (crow run)

  jobs/                       --- batch tasks (crow run <name>)
    train.co                --- crow run train
    sync.co                 --- crow run sync

  views/                       --- web views (imported and wired in main.co)
    home.co                 --- use views.home → page['/', home.render]
    dashboard.co            --- use views.dashboard → page['/dashboard', ...]
    accounts.co             --- /accounts

  components/                 --- reusable UI functions
    nav.co
    chart.co

  layout.co                 --- shared UI wrapper for all pages

  lib/                        --- shared code (organized however you want)
    accounts.co
    ml/
      features.co
      model.co

  db/
    schema.co               --- source of truth for tables

  tests/                      --- integration tests
    accounts_test.co

  config/                     --- configuration files
  models/                     --- trained model artifacts
  data/                       --- data files

Conventions

Folder/FilePurposeAuto-discovered?
crowfileProject identity, entry points, depsRequired for projects
main.coDefault entry point. crow run runs thisYes — convention
views/Web views. Import with use views.X, wire with page[]Explicit
layout.coShared UI wrapper for all pagesNo — import with use, wrap each page's view
db/schema.coThe tables the program declares (its CREATE TABLE statements; crow db diff, crow db apply)No — import with use
jobs/Batch tasks. crow run <name>Yes — listed in crowfile
tests/Integration tests, as test blocksNo — run with crow test tests/x.co
lib/Your code, any organizationNo — import with use
components/Reusable UI functionsNo — import with use
config/Configuration filesNo — import with use

The toolchain reads two things by convention: main.co, the default entry, and the jobs the crowfile names. Every other folder is a habit, and its files are imported.

Entry Points

main.co is the long-running entry point (e.g., web server). jobs/ contains batch tasks that run and exit.

crow run              --- runs main.co (long-running)
crow run train        --- runs jobs/train.co (batch, exits)
crow run sync         --- runs jobs/sync.co (batch, exits)

Rule: main.co is the thing that stays running. Everything else runs and exits.

Growth Path

StageWhat you have
Day 1hello.co — one file, no project
Week 1crow new myapp — crowfile + main.co
Week 2Add views/, lib/ — split code out
Month 1Add db/schema.co, jobs/train.co
Month 6Full project — multiple entry points, lib files, pages, components

No restructuring at any step. You just add files.

crowfile

Every Crow project has a crowfile at the root. It is a plain Crow file — the toolchain evaluates it and reads these variables. A name it sets is a key the toolchain reads, not a program's variable, so a key may share a builtin's name, as cpu and gpu do (they had stopped every command as variables taking a builtin's name):

--- crowfile

name = 'myapp'
entry = 'main.co'                 --- optional: main.co is the default
db = 'sqlite://dev.db'              --- optional: lets crow check read the schema
cpu = 4                             --- optional: default thread count

deps = map[models = 'github.com/agncrow/crow-models@v0.1.0', utils = './libs/utils']
jobs = map[train = 'jobs/train.co', sync = 'jobs/sync.co']
KeyMeaning
nameProject name (written by crow new)
entryEntry point for crow run; defaults to main.co
dbDatabase url used by crow check and the editor for schema-aware checks
schema'reconcile', 'verify' or 'trust': what opening a connection does with the tables the program declares (see db, The schema a program declares); run from source reconciles, a built program verifies
cpu, gpuDefault resource counts (whole numbers of 1 or more), taken by crow run, crow run -i, crow build and the jobs alike — see Resource Control
depsMap of dependency name → a git repository and its version (github.com/org/repo@v1.0.0), or a local path (crow install; see Package Manager)
jobsMap of job name → file, run with crow run <name>

crow new writes only name; add keys as you need them. No need to list main.co — it is the default entry point by convention.

Dependencies can be:

FormatExampleDescription
Repository and versionmodels = 'github.com/agncrow/crow-models@v0.1.0'A git repository at a tag (or a branch, or a commit), pinned by crow.lock
Local pathutils = './libs/utils'Linked from a directory on this machine

File Extension

Crow source files use the .co extension.

main.co
lib/accounts.co
views/dashboard.co
jobs/train.co

CLI

crow                        --- start interactive REPL
crow file.co              --- the short form of crow run file.co
crow new myapp              --- create new project (crowfile + main.co)
crow new myapp --web        --- create web project (+ views/home.co)
crow new myapp --ml         --- create ML project (+ jobs/train.co, lib/model.co)
crow new myapp --db         --- create DB project (+ db/schema.co)
crow new myapp --web --ml --db  --- all capabilities
crow install                --- install deps into crow_modules/, pinned by crow.lock (--update [name] moves them)
crow setup                  --- fetch the pinned Rust toolchain and this release's runtime into ~/.crow for compiled builds, each file verified (--check: what is present)
crow upgrade                --- replace the binary the install line put in ~/.crow/bin with the latest release, held to its published hash (--version X: that release, a downgrade too; --check: whether a newer one is out); a crow installed otherwise is left as it is and told how it upgrades
crow build                  --- transpile to Rust and compile to native binary
crow build file.co        --- transpile a specific file
crow build --ir file.co   --- print the program's one typed form, the IR (docs/ir-design.md); nothing is compiled
crow build --rs file.co   --- print the Rust the program compiles to, printed from the IR; nothing is compiled
crow build --types file.co --- print every type inference settled, each body's locals included; nothing is compiled
crow run                    --- transpile + compile + run main.co; with no compiler on the machine yet, interpreted and told so (crow setup fetches one)
crow run file.co          --- transpile + compile + run a specific file (a dev build: the runtime crates at optimization level 1, the program at 0, no LTO)
crow run --opt 1 file.co  --- the same, the program at level 1 (0 to 3, 0 unless said; the runtime crates follow above 1; crow build is 3 with LTO)
crow run train              --- run a named job from the crowfile jobs map (crow run -i train interprets it)
crow run -i file.co       --- run with interpreter (instant start, for exploring)
crow dev [file.co]        --- serve with the interpreter, reloading as the program's files change (sessions kept)
crow stop                   --- stop the running app (pidfile, then project binary, then a program the interpreter serves and crow dev's watcher), each found running in this project's directory
crow stop book              --- stop the book this project is serving
crow book [--port N]        --- in the crow repository: serve the Crow Book with every code block runnable in place;
                                inside an app: derive and serve the app's own book — pages with trees and wireframes,
                                a chapter per part, the data, the changes since the accepted baseline (--write: build only);
                                the app's book serves next to the app, on its serve port plus one (finch on 3100 → 3101);
                                its runner answers only the book's own page on this machine
crow test                   --- run the test blocks of the project (or: crow test file.co)
crow test --parity          --- run tests/parity/ in both modes and diff output
crow test --served          --- serve tests/served/ and the example apps in both modes, drive each page alike, and compare
crow test --compiled file.co --- the test blocks, with every app they open compiled
crow test --errors          --- in the crow repository: the wrong programs of tests/errors, each refusal held to its reviewed .expect (--bless writes them)
crow test --modules         --- in the crow repository: each standard module written in Crow, its own tests
crow test --fuzz n --mistakes --- generated programs with one mistake planted in each, each refusal held to the rules every message keeps
crow test --packages        --- in the crow repository: each package's tests, and its example.co in both modes
crow test --fuzz [n]        --- n programs generated from a seed, each run in both modes; one they disagree on is kept (--seed s repeats a run)
crow test --fixtures        --- the tests where the database servers and the model are not: each read and each request is what crow check --record-fixtures kept
crow test --book            --- execute every code block of the book in both modes
crow test --docs            --- the docs cover every compiler name, and name nothing the compiler lacks
crow test --bench           --- run tests/bench/ in both modes and print each section's seconds side by side (--quick: small sizes; --write: record docs/benchmarks.md)
crow check                  --- automatic verification (uses crowfile; or: crow check file.co); records and diffs the app baseline
crow check --accept         --- accept every baseline change at once (the loop's `a`, for a script or a deliberate sweep)
crow check --show [name]    --- the app as a tree: pages, views under their guards, controls and handlers, facts; or one declaration's artifact
crow check --db <url>       --- + schema-aware database checks (or db = '…' in the crowfile)
crow check --record-fixtures --- the tests read the real databases and send their requests, and what each statement and each request answers is kept in .crow/fixtures
crow check --strict         --- exit 1 on any finding (CI)
crow check --verbose        --- every finding with its fix, and the purity and snapshot details, without waiting
crow check --self           --- in the crow repository: Crow held to itself (below; crow-check.md)
crow fix file.co             --- apply the fixes the program's refusal names (a slip, an import, a habit), then list them
crow fix file.co --hazard N [--option M] [--db url]   --- apply a fix from the check report, as it prints the command; a constraint's migration is written numbered and applied as crow db apply applies one
crow fmt                    --- format project (uses crowfile; or: crow fmt file.co)
crow fmt --check            --- check if formatting needed (exit 1 if not)
crow fmt --fix-interpolation  --- migrate {expr} strings to `expr`
crow fmt --fix-views        --- migrate ui-building fns to view declarations
crow db diff [file.co]      --- the SQL that makes each database hold the tables the program declares (db, The schema a program declares)
crow db apply <dir> <url>   --- run the migrations in dir that the database has not applied yet, each recorded in _crow_migrations
crow db describe <url> <sql> --- the columns a statement answers on that database, as JSON
crow lsp                    --- language server over stdio (see crow-lsp.md)
crow kernel install         --- make Crow a Jupyter kernel (notebooks, JupyterLab, VS Code, jupyter console)
crow kernel <connection>    --- the kernel itself, as Jupyter starts it
crow launch 4 file.co     --- build + run on 4 distributed processes

crow test runs the test blocks you wrote (see Verification). crow test --parity is the parity harness: every tests/parity/*.co file runs in both execution modes and any difference in output (or exit status) is a failure — this enforces the parity rule mechanically. crow test --book does the same for every code block in the book. crow test --fixtures runs the tests where the database servers are not — CI, a laptop without the data — from what crow check --record-fixtures kept in .crow/fixtures: a connection to a Postgres or ClickHouse server reaches nothing; a statement that reads is answered from its fixture, and one no fixture holds is the query's error, which a test that fails names (no fixture held `SELECT …` — `crow check --record-fixtures` records it where the database is); a write answers as one that landed and changes nothing, so a test that reads back what it wrote to a server needs the server; a request is answered from its fixture the same way, and one no fixture holds is the request's error value, named alike (Requests). A sqlite file is the program's own and opens as always. Each test runs as the recording ran it — the clock at 2026-01-01 00:00 UTC until the test moves it, the random draws seeded — so a statement that splices in the time matches what was kept, and with --compiled the apps the tests open answer the same way. A statement matches its fixture by its text as written, a list handed to IN ? as that one ?, whatever its parameters. crow test --fuzz does it for programs no one wrote: generated from a seed — ints, floats, text, lists and maps, records, parsed documents read and spliced into text, arithmetic and comparisons, the text, list and map builtins, conversions read with .ok, branches, loops and functions — and run by the interpreter and compiled; a program the two disagree on, or one either stops on with a bug in Crow, is kept under target/crow_fuzz/ with the first line they differ on, and the same seed makes the same programs on every machine. crow test --docs reads the compiler's own name tables and fails if a builtin, module function, or element is missing from this specification, if a reference row here names something the compiler doesn't have, if a module or core builtin has no verified example in the book, or if an example file is not indexed — so the language and its documentation cannot drift apart. crow check runs your test blocks plus everything automatic — edge cases, determinism contracts, live-loop hazards, and (with a database url) the schema-aware concurrency checks documented in crow-check.md. crow check --self holds Crow itself to the same rule: it writes its cases from the compiler's own tables — every builtin with every kind its parameters take, every collection at every index, every operator over every pair of operands, the shapes a value meets — and runs each in five configurations that must agree (interpreted, walked in order, unfused, without moves, compiled); the findings it knows only shrink (crow-check.md).

crow build transpiles .co source files to Rust and compiles to a native binary. crow run builds and then executes the binary.

.env auto-loading: Both crow run and crow build automatically load a .env file from the current directory if it exists. Each line should be KEY=VALUE. Lines starting with # are comments. Quoted values ('...' or "...") are unquoted. Real environment variables take priority over .env values — the file only sets variables that aren't already set.

crow fmt auto-formats Crow source files. Formatting rules:

  • 4-space indentation, the unit every example here and in the book writes (normalizes tabs, 2-space, or mixed indentation)
  • No trailing whitespace
  • At most one consecutive blank line
  • Single newline at end of file
  • No leading blank lines

The formatter validates syntax before formatting. It works at the source level (not AST), so comments, then chains, and all syntax are preserved exactly. crow fmt --check exits with code 1 if any file needs formatting (useful for CI).

REPL

Running crow with no arguments starts an interactive REPL. Variables and functions persist between lines. Expression results are printed automatically.

> x = 10
> x + 5
15
> fn double[n]
      n * 2

> double[x]
20

Multi-line blocks (functions, if, for, while, struct) are entered by typing the first line, then indenting the body. An empty line or a non-indented line ends the block.

Notebooks

crow kernel install makes Crow a Jupyter kernel: it writes the kernel's spec where Jupyter looks (jupyter --data-dir's kernels/crow), so JupyterLab, the notebook, VS Code's notebooks and jupyter console offer Crow. A notebook is one interpreter session, the REPL's: a cell sees what the cells before it defined, what it logs appears under it as it runs, and its last expression's value is its result — a frame shown as a table of its first 50 rows. A cell that fails shows why, and the session goes on. Interrupting the kernel stops the running cell at its next loop turn, and the session keeps what it had. crow kernel <connection-file> is the kernel itself, which Jupyter starts with the file naming its sockets and the key each message is signed with (the Jupyter messaging protocol, 5.3). A cell is a fragment as a REPL line is, and a name it defines outlives it however many statements the cell has (a list a cell defined and read had reached the next cell as done).

Package Manager

A package is a git repository, a version is one of its tags (or a branch, or a commit), and crow.lock pins the commit each dependency checked out. The crowfile names each dependency by its repository and version, or by a path for a package beside the program:

deps = map[models = 'github.com/agncrow/crow-models@v0.1.0', mine = '../mine']

crow install checks each one out into crow_modules/<name>, and the program imports its files with use (use models.line reads crow_modules/models/line.co). A package is rooted at itself: its files reach each other by their paths inside it, so use line in one of its files is the package's line.co, whatever the program beside it holds (it had been the program's own line.co when there was one). The first install of a dependency takes the commit its version names and writes it to crow.lock; every install after that — on another machine, in CI — checks out that commit, whatever the tag names by then, and says so when a tag has moved. crow install --update models takes what models's version names now, and --update alone every dependency's; both rewrite the lock. A package's own dependencies — its crowfile's deps — are installed beside it in the same crow_modules/, pinned by the same lock, and its files reach them as the program does (use market.bars in one of models's files reads crow_modules/market/bars.co); a path a package names is read from the package's directory. A project holds one version of a package (below, Versions). (A package's dependencies had not been installed at all.) Commit crow.lock with the program, and leave crow_modules/ out of version control (crow new writes that .gitignore). A program run before its dependencies are installed is told which one is missing and to run crow install.

A repository on a forge (github.com, gitlab.com, codeberg.org, bitbucket.org) is the first two segments of its path, and what follows is the directory inside it that is the package: github.com/agncrow/crow/packages/models@v0.6.0. Any other place is a url, with the package's directory after a //: https://git.example.com/pkgs.git//models@v1, file:///srv/pkgs.git@v2. A local path is linked as it is, for working on a package beside a program.

Versions. A project holds one version of a package. Two requirements of one package — the program's and a package's, or two packages' — at tags of one repository and one major take the higher of the two, and the install says so: models: v0.1.0 wanted by the project, v0.1.1 by the package vectors — v0.1.1 taken for both. Within a major, the higher is the one that is supposed to serve both. Tags of two majors are two APIs, and a branch or a commit has no order, so those are refused naming who wanted each: models is wanted at two sources — '…@v0.1.0' by the project and '…@v1.0.0' by the package vectors: a project holds one version of a package, and only tags of one major can be joined (the higher is taken), so name the one they can share.

The lock holds the bytes. Beside each dependency's commit, crow.lock records the hash of what was installed — every file of the package by its path and its bytes — and every command that reads a program (crow run, crow build, crow test, crow check) holds crow_modules/ to it before reading anything of it: crow_modules/models differs from what crow.lock installed (…@v0.1.1 at 3f9a0c1d2e4b): run crow install to restore it, or change the package where it lives. An install restores a changed package from its commit, and refuses a fetch of a locked commit that hashes otherwise, since a commit's content cannot change. A linked package is beside the program by choice and is not held.

An attested hash. A requirer that verified a version may write the hash after it — models = 'github.com/agncrow/crow-models@v0.1.2#sha256:3f9a…' — in the program's crowfile or in a package's, and the install holds the bytes to every attestation it meets: one that does not match is refused naming the requirer and both hashes, and the package is not left installed. The lock records the source without the attestation.

Design Note: versions and checksums. Considered, for two requirements of one package: several versions in one project, each under its own namespace, as Cargo allows for incompatible majors (rejected: every call in Crow is qualified by a file's name, and crow_modules/<name> is one directory, so two versions of one name would need either a second naming or a resolver; names are never ambiguous); nested copies, as npm keeps (rejected: the same, and a part is a file reached by its path). Decided: Go's rule, one version, the higher within a major, refusal across majors. For checksums: a transparency log of versions and hashes run as a service, as Go's checksum database is (Sigstore's public log would serve without running one); its one protection beyond the lock's hash is the first install of a version nobody has recorded, at the cost of a service to keep up, a key, proof checking in the client and a lookup per install that says what a project depends on. Deferred (Vijay, 2026-10-19): the lock's hash and the attested hash give the same guarantee among everyone who commits a crowfile or a lock, with nothing to run; a log is for an ecosystem of strangers, and can share the server a discovery index would need.

Design Note: no registry. Considered: a central index mapping package names to repositories and versions (the index.json repository crow install had read), and a registry server that packages are published to, as crates.io and npm are. Decided: a dependency names its repository, as Go modules do. There is nothing to run or keep up, a name cannot be taken by someone else, and a tag is the one version there is; the lock is what makes every install the same, which a registry's immutable versions otherwise give.

19. Design Notes

The language-wide rejections, each with what was considered and why it lost. Notes about a single feature sit beside that feature (search this document for "Design Note").

Names Are Never Ambiguous

A bare name in Crow means exactly one thing. Collisions are never resolved silently — not by shadowing, not by precedence, not by context. This is one rule applied at every level of the language:

1. Definitions cannot take reserved names. A variable, function, view, struct, parameter, or loop variable that collides with a name the language or an imported module owns is a compile error at the definition line:

range = [10, 20, 30]     --- error: 'range' is a core builtin
use math
sum = 0                  --- error: 'sum' is provided by math (line 2)
e = 100                  --- error: 'e' is a math constant
math = 5                 --- error: 'math' is a module name

Every error names the source and both fixes: rename your definition, or import the module qualified (use math as m).

A program's own functions are names it owns, and a variable, a parameter or a loop variable may not take one either: fn first[scores] beside fn scores[x] is refused at the parameter, 'scores' is a function of this program — pick another name for this parameter: scores[…] would call the function, since scores[0] would read as the call. (Both modes had read it so, a silent wrong answer.) A standard module's function, inlined, sees none of the program's names, and its locals are its own.

A name is declared once: two functions, two records, or a function and a derivation of one name are refused at the second, naming the first (the later had silently replaced the earlier). A module's form is reserved only where the module is imported: without use db, a program's own connect is the program's, and its calls are the program's function's.

2. Three namespace tiers. Core (~75 names: log, len, range, map, set, type…) is reserved in every file, held by the budget rule — until 1.0 a core name is added only as a breaking change the changelog lists first (a name it reserves may be one a program used), and from 1.0 the core does not grow, so this reservation never breaks existing programs. Stdlib modules (math, frame, io, db, web, ui) reserve their exports only in files that import them bare. Everything else — user modules today, registry packages tomorrow — is qualified-only: the import reserves exactly one name (the prefix), and the module can export ten thousand names without taking any of yours. The language owns the bare namespace; packages get prefixes. A module of the program's takes no standard module's name either: use math beside a math.co would name two modules, and is refused at the import (Importing).

3. Aliased imports are qualified-only (use math as m — see Aliased Imports). This is the escape valve when you want a name a module owns: rename what you own, or alias what you don't.

4. Fields are exempt. Struct fields, map keys, JSON fields, and frame columns live inside their containers, not in the file's namespace — account.sum, row.type, df.mean are always fine. The one exception: the four error-metadata names (ok, reason, kind, trace) are a fixed vocabulary on every value, so struct fields may not use them. A frame's column may — they are names data has: a chart's markers' kind, a log's reason, a check's ok — but f.kind is still the frame's status, never the column, so where the frame is known to hold one the dot read is refused, naming the way to the column: rename[f, 'kind', 'another'], or select. (describe names its columns name and type: its kind had been read as nothing.)

5. Vocabulary zones are language-owned. view blocks activate the HTML element vocabulary — the only zone, granted because HTML is an external, universal, unchangeable vocabulary that could not be renamed, reserved, or qualified without wrecking either learnability or ordinary code. User modules cannot define zones (that mechanism is a macro system, and Crow rejects macros); they export functions and views instead, reached by prefix — exactly how JSX fixes lowercase tags while components are imported references.

6. The backend's words are not reserved. Crow compiles to Rust, but Rust's keywords are not Crow's: a name Crow does not reserve — move, impl, match, loop, final — is yours as a variable, function, parameter, loop variable or struct field, in both modes. The compiled program renames it for Rust behind the scenes and still names it as you wrote it in a trace.

Why not shadowing (Python/Go-style)? Because Crow's brackets unify calling and indexing, shadowing produces silent wrong answers: range = [10, 20, 30] then range[0] — delete the assignment during a refactor and the same expression silently becomes range(0) = []. MATLAB, the one mainstream language sharing this syntax collision, chose shadowing — and it has been a documented footgun for forty years. Languages where shadowing is safe (Python, Go, Julia) are exactly the ones whose call syntax is distinct from indexing. Crow's is not, so a collision must be an error, at the earliest possible moment, with the fix in the message.
